Linearly Distributive Fox Theorem The author would like to thank Richard Blute for suggesting this topic as part of their doctoral thesis and for his support during the development of the theory, and for many instructive discussions on the topic, along with fellow student Shayesteh Naimabadi. The author also wishes to thank Jean-Simon Pacaud Lemay, whose support and help were instrumental in developing the Examples and Properties Section of the paper, as well as the entire category theory group at Macquarie University for the opportunity to visit and present on the topic. The author also acknowledges the support of the Natural Sciences and Engineering Research Council of Canada (NSERC), under the grant awarded to Richard Blute.
Rose Kudzman-Blais

Abstract
Linearly distributive categories (LDC), introduced by Cockett and Seely to model multiplicative linear logic, are categories equipped with two monoidal structures that interact via linear distributivities. A seminal result in monoidal category theory is the Fox theorem, which characterizes cartesian categories as symmetric monoidal categories whose objects are equipped with canonical comonoid structures. The aim of this work is to extend the Fox theorem to LDCs and characterize the subclass of cartesian LDCs. To do so, we introduce the concepts of medial linearly distributive categories, medial linear functors, and medial linear transformations. The former are LDCs which respect the logical medial rule, appearing frequently in deep inference, or alternatively are the appropriate structure at the intersection of LDCs and duoidal categories.

1
Introduction

Within monoidal category theory,
cartesian categories
refer to categories whose monoidal product is given by the categorical product and whose monoidal unit is the terminal object. Perhaps the most well-known result concerning cartesian categories is Fox’s theorem. In his seminal 1976 paper “Coalgebras and cartesian categories”, Fox demonstrated that there is a right adjoint to the inclusion functor from cartesian categories into the category of symmetric monoidal categories
[
21
]
. This right adjoint functor consists of taking the cocommutative comonoids of a symmetric monoidal category
(\cX,\oslash,I)
, and their maps, in other words considering objects
A
equipped with coherent diagonal and counit maps:

\Delta_{A}\,\colon A\rightarrow A\oslash A\qquad\qquad e_{A}\,\colon A\rightarrow I

As a corollary of this adjunction, we see that cartesian categories are precisely the symmetric monoidal categories whose objects have a canonical comonoid structure and whose maps preserve these structures.

Cartesian categories have played a key role in the field of categorical logic. Indeed, one of the greatest successes of categorical logic was Lambek’s equivalence between intuitionistic propositional calculus and cartesian closed categories
[
30
]
. If we extend this paradigm and consider cartesian closed categories with a notion of involutive negation to capture the semantics of classical logic, these categories collapse to Boolean algebras due to Joyal’s paradox. Thus, there has not yet been a definitive framework for the categorical semantics of classical logic.

The field of categorical logic continued to thrive with the development of linear logic by Girard
[
23
]
. The categorical semantics for linear logic were subsequently explored by Seely, who identified that the appropriate definition was given by
*
-autonomous categories
[
35
]
, previously introduced by Barr
[
3
]
.
Linearly distributive categories
(LDC)s were later introduced by Cockett and Seely as alternative categorical semantics for linear logic, which take the notions of multiplicative conjunction and disjunction, and their interaction via linear distributivity, as primitive
[
15
]
. In particular, LDCs are categories
{\mathbb{X}}
with two monoidal structures
(\mbox{${\mathbb{X}}$},\otimes,\top)
and
(\mbox{${\mathbb{X}}$},\oplus,\bot)
, the former known as the tensor structure and the latter as the par structure, equipped with linear distributivity natural transformations:

\delta^{L}_{A,B,C}\,\colon A\otimes(B\oplus C)\rightarrow(A\otimes B)\oplus C\quad\qquad\delta^{R}_{A,B,C}\,\colon(A\oplus B)\otimes C\rightarrow A\oplus(B\otimes C).

The theory of categorical linear logic proved useful in developing categorical semantics for classical logic. LDCs provide the minimal framework necessary to discuss two connectives with left and right introduction rules, and cut as the only structural rule. Since any reasonable model of classical logic should at least include such structure, categorical models for classical logic can be built upon the foundations of LDCs (or
*
-autonomous categories, if negation is instead taken as primitive).

As the rules of contraction and weakening are central structural rules of classical logic, which are traditionally modeled by cartesian monoidal structures, a recent paradigm of categorical classical logic has been to add “cartesian” structure to LDCs (or
*
-autonomous categories). In this vein, Führmann and Pym developed the notions of classical categories and Dummett categories, poset-enriched
*
-autonomous categories and LDCs respectively whose objects have coherent notions of
\otimes
-diagonals and counits, and
\oplus
-codiagonals and units
[
22
]
. While, Straßburger and Lamarche both seperately considered
*
-autonomous categories whose objects also have coherent
\otimes
-diagonals and counits, and
\oplus
-codiagonals and units, but whose maps do not necessarily preserve these structures
[
38
,
29
]
.

More recently, Naeimabadi developed the theory of cartesian linear bicategories
[
32
]
and recent work has investigated De Morgan and Peirce’s calculus of relations, providing an axiomatisation based on the concepts of cartesian and linear bicategories
[
7
]
. In fact, the central examples of linear bicategories, the bicategorical analogues of LDCs introduced by Cockett, Koslowski, and Seely
[
17
]
, are also cartesian bicategories. Fox’s theorem is, in fact, central to the theory of cartesian bicategories, as introduced by Carboni and Walters
[
12
]
.

It was within this context that the idea arose to investigate
cartesian linearly distributive categories
(CLDC) and to develop a linearly distributive version of Fox’s theorem. CLDCs refer to LDCs whose tensor structure is cartesian and par structure is cocartesian. They were first introduced alongside LDCs by Cockett and Seely, though the notion received little attention beyond the first paper
[
15
]
.

In developing a linearly distributive analogue of Fox’s theorem, it quickly became apparent that no such construction could be adapted to all LDCs. For the same reason that Fox’s theorem is an adjunction between cartesian categories and symmetric monoidal categories, we needed to restrict to LDCs equipped with additional structure maps:

\Delta_{\bot}\,\colon\bot\rightarrow\bot\otimes\bot\qquad\nabla_{\top}\,\colon\top\oplus\top\rightarrow\top\qquad m\,\colon\bot\rightarrow\top

\mu_{A,B,C,D}\,\colon(A\otimes B)\oplus(C\otimes D)\rightarrow(A\oplus C)\otimes(B\oplus D)

Viewed as a logical entailment, the latter map is known as the
medial rule
, first introduced by Brünnler and Tiu for a system of classical logic within the deep inference framework
[
10
]
. Moreover, medial maps were central to both Straßburger’s and Lamarche’s categorical models for classical logic.

Within monoidal category theory, these medial maps are better known as instances of the interchange maps of
duoidal categories
. Duoidal categories are categories
\cX
with two monoidal structures
(\cX,\diamond,I)
and
(\cX,\star,J)
, equipped with precisely the structure maps introduced above
[
1
]
. The theory of duoidal categories first appeared in the work of Joyal and Street while studying braided monoidal categories
[
26
]
.

With this perspective in mind, we introduce the notion of
medial linearly distributive categories
(MLDC), inspired both by the work of Straßburger and Lamarche in categorical classical logic and by the theory of duoidal categories. MLDCs can be regarded both as the categorical semantics for multiplicative linear logic with the medial rule, and as a appropriate intersection point between LDCs and duoidal categories.

Leveraging the well-developed theory of LDCs and duoidal categories, we define the appropriate functors and transformations between MLDCs. Within this framework, we consider an analogue to comonoids:
medial bimonoids
, objects
A
equipped with coherent
\otimes
-diagonal,
\otimes
-counit,
\oplus
-multiplication, and
\oplus
-unit maps,

\Delta_{A}\,\colon A\rightarrow A\otimes A\qquad e_{A}\,\colon A\rightarrow\top\qquad\nabla_{A}\,\colon A\oplus A\rightarrow A\qquad u_{A}\,\colon\bot\rightarrow A

Considering the bicommutative medial bimonoids and their maps in a symmetric MLDC gives the construction needed to define a right adjoint 2-functor to the inclusion 2-functor from CLDCs to the 2-category of symmetric MLDCs. This yields the desired linearly distributive Fox theorem and, as a corollary, a characterization of CLDCs as symmetric MLDCs whose objects have coherent medial bimonoid structure.

In the process of developing this paper, it became evident how poorly understood CLDCs are. This prompted the author to collaborate with Dr. Pacaud Lemay to explore the nature of CLDCs beyond their characterization via the linearly distributive Fox theorem. This resulted in a separate paper, “Cartesian Linearly Distributive Categories: Revisited”
[
27
]
. While both papers are written to be independently comprehensible, we recommend reading them in tandem to gain a complete perspective on cartesian and cocartesian structure in the context of LDCs.

Outline.
Given that the results developed in this paper are based on the theories of Fox’s theorem, LDCs, and duoidal categories, the paper begins with three substantial preliminary sections. Section
2
presents the basics of monoidal categories, cartesian categories, and Fox’s theorem. Section
3
reviews the literature on LDCs, including linear functors and linear transformations. Section
4
introduces the necessary background on duoidal categories. Then, Section
5
begins by motivating the introduction of the medial rule and subsequently defines MLDCs. The theory of MLDCs is developed: symmetric MLDCs are defined, adding negation is discussed, examples are introduced, and properties are presented. Section
6
defines the appropriate linear functors and linear transformations between symmetric MLDCs. Section
7.1
introduces medial bimonoids and shows that considering such structures and their maps does, in fact, yield a CLDC. Finally, Section
7
proves the main result of this paper: the linearly distributive Fox theorem.

Conventions.
There are a few notational choices made in this paper and we take a moment to detail the most important.

•

Composition of morphisms/arrows in a category is denoted by the symbol
;
and is written in diagrammatic order. Objects in a category are denoted by capital letters, while the morphisms are denoted by lowercase letters.

•

Monoidal products and monoidal units will be denoted with various different symbols to help distinguish the particular type of category in question. In particular:

–

\oslash
and
I
are used for standard monoidal categories
\cX
.

–

\otimes
and
\top
are used for the tensor monoidal structure, and
\oplus
and
\bot
for the par monoidal structure in a linearly distributive category
{\mathbb{X}}
, in agreement with the notation introduced by Cockett and Seely in
[
15
]
, as opposed to Girard’s notation
[
23
]
.

–

\diamond
and
I
are used for the first monoidal structure and
\star
and
J
for the second in a duoidal category
\cX
, in agreement with the notation introduced by Aguiar and Mahajan
[
1
]
.

–

\times
and
\bone
denote the binary categorical product and the terminal object respectively, while
+
and
\bzero
denote the binary categorical coproduct and the initial object in a category.

2
Fox Theorem

We begin by giving a fairly complete description of the Fox theorem for cartesian categories, without proof, as the logic behind the traditional version of the result will be a guiding thread to generalizing it to the context of linearly distributive categories. We invite any reader who is well versed in the Fox theorem to skip this subsection.

2.1
Monoidal Categories

We briefly outline the foundational definitions of monoidal category theory, primarily to establish the notation used throughout this paper and to provide a reference for the axioms, which will frequently appear in the proofs that follow.

Definition 1
.

[
31
, Sec 7.1]

A
monoidal category

(\cX,\oslash,I,\alpha,\rho,\lambda)
consists of:

•

a category
(\cX,;,1_{X})
,

•

the
product
and the
unit
functors

\displaystyle\oslash\,\colon\cX\times\cX\rightarrow\cX

\displaystyle I\,\colon{\bf 1}\rightarrow\cX

•

the
associator
, the
right unitor
and the
left unitor
natural isomorphisms

\displaystyle\alpha\,\colon(\oslash\times 1_{\mbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\mathcal{X}}$}});\oslash\Rightarrow(1_{\mbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\mathcal{X}}$}}\times\oslash);\oslash

\displaystyle\alpha_{X,Y,Z}\,\colon(X\oslash Y)\oslash Z\rightarrow X\oslash(Y\oslash Z)

\displaystyle\rho\,\colon 1_{\mbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\mathcal{X}}$}}\Rightarrow(1_{\mbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\mathcal{X}}$}}\times I);\oslash

\displaystyle\rho_{X}\,\colon X\rightarrow X\oslash I

\displaystyle\lambda\,\colon 1_{\mbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\mathcal{X}}$}}\Rightarrow(I\times 1_{\mbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\mathcal{X}}$}});\oslash

\displaystyle\lambda_{X}\,\colon X\rightarrow I\oslash X

such that the associativity pentagon (
1
) and unit triangle identities (
2
) hold.

Definition 2
.

[
31
, Sec 9.1]

A monoidal category is
braided
if it has a braiding natural isomorphism

\sigma\,\colon\oslash\Rightarrow{switch}_{\mbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\mathcal{X}}$},\mbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\mathcal{X}}$}};\oslash\qquad\qquad\sigma_{X,Y}\,\colon X\oslash Y\rightarrow Y\oslash X

which behaves coherently with associators (
3
) and with unitors (
4
).

(4)

A monoidal category is
symmetric
if it is braided and the braiding is self-inverse (
5
).

(5)

Given a braided monoidal category, we can define a composite of associators and braidings, sometimes known as the “canonical flip”. This natural isomorphism is key to the Fox theorem, therefore, to improve readability, we define it here.

Definition 3
.

Let
\cX
be a braided monoidal category. Define the natural isomorphism

\tau_{W,X,Y,Z}\,\colon(W\oslash X)\oslash(Y\oslash Z)\Rightarrow(W\oslash Y)\oslash(X\oslash Z)

to be the following equivalent composites

\textstyle{(W\oslash X)\oslash(Y\oslash Z)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\alpha_{W,X,Y\oslash Z}}
\textstyle{W\oslash(X\oslash(Y\oslash Z))\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{1_{W}\oslash\alpha_{X,Y,Z}^{-1}}
\scriptstyle{1_{W}\oslash\sigma_{X,Y\oslash Z}}
\textstyle{W\oslash((X\oslash Y)\oslash Z)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{1_{W}\oslash(\sigma_{X,Y}\oslash 1_{Z})}
\textstyle{W\oslash((Y\oslash X)\oslash Z)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{1_{W}\oslash\alpha_{Y,X,Z}}
\textstyle{W\oslash((Y\oslash Z)\oslash X)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{1_{W}\oslash\alpha_{Y,Z,X}}
\textstyle{W\oslash(Y\oslash(Z\oslash X))\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{1_{W}\oslash(1_{Y}\oslash\sigma_{X,Z})}
\textstyle{W\oslash(Y\oslash(X\oslash Z))\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\alpha^{-1}_{W,Y,X\oslash Z}}
\textstyle{(W\oslash Y)\oslash(X\oslash Z)}

2.2
Monoidal Functors and Transformations

The Fox theorem is an adjunction between the 2-categories of symmetric monoidal categories and cartesian categories. As such, we need to the appropriate notion of functors and natural transformations between monoidal categories.

Definition 4
.

[
31
, Sec 9.2]

Consider monoidal categories
(\cX,\oslash,I)
and
(\cY,\oslash,I)
.
A
monoidal functor

(F,m_{I},m_{\oslash})\,\colon(\cX,\oslash,I)\rightarrow(\cY,\oslash,I)
is a functor
F\,\colon\cX\rightarrow\cY
equipped with structure maps:

•

a morphism
m_{I}\,\colon I\rightarrow F(I)
, and

•

a natural transformation

\displaystyle m_{\oslash}\,\colon(F\times F);\oslash\Rightarrow\oslash;F

\displaystyle{m_{\oslash}}_{A,B}\,\colon F(A)\oslash F(B)\rightarrow F(A\oslash B)

satisfying associativity (
6
) and unitality (
7
) coherence conditions.

(7)

A
comonoidal functor

F=(F,n_{I},n_{\oslash})\,\colon(\cX,\oslash,I)\rightarrow(\cY,\oslash,I)
is a monoidal functor from
\cX^{op}
to
\cY^{op}
.

If
\cX
and
\cY
are symmetric, then
(F,m_{I},m_{\oslash})\,\colon(\cX,\oslash,I)\rightarrow(\cY,\oslash,I)
is a
symmetric monoidal functor
if it additionally interacts coherently with the braidings (
8
).

(8)

A monoidal (or comonoidal) functor is called
strong
when the structures maps
m_{I}
and
m_{\oslash}
(or
n_{I}
and
n_{\oslash}
) are isomorphisms.

.

(F,m_{I},m_{\oslash})\,\colon(\cX,\oslash,I)\rightarrow(\cY,\oslash,I)

(9)

Definition 6
.

[
31
, Sec 9.2]

Let
(F,m^{F}_{I},m^{F}_{\oslash}),(G,m^{G}_{I},m^{G}_{\oslash})\,\colon\cX\rightarrow\cY
be monoidal functors. A
monoidal transformation

\alpha\,\colon(F,m^{F}_{I},m^{F}_{\oslash})\Rightarrow(G,m^{G}_{I},m^{G}_{\oslash})
is a natural transformation
\alpha\,\colon F\Rightarrow G
whose component morphisms behave coherently with the monoidal products and the units (
10
).

3.0pt\raise-2.55554pt\hbox{$\textstyle{}$}}}}}}}{\hbox{\kern 249.03738pt\raise-28.94446pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.55554pt\hbox{$\textstyle{G(I)}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces\end{gathered}

(10)

Let
(F,n^{F}_{I},n^{F}_{\oslash}),(G,n^{G}_{I},n^{G}_{\oslash})\,\colon\cX\rightarrow\cY
be comonoidal functors. A
comonoidal transformation

\alpha\,\colon(F,n^{F}_{I},n^{F}_{\oslash})\Rightarrow(G,n^{G}_{I},n^{G}_{\oslash})
is a monoidal transformation between the monoidal functors
(F,n^{F}_{I},n^{F}_{\oslash}),(G,n^{G}_{I},n^{G}_{\oslash})\,\colon\cX^{op}\rightarrow\cY^{op}
.

Proposition 7
.

There is a 2-category
\mathbf{SMON}
of symmetric monoidal categories, strong symmetric monoidal functors and monoidal transformations.

2.3
Cartesian Categories

The Fox theorem is concerned with characterizing the symmetric monoidal categories which are cartesian, therefore we quickly introduce the appropriate sub-2-category
\mathbf{CART}
.

Definition 8
.

A
cartesian category
is a category with finite categorical products and a
cocartesian category
is a category with finite categorical coproducts, where binary products, terminal objects, binary coproducts and initial objects, with the corresponding unique maps, are denoted by

\textstyle{C\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{f}
\scriptstyle{g}
\scriptstyle{\langle f,g\rangle}
\textstyle{A\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{t_{A}}
\textstyle{A\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\iota^{0}_{A,B}}
\scriptstyle{h}
\textstyle{A+B\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{[h,k]}
\textstyle{B\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\iota^{1}_{A,B}}
\scriptstyle{k}
\textstyle{\bzero\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{b_{A}}
\textstyle{A}
\textstyle{A\times B\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\pi^{0}_{A,B}}
\scriptstyle{\pi^{1}_{A,B}}
\textstyle{B}
\textstyle{\bone}
\textstyle{C}
\textstyle{A}

Cartesian categories have a canonical monoidal structure given by the binary categorical product and the terminal object, and similarly for the cocartesian categories:

Proposition 9
.

[
31
, Sec 3.5]

Consider a cartesian category
\cX
, then it is a symmetric monoidal category
(\cX,\times,\bone)
whose monoidal product is the categorical product of
\cX
and monoidal unit is the terminal object of
\cX
:

\displaystyle(X,Y)\mapsto X\times Y

\displaystyle(f\,\colon X\rightarrow X^{\prime},g\,\colon Y\rightarrow Y^{\prime})\mapsto f\times g=\langle\pi^{0}_{X,Y};f,\pi^{1}_{X,Y};g\rangle\,\colon X\times Y\rightarrow X^{\prime}\times Y^{\prime}

with isomorphisms

\displaystyle\alpha_{X,Y,Z}=\langle\pi^{0}_{X\times Y,Z};\pi^{0}_{X,Y},\pi^{1}_{X,Y}\times 1_{Z}\rangle\,\colon(X\times Y)\times Z\rightarrow X\times(Y\times Z)\quad

\displaystyle\rho_{X}=\langle 1_{X},t_{X}\rangle\,\colon X\rightarrow X\times\bone

\displaystyle\lambda_{X}=\langle t_{X},1_{X}\rangle\,\colon X\rightarrow\bone\times X

\displaystyle\sigma_{X,Y}=\langle\pi^{1}_{X,Y},\pi^{0}_{X,Y}\rangle\,\colon X\times Y\rightarrow Y\times X

Consider a cocartesian category
\cX
, then it is a symmetric monoidal category
(\cX,+,\bzero)
whose monoidal product is the categorical coproduct of
\cX
and monoidal unit is the initial object:

\displaystyle(X,Y)\mapsto X+Y

\displaystyle(f\,\colon X\rightarrow X^{\prime},g\,\colon Y\rightarrow Y^{\prime})\mapsto f+g=[f;\iota^{0}_{X^{\prime},Y^{\prime}},g;\iota^{1}_{X^{\prime},Y^{\prime}}]\,\colon X+Y\rightarrow X^{\prime}+Y^{\prime}

with isomorphisms

\displaystyle\alpha_{X,Y,Z}=[\iota^{0}_{X,Y};\iota^{0}_{X+Y,Z},\iota^{1}_{X,Y}+1_{Z}]\,\colon X+(Y+Z)\rightarrow(X+Y)+Z

\displaystyle\rho_{X}=[1_{X},b_{X}]\,\colon X+\bzero\rightarrow X

\displaystyle\lambda_{X}=[b_{X},1_{X}]\,\colon\bzero+X\rightarrow X

\displaystyle\sigma_{X,Y}=[\iota^{1}_{Y,X},\iota^{0}_{Y,X}]\,\colon X+Y\rightarrow Y+X

Proposition 10
.

There is a 2-category
\CART
of cartesian categories, strong symmetric monoidal functors and monoidal transformations, known as
cartesian functors
and
cartesian transformations
in this context. It is a full sub-2-category of
\mathbf{SMC}
.

\cX
denote a monoidal category.

(13)

•

7.53471pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&\cr&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-7.53471pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 14.52599pt\raise 5.89445pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-1.8889pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\Delta_{X}}$}}}\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.55554pt\hbox{$\textstyle{}$}}}}}}}{\hbox{\kern 40.53471pt\raise-28.27777pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.55554pt\hbox{$\textstyle{X\oslash X}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces\end{gathered}

(14)

We denote the category of cocommutative comonoids and comonoid morphisms in
\cX
by
\mathrm{C}[\cX]
.

Lemma 12
.

Given a cartesian category
(\cX,\times,\bone)
, every object
A
in
\cX
has a canonical unique cocommutative
\times
-comonoid
structure

\langle A,\langle 1_{A},1_{A}\rangle\,\colon A\rightarrow A\times A,t_{A}\,\colon A\rightarrow\bone\rangle

and every arrow
f\,\colon A\rightarrow B
in
\cX
is a comonoid morphism with respect to these structures.

The category of cocommutative comonoids in
\cX
inherits a symmetric monoidal structure from
\cX
, which is in fact cartesian.

Lemma 13
.

[
21
]

Given a pair of cocommutative comonoids
\langle X,\Delta_{X},e_{X}\rangle
and
\langle X^{\prime},\Delta_{X^{\prime}},e_{X^{\prime}}\rangle
in
\cX
, then the triple
\langle X\oslash X^{\prime},\Delta_{X\oslash X^{\prime}},e_{X\oslash X^{\prime}}\rangle
defined by

\Delta_{X\oslash X^{\prime}}=X\oslash X^{\prime}\xrightarrow{\Delta_{X}\oslash\Delta_{X^{\prime}}}(X\oslash X)\oslash(X^{\prime}\oslash X^{\prime})\xrightarrow{s_{X,X,X^{\prime},X^{\prime}}}(X\oslash X^{\prime})\oslash(X\oslash X^{\prime})

e_{X\oslash X^{\prime}}=X\oslash X^{\prime}\xrightarrow{e_{X}\oslash e_{X^{\prime}}}I\oslash I\xrightarrow{\rho^{-1}_{I}}I

is a cocommutative comonoid. Consequently,
\mathrm{C}[\cX]
is a symmetric monoidal category and the monoidal structure is given by the categorical product. Thus,
\mathrm{C}[\cX]
is a cartesian category.

Further, this extends canonically to strong symmetric monoidal functors and monoidal transformations. While the original Fox theorem is only an adjunction between categories, it is well-known that it easily extends to include transformations.

Lemma 14
.

[
21
]

1.

Given a strong symmetric monoidal functor
F\,\colon\cX\rightarrow\cY
and a cocommutative comonoid
\langle X,\Delta_{X},e_{X}\rangle
in
\cX
, the triple
\langle F(X),\Delta_{F(X)},e_{F(X)}\rangle
defined by

\Delta_{F(X)}=F(X)\xrightarrow{F(\Delta_{X})}F(X\oslash X)\xrightarrow{{m^{-1}_{\oslash}}_{X,X}}F(X)\oslash F(X)

e_{F(X)}=F(X)\xrightarrow{F(t_{X})}F(I)\xrightarrow{m^{-1}_{I}}I

is a cocommutative comonoid. As such,
F
canonically extends to a cartesian functor between the categories of cocommutative comonoids
\mathrm{C}[F]\,\colon\mathrm{C}[\cX]\rightarrow\mathrm{C}[\cY]
.

2.

Given a monoidal transformation between strong symmetric monoidal functors

\alpha\,\colon F\Rightarrow G\,\colon\cX\rightarrow\cY
and a cocommutative comonoid
\langle X,\Delta_{X},e_{X}\rangle
in
\cX
, the morphism
\alpha_{X}\,\colon F(X)\rightarrow G(X)
is a comonoid morphism. As such,
\alpha
canonically extends to cartesian transformation
\mathrm{C}[\alpha]\,\colon\mathrm{C}[F]\Rightarrow\mathrm{C}[G]
.

We are finally ready state Fox’s theorem:

Theorem 15
.

[
21
]

The functor
\mathrm{C}[-]\,\colon\SMC\rightarrow\CART
, mapping a symmetric monoidal category to its category of cocommutative comonoids and comonoid morphisms, is right adjoint to the inclusion functor
\inc\,\colon\mathbf{CART}\rightarrow\mathbf{SMC}
.

An incredibly useful corollary is the following characterization of cartesian categories:

Corollary 16
.

[
21
]

A symmetric monoidal category
\cX
is cartesian if and only if it is isomorphic to its category of cocommutative comonoids
\mathrm{C}[\cX]
.

This previous result can be unwrapped as a statement about the existence of certain natural transformations, as was stated in
[
25
]
.

Corollary 17
.

[
25
, Thm 4.28]

A symmetric monoidal category
\cX
is cartesian if and only if there are natural transformations

\displaystyle e\,\colon 1_{\mbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\mathcal{X}}$}}\Rightarrow I\,\colon\cX\rightarrow\cX

\displaystyle e_{A}\,\colon A\rightarrow I

\displaystyle\Delta\,\colon 1_{\mbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\mathcal{X}}$}}\Rightarrow copy_{\mbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\mathcal{X}}$}};\oslash\,\colon\cX\rightarrow\cX

\displaystyle\Delta_{A}\,\colon A\rightarrow A\oslash A

such that,
\forall A\in\cX,(A,\Delta_{A},e_{A})
is a cocommutative comonoid and,
\forall A,B\in\cX
,

\displaystyle e_{A\oslash B}=(e_{A}\oslash e_{B});\rho_{I}^{-1}

\displaystyle e_{I}=1_{I}

\displaystyle\Delta_{A\oslash B}=(\Delta_{A}\oslash\Delta_{B});s_{A,A,B,B}

\displaystyle\Delta_{I}=\rho_{I}

Of course, there is a dual statement for cocartesian categories, by taking the category of commutative monoids and monoid morphisms in a symmetric monoidal category. We quickly outline these definitions as they will be used throughout the work.

Definition 18
.

Let
\cX
denote a monoidal category.

•

A
monoid
in
\cX
is a comonoid in
\cX^{op}
, i.e. a triple
\langle X,\nabla_{X},u_{X}\rangle
of an object
A
in
\cX
equipped with two morphisms, the
multiplication
and the
unit

\nabla_{X}\,\colon X\oslash X\rightarrow X\qquad\qquad u_{X}\,\colon I\rightarrow X

satisfying

(16)

(18)

3
Linearly Distributive Categories

Girard introduced linear logic in 1987 to capture the constructive nature of intuitionistic logic and the inherent dualities of classical logic
[
23
]
. The categorical semantics for linear logic were subsequently explored by Seely, who identified that the appropriate framework were
*
-autonomous categories
[
35
]
, previously introduced by Barr
[
3
]
. In this context, the monoidal product is the multiplicative conjunction, while the involutive functor
*
is negation.

Linearly distributive categories (LDC) were introduced by Cockett and Seely as an alternative framework for the semantics of linear logic to
*
-autonomous categories.
[
15
]
. Unlike
*
-autonomous categories, LDCs take multiplicative conjunction and disjunction as primitive. Therefore, they consist of two monoidal structures, which interact via linear distributivities. Monoidal products connected through negation and de Morgan duality inherently possess such a linear distribution. Thus,
*
-autonomous categories are LDCs. However, there are significant examples of LDCs without negation, making them more general.

The current work generalizes the Fox theorem to the context of LDCs in order to characterize the notable subclass of cartesian LDCs (CLDC). In this section, we introduce all the necessary background on LDCs needed, as developed by co-authors Cockett and Seely in the series of papers
[
15
]
,
[
14
]
and
[
16
]
.

3.1
Definition, Negation, Mix and Examples

Definition 19
.

[
15
, Sec 2.1]

A
linearly distributive category

(\mbox{${\mathbb{X}}$},\otimes,\top,\oplus,\bot)
consists of:

•

a category
(\mbox{${\mathbb{X}}$},;,1_{A})
,

•

a
tensor
monoidal structure
(\mbox{${\mathbb{X}}$},\otimes,\top)

–

the
tensor
product and
top
unit functor

\displaystyle\otimes\,\colon\mbox{${\mathbb{X}}$}\times\mbox{${\mathbb{X}}$}\rightarrow\mbox{${\mathbb{X}}$}

\displaystyle\top\,\colon{\bf 1}\rightarrow\mbox{${\mathbb{X}}$}

–

the
\otimes
-associator, right
\otimes
-unitor, and left
\otimes
-unitor natural isomorphisms

\displaystyle\alpha_{\otimes}\,\colon(\otimes\times 1_{\mbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\mathbb{X}}$}});\otimes\Rightarrow(1_{\mbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\mathbb{X}}$}}\times\otimes);\otimes

\displaystyle{\alpha_{\otimes}}_{A,B,C}\,\colon(A\otimes B)\otimes C\rightarrow A\otimes(B\otimes C)

\displaystyle u_{\otimes}^{R}\,\colon 1_{\mbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\mathbb{X}}$}}\Rightarrow 1_{\mbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\mathbb{X}}$}}\otimes\top

\displaystyle{u_{\otimes}^{R}}_{A}\,\colon A\rightarrow A\otimes\top

\displaystyle u_{\otimes}^{L}\,\colon 1_{\mbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\mathbb{X}}$}}\Rightarrow\top\otimes 1_{\mbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\mathbb{X}}$}}

\displaystyle{u_{\otimes}^{L}}_{A}\,\colon A\rightarrow\top\otimes A

such that
(\mbox{${\mathbb{X}}$},\otimes,\top,\alpha_{\otimes},u_{\otimes}^{R},u_{\otimes}^{L})
is a monoidal category,

•

a
par
monoidal structure
(\mbox{${\mathbb{X}}$},\oplus,\bot)

–

the
par
product and
bottom
unit functor

\displaystyle\oplus\,\colon\mbox{${\mathbb{X}}$}\times\mbox{${\mathbb{X}}$}\rightarrow\mbox{${\mathbb{X}}$}

\displaystyle\bot\,\colon{\bf 1}\rightarrow\mbox{${\mathbb{X}}$}

–

the
\oplus
-associator, right
\oplus
-unitor, and left
\oplus
-unitor natural isomorphisms

\displaystyle\alpha_{\oplus}\,\colon(\otimes\times 1_{\mbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\mathbb{X}}$}});\otimes\Rightarrow(1_{\mbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\mathbb{X}}$}}\times\otimes);\otimes

\displaystyle{\alpha_{\oplus}}_{A,B,C}\,\colon A\oplus(B\oplus C)\rightarrow(A\oplus B)\oplus C

\displaystyle u_{\oplus}^{R}\,\colon 1_{\mbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\mathbb{X}}$}}\oplus\bot\Rightarrow 1_{\mbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\mathbb{X}}$}}\

\displaystyle{u_{\oplus}^{R}}_{A}\,\colon A\oplus\bot\rightarrow A

\displaystyle u_{\oplus}^{L}\,\colon\bot\oplus 1_{\mbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\mathbb{X}}$}}\Rightarrow 1_{\mbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\mathbb{X}}$}}

\displaystyle{u_{\oplus}^{L}}_{A}\,\colon\bot\oplus A\rightarrow A

such that
(\mbox{${\mathbb{X}}$},\oplus,\bot,{\alpha_{\oplus}}^{-1},{u_{\oplus}^{R}}^{-1},{u_{\oplus}^{L}}^{-1})
is a monoidal category,

•

left and right linear distributivity natural transformations

\displaystyle\delta^{R}\,\colon(\oplus\times 1_{\mbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\mathbb{X}}$}});\otimes\Rightarrow(1_{\mbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\mathbb{X}}$}}\times\otimes);\oplus

\displaystyle\delta^{R}_{A,B,C}\,\colon(A\oplus B)\otimes C\rightarrow A\oplus(B\otimes C),

\displaystyle\delta^{L}\,\colon(1_{\mbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\mathbb{X}}$}}\times\oplus);\otimes\Rightarrow(\otimes\times 1_{\mbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\mathbb{X}}$}});\oplus

\displaystyle\delta^{L}_{A,B,C}\,\colon A\otimes(B\oplus C)\rightarrow(A\otimes B)\oplus C,

satisfying coherence conditions between units and linear distributivities (
19
), associativities and linear distributivities (
20
), and left and right linear distributivities (
21
).

\displaystyle\begin{split}&{u_{\otimes}^{L}}_{A\oplus B};\delta^{L}_{\top,A,B}={u_{\otimes}^{L}}_{A}\oplus 1_{B}\\

120.22827pt\raise-57.88892pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.55554pt\hbox{$\textstyle{}$}}}}}}}{\hbox{\kern 201.3424pt\raise-57.88892pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.55554pt\hbox{$\textstyle{(A\oplus(B\otimes C))\oplus D}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces\end{gathered}

(21)

\displaystyle\delta^{L}_{A\oplus B,C,D};(\delta^{R}_{A,B,C}\oplus 1_{D})=\delta^{R}_{A,B,C\oplus D};(1_{A}\oplus\delta^{L}_{B,C,D});{\alpha_{\oplus}}_{A,B\otimes C,D}

\displaystyle(\delta^{L}_{A,B,C}\otimes 1_{D});\delta^{R}_{A\otimes B,C,D}={\alpha_{\otimes}}_{A,B\oplus C,D};(1_{A}\otimes\delta^{R}_{B,C,D});\delta^{L}_{A,B,C\otimes D}

Remark 20
.

The reason the structure isomorphisms of the
\oplus
-monoidal structure having the domain and codomain in a different direction that for the
\otimes
-monoidal structure is due to how these inferences are proved in multiplicative linear logic and the graphical language associated with LDCs
[
5
]
. The
\oplus
-unitors and
\oplus
-associator arise more “naturally” in the direction chosen and are in agreement with the notation introduced in Cockett and Seely’s later work with Koslowski on linear bicategories
[
17
]
.

Definition 21
.

[
15
, Sec 3]

A linearly distributive category
(\mbox{${\mathbb{X}}$},\otimes,\top,\oplus,\bot)
is
symmetric
if

•

(\mbox{${\mathbb{X}}$},\otimes,\top)
is a symmetric monoidal category with
\otimes
-braiding

\sigma_{\otimes}\,\colon\otimes\Rightarrow switch_{\mbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\mathbb{X}}$},\mbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\mathbb{X}}$}};\otimes\qquad{\sigma_{\otimes}}_{A,B}\,\colon A\otimes B\rightarrow B\otimes A

•

(\mbox{${\mathbb{X}}$},\oplus,\bot)
is a symmetric monoidal category with
\oplus
-braiding

\sigma_{\oplus}\,\colon\oplus\Rightarrow switch_{\mbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\mathbb{X}}$},\mbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\mathbb{X}}$}};\oplus\qquad{\sigma_{\oplus}}_{A,B}\,\colon A\oplus B\rightarrow B\oplus A

such that the linear distributivities interact coherently with the braidings (
22
).

(22)

Many models of multiplicative linear logic, in particular the ones we are interested in, also satisfy an additional rule: the binary MIX rule
A\otimes B\vdash A\oplus B
. With the cut rule present, satisfying the binary MIX rule is equivalent to satisfying the nullary MIX rule
\bot\vdash\top
. In terms of LDCs, this amounts to the existence of a map
m\,\colon\bot\rightarrow\top
such that the two possible induced maps
A\otimes B\rightarrow A\oplus B
are equal. This map will be essential to the following discussions.

Definition 22
.

[
14
, Def 6.2]

A linearly distributive category
{\mathbb{X}}
is
mix
if there is a morphism
m\,\colon\bot\rightarrow\top
such that

167.82458pt\raise-57.88892pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.55554pt\hbox{$\textstyle{A\oplus B}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces\end{gathered}

(23)

In the process of developing categorical semantics fo classical logic based on LDCs, Führmann and Pym gave a rather useful way to determine whether or not a LDC is mix.

Lemma 23
.

[
22
, Lem 3.10]

A linearly distributive category
{\mathbb{X}}
with a map
m\,\colon\bot\rightarrow\top
is mix if and only if

(1_{\bot}\otimes m);{u_{\otimes}^{R}}_{\bot}^{-1}=(m\otimes 1_{\bot});{u_{\otimes}^{L}}_{\bot}^{-1}\,\colon\bot\otimes\bot\rightarrow\bot

(24)

If the LDC is symmetric, the above lemma was then utilized to determine that, if
\bot
has a
\otimes
-comonoid structure or if
\top
has a
\oplus
-monoid structure, the LDC is mix.

Theorem 24
.

[
22
, Thm 3.11]

Every symmetric linearly distributive category with a
\otimes
-comonoid

\Delta_{\bot}\,\colon\bot\rightarrow\bot\otimes\bot\qquad e_{\bot}\,\colon\bot\rightarrow\top

is mix with
m=e_{\bot}
. Dually, every symmetric linearly distributive category with a
\oplus
-monoid

\nabla_{\top}\,\colon\top\oplus\top\rightarrow\top\qquad u_{\top}\,\colon\bot\rightarrow\top

is mix with
m=u_{\top}
.

There is a stronger version of the nullary MIX rule, which often holds in categorical models:
\bot\dashv\vdash\top
. In this case,
\bot\cong\top
and we call such LDCs isomix.

Definition 25
.

[
14
, Def 6.5]

An linearly distributive category is
isomix
if it is mix and
m\,\colon\bot\rightarrow\top
is an isomorphism.

In fact, we do not need to check (
23
) if the two monoidal units are isomorphic:

Lemma 26
.

[
14
, Lem 6.6]

A linearly distributive category where
\top\cong\bot
is isomix.

We can also consider LDCs where the tensor monoidal product and the parr monoidal product are isomorphic to one another.

Definition 27
.

[
18
, Sec 2.3]

A linearly distributive category is
compact
if it is isomix and the mix maps are isomorphisms.

While LDCs make multiplicative conjunction and disjunction primitive, we also want to be able to consider negation in this categorical framework and have it recover
*
-autonomous categories. We shall present the negation in terms of complemented objects.

Definition 28
.

[
16
, Def A.5]

Consider a linearly distributive category
{\mathbb{X}}
. The objects
A,A^{c}
in
{\mathbb{X}}
form a complementation pair if there are morphisms

\gamma\,\colon A\otimes A^{c}\rightarrow\bot\qquad\qquad\tau\,\colon\top\rightarrow A^{c}\oplus A

such that

(25)

The pair is denoted by
(A,A^{c},\gamma,\tau)
.
A
is said to be the left complement and
A^{c}
is the right complement.

Definition 29
.

[
15
, Def 4.1, 4.3]

A linearly distributive category has
negation
if there are object function
(-)^{\perp}
and
{{}^{\perp}(-)}
, together with the following parametrized family of maps

\displaystyle\gamma_{A}^{R}\,\colon A\otimes A^{\perp}\rightarrow\bot

\displaystyle\tau^{R}_{A}\,\colon\top\rightarrow A\oplus{{}^{\perp}A}

\displaystyle\gamma_{A}^{L}\,\colon{{}^{\perp}A}\otimes A\rightarrow\bot

\displaystyle\tau^{L}_{A}\,\colon\top\rightarrow A^{\perp}\oplus A

such
(A,A^{\perp},\gamma^{R}_{A},\tau^{L}_{A})
and
({{}^{\perp}A},A,\gamma^{L}_{A},\tau^{R}_{A})
form a complementation pairs.

A symmetric linear distributive category has
negation
if additionally
(-)^{\perp}={{}^{\perp}(-)}
and

\gamma_{A}^{L}=A^{\perp}\otimes A\xrightarrow{{\sigma_{\otimes}}_{A^{\perp},A}}A\otimes A^{\perp}\xrightarrow{\gamma^{R}_{A}}\bot\qquad\qquad\tau^{L}_{A}=\top\xrightarrow{\tau^{R}_{A}}A\oplus A^{\perp}\xrightarrow{{\sigma_{\oplus}}_{A,A^{\perp}}}A^{\perp}\oplus A

We shall only consider the symmetric case. Notice that the above definition does not require
(-)^{\perp}
to act on morphisms, nor does it ask for the many natural isomorphisms which connect the multiplicative connectives and negation together. These are in fact a consequence of the above structure, and excluding them from the definition allows it to be much simpler to verify in examples. We outline in the following lemma how
(-)^{\perp}
defines a functor and some of the isomorphisms we will need in Section
5.3
.

Lemma 30
.

[
15
, Lem 4.4]

In a symmetric linearly distributive category with negation
(\mbox{${\mathbb{X}}$},\otimes,\top,\oplus,\bot)
, the object function
(-)^{\perp}
is a contravariant functor, which acts on morphisms as follows. Given
f\,\colon A\rightarrow B
in
{\mathbb{X}}
,
f^{\perp}\,\colon B^{\perp}\rightarrow A^{\perp}
is defined by

\begin{gathered}\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.55554pt\hbox{\kern 96.30331pt\raise-67.4775pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-1.9037pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{{\gamma^{L}_{B}}\oplus 1_{A^{\perp}}}$}}}\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces\end{gathered}

and we have the following adjunctions

\displaystyle A\otimes-\dashv A^{\perp}\oplus-

\displaystyle A^{\perp}\otimes-\dashv A\oplus-

\displaystyle-\otimes B\dashv-\oplus B^{\perp}

\displaystyle-\otimes B^{\perp}\dashv-\oplus B

corresponding to the following bijections

\displaystyle\frac{A\otimes B\rightarrow B}{B\rightarrow A^{\perp}\oplus C}

\displaystyle\frac{A^{\perp}\otimes B\rightarrow C}{B\rightarrow A\oplus C}

\displaystyle\frac{A\otimes B\rightarrow C}{A\rightarrow C\oplus B^{\perp}}

\displaystyle\frac{A\otimes B^{\perp}\rightarrow C}{A\rightarrow C\oplus B}

Moreover, there are following invertible morphisms and natural isomorphisms

\displaystyle\beta_{\top}\,\colon\bot^{\perp}\rightarrow\top

\displaystyle\epsilon_{A,B}\,\colon(A\oplus B)^{\perp}\rightarrow B^{\perp}\otimes A^{\perp}

\displaystyle\chi_{\bot}\,\colon\bot\rightarrow\top^{\perp}

\displaystyle\phi_{A,B}\,\colon A^{\perp}\oplus B^{\perp}\rightarrow(B\otimes A)^{\perp}

Definition 31
.

A
*
-autonomous category is a symmetric monoidal category
(\mbox{${\mathbb{X}}$},\otimes,\top)
equipped with a full and faithful functor

(-)^{\perp}\,\colon\mbox{${\mathbb{X}}$}^{op}\rightarrow\mbox{${\mathbb{X}}$}

such that there is a natural isomorphism
\mbox{${\mathbb{X}}$}(A,\otimes B,C^{\perp})\cong\mbox{${\mathbb{X}}$}(A,(B\otimes C)^{\perp})
.

Theorem 32
.

[
15
, Thm 4.5]

The notions of symmetric linearly distributive categories with negation and
*
-autonomous categories coincide.

Example 33
.

Let us list three important class of examples of LDCs.

1.

Every monoidal category
(\cX,\oslash,I)
can be viewed as a linearly distributive category, when taking the tensor and par structures to be equal to the original monoidal structure, i.e.
\otimes=\oplus=\oslash
and
\top=\bot=I
. In this case, the linear distributivities are just
\oslash
-associators. We call such LDCs
(\cX,\oslash,I,\oslash,I)

degenerate
.

2.

By Theorem
32
, every
*
-autonomous category
(\cX,\oslash,I,\star)
is a linearly distributive category, with the tensor structure given by the original monoidal structure and the par structure given by the de Morgan dual, i.e.
A\oplus B=(B^{\star}\oslash A^{\star})^{\star}
and
\bot=I^{\star}
. Some notable examples of
*
-autonomous categories include the category of finite-dimensional vector spaces, the category of sup-lattices, the category of coherence spaces and the category of finiteness spaces.

3.

Cockett and Seely introduced a class of examples known as
shifted tensor
LDCs in
[
13
]
. These are the categorical analogue of shift monoids and are defined as follows.

Definition 34
.

[
16
, Sec 5.2]

Consider a monoidal category
(\mbox{${\mathbb{X}}$},\otimes,\top)
. An object
\bot\in\mbox{${\mathbb{X}}$}
is said to have a
tensor inverse
if there is an object
\bot^{-1}
equipped with two isomorphisms

s^{L}\,\colon\bot\otimes\bot^{-1}\rightarrow\top\qquad s^{R}\,\colon\bot^{-1}\otimes\bot\rightarrow\top

such that

\begin{gathered}\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.55554pt\hbox{\kern 6.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 88.58347pt\raise-55.19832pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawline@}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawline@}}{\hbox{\kern-3.0pt\raise-59.16895pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.55554pt\hbox{$\textstyle{}$}}}}}}}{\hbox{\kern 67.62508pt\raise-59.16895pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.55554pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\bot^{-1}}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces\end{gathered}

Suppose there is an object
\bot\in\mbox{${\mathbb{X}}$}
with a tensor inverse, then define a monoidal product by
A\oplus B=A\otimes(\bot^{-1}\otimes B)
, known as the
\bot
-shifted tensor
.

Proposition 35
.

[
13
, Prop 5.3, 5.4]

Consider a monoidal category
(\mbox{${\mathbb{X}}$},\otimes,\top)
with an object
\bot\in\mbox{${\mathbb{X}}$}
with a tensor inverse, then
(\mbox{${\mathbb{X}}$},\otimes,\top,\oplus,\bot)
, where
\oplus
is the
\bot
-shifted tensor, is a linearly distributive category with invertible linear distributivities. Moreover, every linearly distributive categories with invertible linear distributivities has a tensor inverse for
\bot
whose shift tensor product is naturally equivalent to the parr product.

3.2
Linear Functors and Transformations

In the same spirit as the Fox theorem, we will need the appropriate notions of functors and transformations between linearly distributive categories.

Definition 36
.

[
16
, Def 1]

Let
{\mathbb{X}}
and
{\mathbb{Y}}
be linearly distributive categories. A
linear functor

F=(F_{\otimes},F_{\oplus})\,\colon\mbox{${\mathbb{X}}$}\rightarrow\mbox{${\mathbb{Y}}$}
consists of:

•

a monoidal functor
(F_{\otimes},m_{\top},m_{\otimes})\,\colon(\mbox{${\mathbb{X}}$},\otimes,\top)\rightarrow(\mbox{${\mathbb{Y}}$},\otimes,\top)
, equipped with

–

a morphism
m_{\top}\,\colon\top\rightarrow F_{\otimes}(\top)
,

–

a natural transformation

\displaystyle m_{\otimes}\,\colon(F_{\otimes}\times F_{\otimes});\otimes\Rightarrow\otimes;F_{\otimes}

\displaystyle{m_{\otimes}}_{A,B}\,\colon F_{\otimes}(A)\otimes F_{\otimes}(B)\rightarrow F_{\otimes}(A\otimes B)

•

a comonoidal functor
(F_{\oplus},n_{\bot},n_{\oplus})\,\colon(\mbox{${\mathbb{X}}$},\oplus,\bot)\rightarrow(\mbox{${\mathbb{Y}}$},\oplus,\bot)
, equipped with

–

a morphism
n_{\bot}\,\colon F_{\oplus}(\bot)\rightarrow\bot
,

–

a natural transformation

\displaystyle n_{\oplus}\,\colon\oplus;F_{\oplus}\Rightarrow(F_{\oplus}\times F_{\oplus});\oplus

\displaystyle{n_{\oplus}}_{A,B}\,\colon F_{\oplus}(A\oplus B)\rightarrow F_{\oplus}(A)\oplus F_{\oplus}(B)

•

four natural transformations, known as
linear strengths
,

\displaystyle v_{\otimes}^{R}\,\colon\oplus;F_{\otimes}\Rightarrow(F_{\oplus}\times F_{\otimes});\oplus

\displaystyle{v_{\otimes}^{R}}_{A,B}\,\colon F_{\otimes}(A\oplus B)\rightarrow F_{\oplus}(A)\oplus F_{\otimes}(B)

\displaystyle v_{\otimes}^{L}\,\colon\oplus;F_{\otimes}\Rightarrow(F_{\otimes}\times F_{\oplus});\oplus

\displaystyle{v_{\otimes}^{L}}_{A,B}\,\colon F_{\otimes}(A\oplus B)\rightarrow F_{\otimes}(A)\oplus F_{\oplus}(B)

\displaystyle v_{\oplus}^{R}\,\colon(F_{\otimes}\times F_{\oplus});\otimes\Rightarrow\otimes;F_{\oplus}

\displaystyle{v_{\oplus}^{R}}_{A,B}\,\colon F_{\otimes}(A)\otimes F_{\oplus}(B)\rightarrow F_{\oplus}(A\otimes B)

\displaystyle v_{\oplus}^{L}\,\colon(F_{\oplus}\times F_{\otimes});\otimes\Rightarrow\otimes;F_{\oplus}

\displaystyle{v_{\oplus}^{L}}_{A,B}\,\colon F_{\oplus}(A)\otimes F_{\otimes}(B)\rightarrow F_{\oplus}(A\otimes B)

subject to various coherence conditions determining the interaction of the linear strengths with the units (
26
), with the associativities (
27
), with the linear distributivities (
28
), and with other linear strengths via associators (
29
) and via linear distributivities (
30
).

\displaystyle{\nu^{R}_{\oplus}}_{A\oplus B,C};F_{\oplus}(\delta^{R}_{A,B,C});{n_{\oplus}}_{A,B\otimes C}=({\nu^{R}_{\otimes}}_{A,B}\otimes 1_{F_{\oplus}(C)});\delta^{R}_{F_{\oplus}(A),F_{\otimes}(B),F_{\oplus}(C)};(1_{F_{\oplus}(A)}\oplus{\nu^{R}_{\oplus}}_{B,C})

\displaystyle{\nu^{L}_{\oplus}}_{A,B\oplus C};F_{\oplus}(\delta^{L}_{A,B,C});{n_{\oplus}}_{A\otimes B,C}=(1_{F_{\oplus}(A)}\otimes{\nu^{L}_{\otimes}}_{B,C});\delta^{L}_{F_{\oplus}(A),F_{\otimes}(B),F_{\oplus}(C)};({\nu^{L}_{\oplus}}_{A,B}\oplus 1_{F_{\oplus}(C)})

If
{\mathbb{X}}
and
{\mathbb{Y}}
are symmetric linearly distributive categories, then a linear functor
F=(F_{\otimes},F_{\oplus})
is
symmetric
if

•

F_{\otimes}\,\colon(\mbox{${\mathbb{X}}$},\otimes,\top)\rightarrow(\mbox{${\mathbb{Y}}$},\otimes,\top)
is a symmetric monoidal functor,

•

F_{\oplus}\,\colon(\mbox{${\mathbb{X}}$},\oplus,\bot)\rightarrow(\mbox{${\mathbb{Y}}$},\oplus,\bot)
is a symmetric comonoidal functor, and

•

the linear strengths interact coherently with the braidings (
31
)

(31)

Remark 37
.

In the symmetric case, we can alternatively drop the requirement that the left (or right) linear strengths exist instead of adding (
31
). This remains a linear functor as we can always define the missing pair of linear strengths via the braiding. Therefore, when defining symmetric linear functor, we often only give one pair of linear strengths.

Definition 38
.

[
16
, Def 3]

Let
F,G\,\colon\mbox{${\mathbb{X}}$}\rightarrow\mbox{${\mathbb{Y}}$}
be linear functors between linearly distributive categories. A
linear transformation

\alpha\,\colon F\Rightarrow G
consists of:

•

a monoidal transformation
\alpha_{\otimes}\,\colon(F_{\otimes},m_{\top}^{F},m_{\otimes}^{F})\Rightarrow(G_{\otimes},m_{\top}^{G},m_{\otimes}^{G})
and

•

a comonoidal transformation
\alpha_{\oplus}\,\colon(G_{\oplus},n_{\bot}^{G},n_{\oplus}^{G})\Rightarrow(F_{\oplus},n_{\bot}^{F},n_{\oplus}^{F})
,

which commute coherently with the linear strengths of
F
and
G
(
32
).

0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 72.27327pt\raise-57.88892pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.55554pt\hbox{$\textstyle{F_{\oplus}(A)\oplus G_{\otimes}(B)}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces\end{gathered}

(32)

\displaystyle{\alpha_{\otimes}}_{A\oplus B};{v_{\otimes}^{R}}^{G}_{A,B};({\alpha_{\oplus}}_{A}\oplus 1_{G_{\otimes}(B)})={v_{\otimes}^{R}}^{F}_{A,B};(1_{F_{\oplus}(A)}\oplus{\alpha_{\otimes}}_{B})

\displaystyle{\alpha_{\otimes}}_{A\oplus B};{v_{\otimes}^{L}}^{G}_{A,B};(1_{G_{\otimes}(A)}\oplus{\alpha_{\oplus}}_{B})={v_{\otimes}^{L}}^{F}_{A,B};({\alpha_{\otimes}}_{A}\oplus 1_{F_{\oplus}(B)})

\displaystyle(1_{G_{\oplus}(A)}\otimes{\alpha_{\otimes}}_{B});{v_{\oplus}^{L}}^{G}_{A,B};{\alpha_{\oplus}}_{A\otimes B}=({\alpha_{\oplus}}_{A}\otimes 1_{F_{\otimes}(B)});{v_{\oplus}^{L}}^{F}_{A,B}

\displaystyle({\alpha_{\otimes}}_{A}\otimes 1_{G_{\oplus}(B)});{v_{\oplus}^{R}}^{G}_{A,B};{\alpha_{\oplus}}_{A\otimes B}=(1_{F_{\otimes}(A)}\otimes{\alpha_{\oplus}}_{B});{v_{\oplus}^{R}}^{F}_{A,B}

Proposition 39
.

[
16
, Prop 4]

Linearly distributive categories, linear functors, and linear transformations form a 2-category, which is denoted by
\LDC
. Restricting to symmetric linearly distributive categories and symmetric linear functors equally gives a 2-category, denoted
\mathbf{SLDC}
. Both these 2-categories are closed under products.

While not defined in their paper
[
16
]
, it is immediate that we can extend the notion of strong monoidal functors to the context of linear functors:

Definition 40
.

A linear functor
F\ =(F_{\otimes},F_{\oplus})\,\colon\mbox{${\mathbb{X}}$}\rightarrow\mbox{${\mathbb{Y}}$}
is
strong
if

•

F_{\otimes}\,\colon(\mbox{${\mathbb{X}}$},\otimes,\top)\rightarrow(\mbox{${\mathbb{Y}}$},\otimes,\top)
is a strong monoidal functor, and

•

F_{\oplus}\,\colon(\mbox{${\mathbb{X}}$},\oplus,\bot)\rightarrow(\mbox{${\mathbb{Y}}$},\oplus,\bot)
is a strong comonoidal functor.

We will also consider linear functors whose component functors are equal. These were first defined by
[
6
]
and then further explored by
[
18
]
.

Definition 41
.

[
18
, Def 3.1]

Consider linearly distributive categories
{\mathbb{X}}
and
{\mathbb{Y}}
, a a linear functor
F=(F_{\otimes},F_{\oplus})\,\colon\mbox{${\mathbb{X}}$}\rightarrow\mbox{${\mathbb{Y}}$}
is
Frobenius
if

1.

F_{\otimes}=F_{\oplus}
,

2.

{\nu^{R}_{\otimes}}_{A,B}={\nu^{L}_{\otimes}}_{A,B}={n_{\oplus}}_{A,B}
, and

3.

{\nu^{R}_{\oplus}}_{A,B}={\nu^{L}_{\oplus}}_{A,B}={m_{\otimes}}_{A,B}
.

Given the degeneracy, we can give an alternative characterization of such linear functors.

Proposition 42
.

[
18
, Lem 3.2]

Consider linearly distributive categories
{\mathbb{X}}
and
{\mathbb{Y}}
, then the following notions coincide:

•

Frobenius linear functors
F=(F_{\otimes},F_{\oplus})\,\colon\mbox{${\mathbb{X}}$}\rightarrow\mbox{${\mathbb{Y}}$}
, and

•

\otimes
-monoidal-
\oplus
-comonoidal functors
(F,m_{\otimes},m_{\top},n_{\oplus},n_{\bot})\,\colon\mbox{${\mathbb{X}}$}\rightarrow\mbox{${\mathbb{Y}}$}
satisfying (
33
).

(36)

Lemma 45
.

[
18
, Lem 3.10]

Suppose
F,G\,\colon\mbox{${\mathbb{X}}$}\rightarrow\mbox{${\mathbb{Y}}$}
are Frobenius linear functors and
\alpha\,\colon F\Rightarrow G
is a
\otimes
-monoidal and
\oplus
-comonoidal natural isomorphism, then
(\alpha,\alpha^{-1})\,\colon F\Rightarrow G
is a linear transformation.

As Frobenius linear functors compose and identity linear functors are Frobenius, restricting to such 1-cells determines a sub-2-category.

Proposition 46
.

Linearly distributive categories, Frobenius linear functors and linear transformations form a sub-2-category of
\LDC
, denoted by
\mathbf{FLDC}

3.3
Cartesian Linearly Distributive Categories

We now introduce cartesian LDCs, the subclass we are interested in characterizing via the linearly distributive Fox theorem.

Definition 47
.

[
15
, Sec 2]

A
cartesian linearly distributive category

{\mathbb{X}}
is a symmetric linearly distributive category whose tensor
\otimes
is the categorical product
\times
in
{\mathbb{X}}
, with top
\top
the terminal object
\bone
in
{\mathbb{X}}
, and par
\oplus
is the categorical coproduct
+
in
{\mathbb{X}}
, with
\bot
the initial object
\bzero
in
{\mathbb{X}}
.

The development of the linearly distributive Fox theorem renewed interest in cartesian LDCs, which were further explored in their own right by the author and co-author Jean-Simon Pacaud Lemay in the article “Cartesian Linearly Distributive Categories Revisited”
[
27
]
. This work examines the distinctive structures and properties of cartesian LDCs, discusses key classes of examples, and reassesses previously proposed instances of cartesian LDCs. We recommend reading both articles, as they complement each other, though each is designed to be accessible and valuable independently.

Cartesian LDCs themselves form a 2-category with the appropriate notion of linear functor as 1-cells, mirroring the strong monoidal functors in the 2-category of cartesian categories.

Proposition 48
.

There is a sub 2-category of
\LDC
consisting of cartesian linearly distributive categories, strong symmetric linear functors, known as
cartesian linear functors
, and linear transformations, denoted by
\CLDC
, closed under products. Moreover, there is a sub 2-category of
\LDC
consisting of cartesian linearly distributive categories, strong symmetric Frobenius linear functors and linear transformations, denoted by
\mathbf{FCLDC}
.

4
Duoidal Categories

Linearly distributive categories are of course not the only categories with two monoidal structures. Duoidal categories represent another variant, characterized by two monoidal products that interact via additional structure maps, most notably the interchange law. Unlike LDCs, the duoidal structure arises canonically when considering finite products and coproducts. The theory of duoidal categories will play a crucial role in the definitions and results presented in this paper, so we present all the necessary background in this section.

The earliest appearance of a form of the interchange law between monoidal structures in the literature is found in Joyal and Street’s work on braided monoidal categories
[
26
]
. They demonstrate that a duoidal category with structure maps that are isomorphisms corresponds to a braided monoidal category via a categorical Eckmann-Hilton argument. Subsequently, Balteanu, Fiedorowicz, Schwänzl, and Vogt defined 2-fold monoidal categories as monoids in the monoidal category of monoidal categories
[
2
]
. In their definition, the interchange law is not required to be an isomorphism, but the unit objects of both monoidal structures are assumed to coincide, and the monoidal structures themselves are strict. Building on this foundation, Forcey, Siehler, and Sowers generalized the definition by allowing distinct unit objects and non-strict associators, while retaining equalities for unitors and certain structure maps
[
20
]
.

The currently accepted definition of duoidal category was first introduced by Aguiar and Mahajan under the name 2-monoidal categories
[
1
]
1
1

1

We will refrain from using the 2-monoidal category terminology introduced by Aguiar and Mahajan to avoid confusion with monoidal 2-categories, which are 2-categories with a coherent monoidal structure.
. The background on duoidal categories presented in this section is almost entirely based on Chapter 6 of their monograph, and readers seeking additional details are encouraged to consult it. The now-standard name for duoidal categories was suggested by Street and first appeared in the literature in
[
4
]
and
[
8
]
.

4.1
Definition, Symmetry and Examples

Definition 49
.

[
1
, Def 6.1]

A
duoidal category

(\cX,\diamond,I,\star,J)
is category
\cX
with two monoidal structures
(\cX,\diamond,I,\alpha_{\diamond},\rho_{\diamond},\lambda_{\diamond})
and
(\cX,\star,J,\alpha_{\star},\rho_{\star},\lambda_{\star})
equipped with morphisms

\displaystyle\Delta_{I}

\displaystyle\,\colon I\rightarrow I\star I

\displaystyle\qquad\mu_{J}

\displaystyle\,\colon J\diamond J\rightarrow J

\displaystyle\qquad\iota

\displaystyle\,\colon I\rightarrow J

and an
interchange
natural transformation

\displaystyle\zeta\,\colon(\star\times\star);\diamond\Rightarrow s_{\mbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\mathcal{X}}$},\mbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\mathcal{X}}$},\mbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\mathcal{X}}$},\mbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\mathcal{X}}$}};(\diamond\times\diamond);\star

\displaystyle\zeta_{A,B,C,D}\,\colon(A\star B)\diamond(C\star D)\rightarrow(A\diamond C)\star(B\diamond D)

such that

•

(J,\nabla_{J},\iota)
is a
\diamond
-monoid,

•

(I,\Delta_{I},\iota)
is a
\star
-comonoid,

•

interchange maps interact coherently with associativities (
37
) and with unitors (
38
).

(38)

\displaystyle{\lambda_{\diamond}}_{A\star B};(\Delta_{I}\diamond 1_{A\star B});\zeta_{I,I,A,B}={\lambda_{\diamond}}_{A}\star{\lambda_{\diamond}}_{B}\quad\quad\quad{\rho_{\diamond}}_{A\star B};(1_{A\star B}\diamond\Delta_{I});\zeta_{A,B,I,I}={\rho_{\diamond}}_{A}\star{\rho_{\diamond}}_{B}

\displaystyle({\lambda_{\star}}_{A}\diamond{\lambda_{\star}}_{B});\zeta_{J,A,J,B};(\mu_{J}\star 1_{A\diamond B})={\lambda_{\star}}_{A\diamond B}\quad\quad({\rho_{\star}}_{A}\diamond{\rho_{\star}}_{B});\zeta_{A,J,B,J};(1_{A\diamond B}\star\mu_{J})={\rho_{\star}}_{A\diamond B}

Definition 50
.

A duoidal category
(\cX,\diamond,I,\star,J)
is
normal
if
\iota^{-1}\,\colon I\rightarrow J
is invertible, in other words the units are isomorphic. Further, a duoidal category is
strong
if all its structure maps are invertible.

Definition 51
.

[
1
, Def 6.5]

A duoidal category
(\cX,\diamond,I,\star,J)
is
braided
if

•

(\cX,\diamond,I)
is a braided monoidal category with
\diamond
-braiding

\sigma_{\diamond}\,\colon\diamond\Rightarrow switch_{\mbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\mathcal{X}}$},\mbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\mathcal{X}}$}};\diamond\qquad{\sigma_{\diamond}}_{A,B}\,\colon A\diamond B\rightarrow B\diamond A

•

(\cX,\star,J)
is a braided monoidal category with
\oplus
-braiding

\sigma_{\star}\,\colon\star\Rightarrow switch_{\mbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\mathcal{X}}$},\mbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\mathcal{X}}$}};\star\qquad{\sigma_{\star}}_{A,B}\,\colon A\star B\rightarrow B\star A

such that

•

(J,\mu_{J},\iota)
is a braided
\diamond
-monoid and
(I,\Delta_{I},\iota)
is a cobraided
\star
-comonoid, i.e. (
39
)

•

interchange maps interact coherently with the braidings (
40
).

(40)

\displaystyle\zeta_{A,B,C,D};{\sigma_{\star}}_{A\diamond C,B\diamond D}=({\sigma_{\star}}_{A,B}\diamond{\sigma_{\star}}_{C,D});\zeta_{B,A,D,C}

\displaystyle\zeta_{A,B,C,D};({\sigma_{\diamond}}_{A,C}\star{\sigma_{\diamond}}_{B,D})={\sigma_{\diamond}}_{A\star B,C\star D};\zeta_{C,D,A,B}

The notion of symmetric duoidal categories has not yet appeared in the literature as such, since the motivation for duoidal structures is often braided monoidal categories, although it is a trivial extension of braided duoidal categories. Symmetry is essential to the Fox theorem and therefore will be a key component of this work.

Definition 52
.

A braided duoidal category
(\cX,\diamond,I,\star,J)
is
symmetric
if

•

(\cX,\diamond,I)
is a symmetric monoidal category, and

•

(\cX,\star,J)
is a symmetric monoidal category.

Given a symmetric duoidal category, there are two canonical flips, one for
\diamond
and for
\star
. We note here how these canonical flips interact with the duoidal structure maps.

Proposition 53
.

The following diagrams commute in any symmetric duoidal category:

Example 54
.

We list here some examples of duoidal categories.

1.

[
1
, Prop 6.10]
Every monoidal category
(\cX,\oslash,I)
with a braiding
\sigma
gives a duoidal category
(\cX,\oslash,I,\oslash,I)
with structure maps given by unitors, an identity map, and the canonical flip:

\displaystyle\Delta_{I}={u_{\otimes}}_{I}\,\colon I\rightarrow I\oslash I\qquad\mu_{I}={u_{\otimes}^{-1}}_{I}\,\colon I\oslash I\rightarrow I\qquad\iota=1_{I}\,\colon I\rightarrow I

\displaystyle\zeta_{A,B,C,B}=\tau_{A,B,C,D}\,\colon(A\oslash B)\oslash(C\oslash D)\rightarrow(A\oslash C)\oslash(B\oslash D)

Here we state Joyal and Street’s result about braided monoidal categories
[
26
]
in the terminology of duoidal categories:

Proposition 55
.

[
1
, Prop 6.11]

Given a strong duoidal category
(\cX,\diamond,I,\star,J)
, then
(\cX,\diamond,I)
and
(\cX,\star,J)
are isomorphic braided monoidal categories and the interchange natural transformation is the canonical flip.

4.2
Duoidal Functors and Transformations

Aguiar and Mahajan define two different types of functors between duoidal categories: bilax duoidal functors and double lax duoidal functors. We shall only need the former.

Definition 56
.

[
1
, Def 6.50]

Let
(\cX,\diamond,I,\star,J)
and
(\cY,\diamond,I,\star,J)
denote duoidal categories.

A
bilax duoidal functor

(F,p_{I},p_{\diamond},q_{J},q_{\star})\,\colon(\cX,\diamond,I,\star,J)\rightarrow(\cY,\diamond,I,\star,J)
is a functor
F\,\colon\cX\rightarrow\cY
such that

•

(F,p_{I},p_{\diamond})\,\colon(\cX,\diamond,I)\rightarrow(\cY,\diamond,I)
is a monoidal functor with

–

a morphism
p_{I}\,\colon I\rightarrow F(I)
,

–

a natural transformation

p_{\diamond}\,\colon(F\times F);\diamond\Rightarrow\diamond;F\qquad\qquad{p_{\diamond}}_{A,B}\,\colon F(A)\diamond F(B)\rightarrow F(A\diamond B)

•

(F,q_{J},q_{\star})\,\colon(\cX,\star,J)\rightarrow(\cY,\star,J)
is a comonoidal functor with

–

a morphism
q_{J}\,\colon F(J)\rightarrow J
,

–

a natural transformation

q_{\star}\,\colon\star;F\Rightarrow(F\times F);\star\qquad\qquad{q_{\star}}_{A,B}\,\colon F(A\star B)\rightarrow F(A)\star F(B)

such that (
43
) and (
44
).

(44)

Definition 57
.

[
1
, Def 6.51]

A
bilax duoidal transformation

\alpha\,\colon(F,p_{I}^{F},p_{\diamond}^{F},q_{J}^{F},q_{\star}^{F})\Rightarrow(G,p_{I}^{G},p_{\diamond}^{G},q_{J}^{G},q_{\star}^{G})
is a natural transformation
\alpha\,\colon F\rightarrow G
such that

•

\alpha\,\colon(F,p_{I}^{F},p_{\diamond}^{F})\Rightarrow(G,p_{I}^{G},p_{\diamond}^{G})
is a monoidal transformation and

•

\alpha\,\colon(F,q_{J}^{F},q_{\star}^{F})\Rightarrow(G,q_{J}^{G},q_{\star}^{G})
is a comonoidal transformation.

Proposition 58
.

[
1
, Prop 6.52]

There is a 2-category of duoidal categories, bilax duoidal functors and bilax duoidal transformations, denoted
\DUO
.

Just as a monoid in a monoidal category
\cX
can be equivalently defined as a monoidal functor from the terminal category to
\cX
, Aguiar and Mahajan define two types of “monoid-like” structures in a duoidal category based upon their two type of functors: duoidal bimonoids and duoidal double monoids. Once more, we only introduce the former.

Definition 59
.

[
1
, Def 6.25]

•

A
duoidal bimonoid
in duoidal category
\cX
is a quintuple
\langle A,\nabla_{A},u_{A},\Delta_{A},e_{A}\rangle
of an object
A
in
\cX
equipped with four morphisms

\nabla_{A}\,\colon A\diamond A\rightarrow A\qquad u_{A}\,\colon I\rightarrow A\qquad\Delta_{A}\,\colon A\rightarrow A\star A\qquad e_{A}\,\colon A\rightarrow J

–

\langle A,\nabla_{A},u_{A}\rangle
is a
\diamond
-monoid,

–

\langle A,\Delta_{A},e_{A}\rangle
is a
\star
-comonoid

such that the two structures are compatible (
45
).

(45)

•

A morphism of duoidal bimonoids is a morphism of the underlying
\diamond
-monoids and
\star
-comonoid.

Proposition 60
.

[
1
, Cor 6.53]

A bilax duoidal functor preserves bimonoids and morphisms between bimonoids.

4.3
Cartesian and Cocartesian Duoidal Categories

As previously stated, duoidal structure arises canonically whenever a monoidal category has finite products or finite coproducts. We detail the construction here.

Proposition 61
.

[
1
, Ex 6.19]

1.

Consider a monoidal category
(\cX,\oslash,I)
with finite products, then
(\cX,\oslash,I,\times,\bone)
is a duoidal category with structure maps

\Delta_{I}=\langle 1_{I},1_{I}\rangle\,\colon I\rightarrow I\times I\qquad\mu_{\bone}=t_{\bone\oslash\bone}\,\colon\bone\oslash\bone\rightarrow\bone\qquad\iota=t_{I}\,\colon I\rightarrow\bone

and interchange natural transformation

\zeta_{A,B,C,D}=\langle\pi^{0}_{A,B}\oslash\pi^{0}_{C,D},\pi^{1}_{A,B}\oslash\pi^{1}_{C,D}\rangle\,\colon(A\times B)\oslash(C\times D)\rightarrow(A\oslash C)\times(B\oslash D)

2.

Consider a monoidal category
(\cX,\oslash,I)
with finite coproducts, then
(\cX,+,\bzero,\oslash,I)
is a duoidal category with structure maps

\Delta_{\bzero}=b_{\bzero\oslash\bzero}\,\colon\bzero\rightarrow\bzero\oslash\bzero\qquad\mu_{I}=[1_{I},1_{I}]\,\colon I+I\rightarrow I\qquad\iota=b_{I}\,\colon\bzero\rightarrow I

and interchange natural transformation

\zeta_{A,B,C,D}=[\iota^{0}_{A,C}\oslash\iota^{0}_{B,D},\iota^{1}_{A,C}\oslash\iota^{1}_{B,D}]\,\colon(A\oslash B)+(C\oslash D)\rightarrow(A+C)\oslash(B+D)

3.

Consider now a category
\cX
with finite products and coproducts, then
(\cX,+,\bzero,\times,\bone)
is of course a symmetric duoidal category with structure maps

\displaystyle\Delta_{\bzero}=b_{\bzero\times\bzero}=\langle 1_{\bzero},1_{\bzero}\rangle\,\colon\bzero\rightarrow\bzero\times\bzero

\displaystyle\mu_{\bone}=t_{\bone+\bone}=[1_{\bone},1_{\bone}]\,\colon\bone+\bone\rightarrow\bone

\displaystyle\iota=t_{\bzero}=b_{\bone}\,\colon\bzero\rightarrow\bone

and interchange natural transformation

\displaystyle\zeta_{A,B,C,D}=\langle\pi^{0}_{A,B}+\pi^{0}_{C,D},\pi^{1}_{A,B}+\pi^{1}_{C,D}\rangle=

\displaystyle[\iota^{0}_{A,C}\times\iota^{0}_{B,D},\iota^{1}_{A,C}\times\iota^{1}_{B,D}]\,\colon(A\times B)+(C\times D)\rightarrow(A+C)\times(B+D)

This idea extends to monoidal functors between monoidal categories with finite products or finite coproducts as follows.

Lemma 62
.

Given cartesian categories
\cX
and
\cY
, any functor
F\,\colon\cX\rightarrow\cY
becomes a symmetric comonoidal functor with structure maps

q_{\bone}=t_{F(\bone)}\,\colon F(\bone)\rightarrow\bone\quad\quad{q_{\times}}_{A,B}=\langle F(\pi^{0}_{A,B}),F(\pi^{1}_{A,B})\rangle\,\colon F(A\times B)\rightarrow F(A)\times F(B)

Further, any natural transformation
\alpha\,\colon F\Rightarrow G
becomes a symmetric comonoidal transformation between the induced symmetric comonoidal functors.

Dually, given cocoartesian categories
\cX
and
\cY
, any functor
G\,\colon\cX\rightarrow\cY
becomes a symmetric monoidal functor with structure maps

p_{\bzero}=b_{F(\bzero)}\,\colon\bzero\rightarrow F(\bzero)\quad\quad{p_{+}}_{A,B}=[F(\iota^{0}_{A,B}),F(\iota^{1}_{A,B})]\,\colon F(A)+F(B)\rightarrow F(A+B)

Any natural transformation
\alpha\,\colon F\Rightarrow G
becomes a symmetric monoidal transformation between the induced symmetric monoidal functors.

Proposition 63
.

[
1
, Ex 6.68]

1.

Consider duoidal categories
(\cX,\oslash,I,\times,\bone)
and
(\cY,\oslash,I,\times,\bone)
whose second monoidal structures are cartesian as described above. Then, consider a monoidal functor
(F,p_{I},p_{\oslash})\,\colon(\cX,\oslash,I)\rightarrow(\cY,\oslash,I)
. By the above Lemma,
(F,q_{\bone},q_{\times})\,\colon(\cX,\times,\bone)\rightarrow(\cY,\times,\bone)
is a comonoidal functor. This further determines a bilax duoidal functor

(F,p_{I},p_{\oslash},q_{\bone},q_{\times})\,\colon(\cX,\oslash,I,\times,\bone)\rightarrow(\cY,\oslash,I,\times,\bone)

2.

Consider duoidal categories
(\cX,+,\bzero,\oslash,I)
and
(\cY,+,\bzero,\oslash,I)
whose first monoidal structures are cocartesian. Then, any comonoidal functor
(F,q_{I},q_{\oslash})\,\colon(\cX,\oslash,I)\rightarrow(\cY,\oslash,I)
is equally a monoidal functor
(F,p_{\bzero},p_{+})\,\colon(\cX,+,\bzero)\rightarrow(\cY,+,\bzero)
by the above Lemma. This determines a bilax duoidal functor

(F,p_{\bzero},p_{+},q_{I},q_{\oslash})\,\colon(\cX,+,\bzero,\oslash,I)\rightarrow(\cY,+,\bzero,\oslash,I)

3.

If we now consider a categories
\cX
and
\cY
with finite products and coproducts, then every functor
F\,\colon\cX\rightarrow\cY
is canonically a bilax duoidal functor.

Remark 64
.

Given comonoidal functors
(F,q^{F}_{\bone},q^{F}_{\times})\,\colon(\cX,\times,\bone)\rightarrow(\cY,\times,\bone)
induced as described above, the standard composition of comonoidal functors is equal to the induced comonoidal structure of the composed functor
F;G
. Similarly, for the monoidal functors described above.

5
Medial Linearly Distributive Categories

We can immediately provide a characterization of cartesian LDCs
(\mbox{${\mathbb{X}}$},\times,\bone,+,\bzero)
by recognizing that
(\mbox{${\mathbb{X}}$},\times,\bone)
is a cartesian monoidal structure and
(\mbox{${\mathbb{X}}$},+,\bzero)
is a cocartesian monoidal structure, therefore Corollary
17
applies to the former and its dual applies to the latter.

Proposition 65
.

A symmetric linearly distributive category
{\mathbb{X}}
is cartesian if and only if there are natural transformations

\Delta_{A}\,\colon A\rightarrow A\otimes A\quad\quad e_{A}\,\colon A\rightarrow\top\quad\quad\nabla_{A}\,\colon A\oplus A\rightarrow A\quad\quad u_{A}:\bot\rightarrow A

such that,
\forall A,B\in\mbox{${\mathbb{X}}$}
,

•

\langle A,\Delta_{A},e_{A}\rangle
determines a cocommutative
\otimes
-comonoid,

•

\langle A,\nabla_{A},u_{A}\rangle
determines a commutative
\oplus
-monoid, and

\Delta_{A\otimes B}=(\Delta_{A}\otimes\Delta_{B});\tau^{\otimes}_{A,A,B,B}\qquad e_{A\otimes B}=(e_{A}\otimes e_{B});{u^{R}_{\otimes}}_{\top}^{-1}

\nabla_{A\oplus B}=\tau^{\oplus}_{A,B,A,B};(\nabla_{A}\oplus\nabla_{B})\qquad u_{A\oplus B}={u^{R}_{\oplus}}^{-1}_{\bot};(u_{A}\oplus u_{B})

\Delta_{\top}={u^{R}_{\otimes}}_{\top}\qquad e_{\top}=1_{\top}\qquad\nabla_{\bot}={u^{R}_{\oplus}}_{\bot}\qquad u_{\bot}=1_{\bot}

The natural transformations of course have component morphisms for all objects in the symmetric LDC. Therefore, there will be maps

e_{\bot},u_{\top}\,\colon\bot\rightarrow\top\qquad u_{\bot\otimes\bot},\Delta_{\bot}\,\colon\bot\rightarrow\bot\otimes\bot\qquad e_{\top\oplus\top},\nabla_{\top}\,\colon\top\oplus\top\rightarrow\top

and, given
A,B\in\mbox{${\mathbb{X}}$}
, there will be a maps

\Delta_{A\oplus B}\,\colon A\oplus B\rightarrow(A\oplus B)\otimes(A\oplus B)\qquad\nabla_{A\otimes B}\,\colon(A\otimes B)\oplus(A\otimes B)\rightarrow A\otimes B

The conditions of Proposition
65
give us additional information about these maps. Indeed, by naturality of
e
and equation
e_{\top}=1_{\top}
, we see that

e_{\bot}=u_{\top}\qquad e_{\top\oplus\top}=\nabla_{\top}\qquad e_{A\oplus B}=(e_{A}\oplus e_{B});\nabla_{\top}

Similarly, by naturality of
u
and equation
u_{\bot}=1_{\bot}
,

u_{\top}=e_{\bot}\qquad u_{\bot\otimes\bot}=\Delta_{\bot}\qquad u_{A\otimes B}=\Delta_{\bot};(u_{A}\otimes u_{B})

Furthermore, we can determine a formula for diagonal morphism
\Delta_{A\otimes B}
:

\begin{gathered}\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern Therefore,

\Delta_{A\oplus B}=A\oplus B\xrightarrow{\Delta_{A}\oplus\Delta_{B}}(A\otimes A)\oplus(B\otimes B)\xrightarrow{\mu^{0}_{A,A,B,B}}(A\oplus B)\otimes(A\oplus B)

for some natural transformation

\displaystyle\mu^{0}_{A,B,C,D}\,\colon(A\otimes B)\oplus(C\otimes D)\rightarrow(A\oplus C)\otimes(B\oplus D)

\displaystyle=\Delta_{(A\otimes B)\oplus(C\otimes D)};(((1_{A}\otimes e_{B});{u^{R}_{\otimes}}^{-1}_{A}\oplus(1_{C}\otimes e_{D});{u^{R}_{\otimes}}^{-1}_{C})\otimes((e_{A}\otimes 1_{B});{u^{L}_{\otimes}}^{-1}_{B}\oplus(e_{C}\otimes 1_{D});{u^{L}_{\otimes}}^{-1}_{D}))

Similarly, by naturality of
\nabla
and monoid coherence conditions (
16
),
