This determines that

\nabla_{A\otimes B}=(A\otimes B)\oplus(A\otimes B)\xrightarrow{\mu^{1}_{A,B,A,B}}(A\oplus A)\otimes(B\oplus B)\xrightarrow{\nabla_{A}\otimes\nabla_{B}}A\otimes B

where
\mu^{1}_{A,B,A,B}
is the natural transformation

\displaystyle\mu^{1}_{A,B,C,D}\,\colon(A\otimes B)\oplus(C\otimes D)\rightarrow(A\oplus C)\otimes(B\oplus D)

\displaystyle=(({u^{R}_{\oplus}}^{-1}_{A};(1_{A}\otimes e_{B})\oplus{u^{R}_{\oplus}}^{-1}_{C};(1_{C}\otimes e_{D}))\otimes({u^{L}_{\oplus}}^{-1}_{B};(e_{A}\otimes 1_{B})\oplus{u^{L}_{\oplus}}^{-1}_{D};(e_{C}\otimes 1_{D}));\nabla_{(A\oplus C)\otimes(B\oplus D)}

We can then demonstrate that
\mu^{0}_{A,B,C,D}=\mu^{1}_{A,B,C,D}
, though the detailed computations are omitted here due to their length.

Corollary 66
.

Consider a symmetric linearly distributive category
{\mathbb{X}}
satisfying the conditions of Proposition
65
, then there are maps

m\,\colon\bot\rightarrow\top\qquad\Delta_{\bot}\,\colon\bot\rightarrow\bot\otimes\bot\qquad\nabla_{\top}\,\colon\top\oplus\top\rightarrow\top

and a natural transformation

\mu\,\colon(\otimes\times\otimes);\oplus\Rightarrow s_{\mbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\mathbb{X}}$},\mbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\mathbb{X}}$},\mbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\mathbb{X}}$},\mbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\mathbb{X}}$}};(\oplus\times\oplus);\otimes\qquad\mu_{A,B,C,D}\,\colon(A\otimes B)\oplus(C\otimes D)\rightarrow(A\oplus C)\otimes(B\oplus D)

such that

\Delta_{A\oplus B}=(\Delta_{A}\oplus\Delta_{B});\mu_{A,A,B,B}\qquad e_{A\oplus B}=(e_{A}\oplus e_{B});\Delta_{\bot}

\nabla_{A\otimes B}=\mu_{A,B,A,B};(\nabla_{A}\otimes\nabla_{B})\qquad u_{A\otimes B}=\nabla_{\top};(u_{A}\otimes u_{B})

e_{\bot}=u_{\top}=m

The maps indicated above will be central to our main definition and therefore we take the time to discuss their meaning in the following section.

5.1
Medial Rule and Main Definition

Considering that linearly distributive categories (LDCs) provide the categorical semantics for multiplicative linear logic, it is important to examine the logical significance of the map
(A\otimes B)\oplus(C\otimes D)\rightarrow(A\oplus C)\otimes(B\oplus D)
. If we consider, for example, Boolean logic and take
\otimes=\wedge
and
\oplus=\vee
, then
(A\wedge B)\vee(C\wedge D)\vdash(A\vee C)\wedge(B\vee D)
is a valid implication.

This implication appears prominently as an inference rule in multiple deductive systems based on deep inference. Deep inference, more specifically the calculus of structures, is a proof formalism first developed by Guglielmi to generalize Gentzen’s sequent calculus, inspired by key concepts from linear logic
[
24
]
.

This rule was first introduced as an inference rule by Brünnler and Tiu
[
10
]
for a local system of classical logic, SKS, and was named the
medial rule
. In this context, locality refers to the idea that rules do not require a global view of formulae of potentially unbounded size, such as contraction within Gentzen’s system for classical logic
[
9
]
. Locality in SKS is achieved by ensuring that non-local rules, including contraction, are presented in atomic form. However, the general form of contraction must still be admissible in SKS, which necessitates the medial rule. The medial rule also appears in Straßburger’s local system of linear logic, SLLS, to ensure that contraction and co-contraction remain atomic
[
37
]
, and similarly in Tiu’s local system for intuitionistic logic, SISgq
[
40
]
. Following its introduction in deep inference, the medial rule has also been studied as a term-rewriting rule, both in isolation
[
39
]
and alongside the linear distributivity and mix rules
[
19
,
11
]
.

In categorical logic, the medial rule has been examined in the study of categorical semantics for classical logic. Unlike intuitionistic logic, which is modeled by cartesian closed categories, and linear logic, which is modeled by
*
-autonomous or linearly distributive categories, the categorical semantics for classical logic remain unresolved. A naive definition of cartesian
*
-autonomous categories leads only to Boolean algebras as models due to Joyal’s paradox. Consequently, the appropriate categorical framework for classical logic remains an active area of research. The medial rule plays a key role in Straßburger’s Boolean categories
[
38
]
and in Lamarche’s
*
-autonomous categories with finitary medial and the absorption law
[
29
]
. In both cases, the authors begin with
*
-autonomous categories and introduce medial maps alongside the
\bot
contraction map
\bot\rightarrow\bot\otimes\bot
and the
\top
co-contraction map
\top\oplus\top\rightarrow\top
to construct categorical models of classical logic. Furthermore, in both constructions, the categories include the nullary mix map
\bot\rightarrow\top
. In particular, Lamarche’s categories, which will be shown to be related to our main definition in Section
5.3
, were a major inspiration for the present work.

From a categorical perspective, the medial map can be recognized as an instance of the well-known interchange law in duoidal categories, taking
\diamond=\oplus
,
J=\bot
,
\star=\oplus
and
I=\bot
. When combined with
\bot
contraction,
\top
co-contraction and the nullary mix map
\bot\rightarrow\top
, they provide all the necessary structures maps for a duoidal category, beyond the standard maps for monoidal structures.

To establish a linearly distributive Fox theorem, we cannot begin with the 2-category of symmetric LDCs alone. Instead, we must consider symmetric LDCs that include additional medial maps, including nullary mix,
\bot
contraction and
\top
co-contraction. This necessity aligns with the role of the medial rule in SLLS, where it enables the atomic contraction and co-contraction rules to extend to all formulae. Therefore, we must first define what it means for a linearly distributive category to have coherent medial maps.

Definition 67
.

A
medial linearly distributive category

(\mbox{${\mathbb{X}}$},\otimes,\top,\oplus,\bot)
consists of:

•

a category
(\mbox{${\mathbb{X}}$},;,1_{A})
,

•

a
tensor
monoidal structure
(\mbox{${\mathbb{X}}$},\otimes,\top)
.

•

a
par
monoidal structure
(\mbox{${\mathbb{X}}$},\oplus,\bot)
,

•

\bot
-contraction
,
\top
-cocontraction
and
nullary mix
morphisms,

\Delta_{\bot}\,\colon\bot\rightarrow\bot\otimes\bot\qquad\nabla_{\top}\,\colon\top\oplus\top\rightarrow\top\qquad m\,\colon\bot\rightarrow\top

•

a
medial
natural transformation, and

\mu\,\colon(\otimes\times\otimes);\oplus\Rightarrow s_{\mbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\mathbb{X}}$},\mbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\mathbb{X}}$},\mbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\mathbb{X}}$},\mbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\mathbb{X}}$}};(\oplus\times\oplus);\otimes\qquad\mu_{A,B,C,D}\,\colon(A\otimes B)\oplus(C\otimes D)\rightarrow(A\oplus C)\otimes(B\oplus D)

•

left and right
linear distributivity
natural transformations

\displaystyle\delta^{R}\,\colon(\oplus\times 1_{\tiny\mbox{${\mathbb{X}}$}});\otimes\Rightarrow a_{\mbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\mathbb{X}}$},\mbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\mathbb{X}}$},\mbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\mathbb{X}}$}};(1_{\mbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\mathbb{X}}$}}\times\otimes);\oplus

\displaystyle\delta^{R}_{A,B,C}\,\colon(A\oplus B)\otimes C\rightarrow A\oplus(B\otimes C),

\displaystyle\delta^{L}\,\colon(1_{\mbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\mathbb{X}}$}}\times\oplus);\otimes\Rightarrow a_{\mbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\mathbb{X}}$},\mbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\mathbb{X}}$},\mbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\mathbb{X}}$}};(\otimes\times 1_{\mbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\mathbb{X}}$}});\oplus

\displaystyle\delta^{L}_{A,B,C}\,\colon A\otimes(B\oplus C)\rightarrow(A\otimes B)\oplus C,

such that

•

(\mbox{${\mathbb{X}}$},\otimes,\top,\oplus,\bot)
is a mix linearly distributive category,

•

(\mbox{${\mathbb{X}}$},\oplus,\bot,\otimes,\top)
is a duoidal category, and

•

the
medial maps
interact coherently with the linear distributivities (
46
).

\begin{gathered}\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.55554pt\hbox{\kern 71.03926pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&\cr&\cr&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-59.64882pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise X))}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces\end{gathered}

(46)

\displaystyle(\mu_{A,B,C,D}\otimes 1_{X});

\displaystyle{\alpha_{\otimes}}_{A\oplus C,B\oplus D,X};(1_{A\oplus C}\otimes\delta^{R}_{B,D,X})=\delta^{R}_{A\otimes B,C\otimes D,X};(1_{A\otimes B}\oplus{\alpha_{\otimes}}_{C,D,X});\mu_{A,B,C,D\otimes X}

\displaystyle(1_{X}\otimes\mu_{A,B,C,D});

\displaystyle{\alpha_{\otimes}}^{-1}_{X,A\oplus C,B\oplus D};(\delta^{L}_{X,A,C}\otimes 1_{B\oplus D})=\delta^{L}_{X,A\otimes B,C\otimes D};({\alpha_{\otimes}}^{-1}_{X,A,B}\oplus 1_{C\otimes D});\mu_{X\otimes A,B,C,D}

\displaystyle(\delta^{R}_{X,A,B}\oplus 1_{C\otimes D});

\displaystyle{\alpha_{\oplus}}^{-1}_{X,A\otimes B,C\otimes D};(1_{X}\oplus\mu_{A,B,C,D})=\mu_{X\oplus A,B,C,D};({\alpha_{\oplus}}^{-1}_{X,A,C}\otimes 1_{B\oplus D});\delta^{R}_{X,A\oplus C,B\oplus D}

\displaystyle(1_{A\otimes B}\oplus\delta^{L}_{C,D,X});

\displaystyle{\alpha_{\oplus}}_{A\otimes B,C\otimes D,X};(\mu_{A,B,C,D}\oplus 1_{X})=\mu_{A,B,C,D\oplus X};(1_{A\oplus C}\otimes{\alpha_{\oplus}}_{B,D,X});\delta^{L}_{A\oplus C,B\oplus D,X}

Remark 68
.

In the above definition,
(\mbox{${\mathbb{X}}$},\oplus,\bot,\otimes,\top)
being a duoidal category corresponds to

•

(\bot,\Delta_{\bot},m)
is a
\otimes
-comonoid and
(\top,\nabla_{\top},m)
is a
\oplus
-monoid (
47
),

•

the medial maps interact coherently with the associators (
48
) and with the unitors (
49
).

\displaystyle\Delta_{\bot};(1_{\bot}\otimes\Delta_{\bot})=\Delta_{\bot};(\Delta_{\bot}\otimes 1_{\bot});{\alpha_{\otimes}}_{\bot,\bot,\bot}

(47)

\displaystyle(1_{\top}\oplus\nabla_{\top});\nabla_{\top}={\alpha_{\oplus}}_{\top,\top,\top};(\nabla_{\top}\oplus 1_{\top});\nabla_{\top}

\displaystyle\Delta_{\bot};(1_{\bot}\otimes m)={u^{R}_{\otimes}}_{\bot}\qquad\qquad(1_{\top}\oplus m);\nabla_{\top}={u^{R}_{\oplus}}_{\top}

\displaystyle\Delta_{\bot};(m\otimes 1_{\bot})={u^{L}_{\otimes}}_{\bot}\qquad\qquad(m\oplus 1_{\top});\nabla_{\top}={u^{L}_{\oplus}}_{\top}

\displaystyle\begin{split}&\mu_{A\otimes B,C,D\otimes E,F};(\mu_{A,B,D,E}\otimes 1_{C\oplus F});{\alpha_{\otimes}}_{A\oplus D,B\oplus E,C\oplus F}\\
&=({\alpha_{\otimes}}_{A,B,C}\oplus{\alpha_{\otimes}}_{D,E,F});\mu_{A,B\otimes C,D,E\otimes F};(1_{A\oplus D}\otimes\mu_{B,C,E,F})\\
\\
&{\alpha_{\oplus}}_{A\otimes B,C\otimes D,E\otimes F};(\mu_{A,B,C,D}\oplus 1_{E\otimes F});\mu_{A\oplus C,B\oplus D,E,F}\\
&=(1_{A\otimes B}\oplus\mu_{C,D,E,F});\mu_{A,B,C\oplus E,D\oplus F};({\alpha_{\oplus}}_{A,C,E}\otimes{\alpha_{\oplus}}_{B,D,F})\\
\end{split}

(48)

\displaystyle\begin{split}&(\Delta_{\bot}\oplus 1_{A\otimes B});\mu_{\bot,\bot,A,B};({u_{\oplus}^{L}}_{A}\otimes{u_{\oplus}^{L}}_{B})={u_{\oplus}^{L}}_{A\otimes B}\\
&(1_{A\otimes B}\oplus\Delta_{\bot});\mu_{A,B,\bot,\bot};({u_{\oplus}^{R}}_{A}\otimes{u_{\oplus}^{R}}_{B})={u_{\oplus}^{R}}_{A\otimes B}\\
&({u_{\otimes}^{L}}_{A}\oplus{u_{\otimes}^{L}}_{B});\mu_{\top,A,\top,B};(\nabla_{\top}\otimes 1_{A\oplus B})={u_{\otimes}^{L}}_{A\oplus B}\\
&({u_{\otimes}^{R}}_{A}\oplus{u_{\otimes}^{R}}_{B});\mu_{A,\top,B,\top};(1_{A\oplus B}\otimes\nabla_{\top})={u_{\otimes}^{R}}_{A\oplus B}\end{split}

(49)

We will refrain from giving examples at this point and first continue developing the theory of MLDCs. Examples will be the focus of Section
5.4
.

As discussed in the introduction, medial LDCs provide a suitable framework for categories that are both independently linearly distributive and duoidal in a coherent manner. We use the term independently to emphasize that, while certain duoidal categories can inherit a linearly distributive structure from their interchange maps (this construction will be reviewed in Section
5.5
), this is what we intend. We are concerned with categories where the duoidal and linearly distributive structures do not imply one another, yet nonetheless interact coherently. This perspective will be particularly useful, as it allows us to leverage the well-developed theory of duoidal categories, which was be introduced in Section
4
.

Alternatively, medial LDCs can be viewed as providing categorical semantics for the logical system MLL+Medial, an extension of multiplicative linear logic with MIX, enriched by the addition of the medial rule. This variant may be incompatible with standard sequent calculus, as the medial rule is rather unconventional: its premise is a disjunction of formulae, while its conclusion is a conjunction. However, MLL+Medial is likely expressible within the calculus of structures, where linear distributivity and medial maps play a central role in several systems within the deep inference paradigm. Overall, the logical study of MLL+Medial remains an open area for furture research.

Remark 69
.

This is perhaps a similar situation to that of pomset logic and BV. Pomset logic is a non-commutative variant of MLL which includes a non-commutative connective
\lhd
introduced by Retoré motivated by Girard’s categorical model for linear logic, coherence spaces, and the study of proof nets
[
34
]
. While pomset logic was expressible with proof nets, it did not seem to be properly expressible in a sequent calculus. In an attempt remedy the situation, Guglielmi determined it could not be done and that lead to the development to of the calculus of structures, as an alternative to sequent calculus based on the idea of deep inference, along with the logic BV
[
24
]
. It was conjectured in said paper that BV and pomset logic were one and the same. It was determined that, like pomset logic, BV was inexpressible in the sequent calculus
[
41
]
, although recent work has conclusively showed that these two logics are in fact different, while detailing exactly why there are difficulties expressing these logics in sequent calculus
[
33
]
.

5.2
Symmetry

If we revisit Proposition
65
, which provides a preliminary characterization of cartesian LDCs, we observe that for any objects
A
and
B
in a CLDC, the diagonal
\Delta_{A\otimes B}
and the multiplication
\nabla_{A\oplus B}
are constructed using the canonical flip maps associated with the symmetric
\otimes
and
\oplus
monoidal structures. By the same reasoning that led us to define medial LDCs to ensure the presence of the necessary medial maps, we now define symmetric medial LDCs to guarantee the existence of these canonical flip maps.

Definition 70
.

A
symmetric medial linearly distributive category

(\mbox{${\mathbb{X}}$},\otimes,\top,\oplus,\bot)
is a medial linearly distributive category such that

•

(\mbox{${\mathbb{X}}$},\otimes,\top,\oplus,\bot)
is a symmetric linearly distributive category, and

•

(\mbox{${\mathbb{X}}$},\oplus,\bot,\otimes,\top)
is a symmetric duoidal category.

In other words, a medial linear distributive category is symmetric if
(\mbox{${\mathbb{X}}$},\otimes,\top)
and
(\mbox{${\mathbb{X}}$},\oplus,\bot)
are symmetric monoidal categories such that (
22
), (
39
) and (
40
).

There is however an equivalent definition for symmetric medial LDCs which does not introduce the nullary mix map
m\,\colon\bot\rightarrow\top
nor asks for it to be mix. Indeed, in the presence of the braidings, we can define it in terms of the other structure maps and get the mix structure.

It is well-known that given a duoidal category
(\mbox{${\mathbb{X}}$},\oplus,\bot,\otimes,\top)
,
m\,\colon\bot\rightarrow\top
is defined by the rest of the structure.

Proposition 71
.

[
1
, Prop 6.9]

The structure map
m\,\colon\bot\rightarrow\top
is equal to

(50)

The proof that both these composites are equivalent, and themselves equal to
m
follows from the conditions that
(\top,\nabla_{\top},m)
is a
\oplus
-monoid and
(\bot,\Delta_{\bot},m)
is a
\otimes
-comonoid. Therefore, the definition of an arbitrary duoidal category must include the nullary mix map.

However, in the presence of braidings, it can be excluded. It was first noticed by Lamarche in Proposition 2.11 of
[
29
]
that, given a category with two symmetric monoidal structures
(\mbox{${\mathbb{X}}$},\otimes,\top)
and
(\mbox{${\mathbb{X}}$},\oplus,\bot)
, and a coherent medial natural transformation
\mu_{A,B,C,D}
, i.e. (
40
) holds, the above composites are equal.

Further, in the presence of
\bot
-contraction
\Delta_{\bot}\,\colon\bot\rightarrow\bot\otimes\bot
such that
\bot
is a cocommutative
\otimes
-cosemigroup and
\top
-cocontraction map
\nabla_{\top}\,\colon\top\oplus\top\rightarrow\top
such that
\top
with commutative
\oplus
-semigroup, which interact coherently with the medial maps, i.e. (
38
) holds, then the equivalent composites
\bot\rightarrow\top
further endow
\bot
and
\top
with
\otimes
-comonoid and
\oplus
-monoid structures respectively, as shown in Proposition 2.19
[
29
]
.

Then, as
\bot
is endowed with a
\otimes
-comonoid structure and
\top
is endowed with a
\oplus
-monoid structure in a symmetric LDC, Fuhrmann and Pym’s theorem in
[
22
]
implies their counit and unit, which are equal in this instances, induce a mix structure.

Altogether, these observations allows us to provide an alternative definition of symmetric medial LDCs which excludes the nullary mix map and defines it entirely based on the rest of the structure:

Proposition 72
.

A symmetric medial linearly distributive category
(\mbox{${\mathbb{X}}$},\otimes,\top,\oplus,\bot)
is equivalently defined as a symmetric linearly distributive category, equipped with

•

\bot
-contraction and
\top
-cocontraction morphisms,

\Delta_{\bot}\,\colon\bot\rightarrow\bot\otimes\bot\qquad\nabla_{\top}\,\colon\top\oplus\top\rightarrow\top

•

a medial natural transformation, and

\mu\,\colon(\otimes\times\otimes);\oplus\Rightarrow s_{\mbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\mathbb{X}}$},\mbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\mathbb{X}}$},\mbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\mathbb{X}}$},\mbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\mathbb{X}}$}};(\oplus\times\oplus);\otimes\qquad\mu_{A,B,C,D}\,\colon(A\otimes B)\oplus(C\otimes D)\rightarrow(A\oplus C)\otimes(B\oplus D)

such that

•

(\bot,\Delta_{\bot})
is a cocommutative
\otimes
-cosemigroup and
(\top,\nabla_{\top})
is commutative
\oplus
-semigroup (
51
),

•

the medial maps interact coherently with the braidings (
52
), the associators (
53
) and with the unitors (
55
),

•

the medial maps interact coherently with the linear distributivities (
55
), and

•

the medial maps, the linear distributivities and the braidings interact coherently together (
56
).

\displaystyle\begin{split}&\Delta_{\bot}=\Delta_{\bot};{\sigma_{\otimes}}_{\bot,\bot}\qquad\Delta_{\bot};(1_{\bot}\otimes\Delta_{\bot})=\Delta_{\bot};(\Delta_{\bot}\otimes 1_{\bot});{\alpha_{\otimes}}_{\bot,\bot,\bot}\\
&\nabla_{\top}={\sigma_{\oplus}}_{\top,\top};\nabla_{\top}\qquad(1_{\top}\oplus\nabla_{\top});\nabla_{\top}={\alpha_{\oplus}}_{\top,\top,\top};(\nabla_{\top}\oplus 1_{\top});\nabla_{\top}\end{split}

(51)

\displaystyle\begin{split}&\mu_{A,B,C,D};{\sigma_{\otimes}}_{A\oplus C,B\oplus D}=({\sigma_{\otimes}}_{A,B}\oplus{\sigma_{\otimes}}_{C,D});\mu_{B,A,D,C}\\
&\mu_{A,B,C,D};({\sigma_{\oplus}}_{A,C}\otimes{\sigma_{\oplus}}_{B,D})={\sigma_{\oplus}}_{A\otimes B,C\otimes D};\mu_{C,D,A,B}\end{split}

(52)

\displaystyle\begin{split}&\mu_{A\otimes B,C,D\otimes E,F};(\mu_{A,B,D,E}\otimes 1_{C\oplus F});{\alpha_{\otimes}}_{A\oplus D,B\oplus E,C\oplus F}=\\
&({\alpha_{\otimes}}_{A,B,C}\oplus{\alpha_{\otimes}}_{D,E,F});\mu_{A,B\otimes C,D,E\otimes F};(1_{A\oplus D}\otimes\mu_{B,C,E,F})\\
\\

(53)

\displaystyle\begin{split}&(1_{A\otimes B}\oplus\Delta_{\bot});\mu_{A,B,\bot,\bot};({u_{\oplus}^{R}}_{A}\otimes{u_{\oplus}^{R}}_{B})={u_{\oplus}^{R}}_{A\otimes B}\\
&({u_{\otimes}^{R}}_{A}\oplus{u_{\otimes}^{R}}_{B});\mu_{A,\top,B,\top};(1_{A\oplus B}\otimes\nabla_{\top})={u_{\otimes}^{R}}_{A\oplus B}\end{split}

(54)

\displaystyle\begin{split}&(1_{X}\otimes\mu_{A,B,C,D});{\alpha_{\otimes}}^{-1}_{X,A\oplus C,B\oplus D};(\delta^{L}_{X,A,C}\otimes 1_{B\oplus D})=\\
&\delta^{L}_{X,A\otimes B,C\otimes D};({\alpha_{\otimes}}^{-1}_{X,A,B}\oplus 1_{C\otimes D});\mu_{X\otimes A,B,C,D}\\
\\
&(1_{A\otimes B}\oplus\delta^{L}_{C,D,X});{\alpha_{\oplus}}_{A\otimes B,C\otimes D,X};(\mu_{A,B,C,D}\oplus 1_{X})\\
&=\mu_{A,B,C,D\oplus X};(1_{A\oplus C}\otimes{\alpha_{\oplus}}_{B,D,X});\delta^{L}_{A\oplus C,B\oplus D,X}\end{split}

(55)

\displaystyle\begin{split}&(1_{X}\otimes\mu_{A,B,C,D});{\alpha_{\otimes}}^{-1}_{X,A\oplus C,B\oplus D};({\sigma_{\otimes}}_{X,A\oplus C}\otimes 1_{B\oplus D});{\alpha_{\otimes}}_{A\oplus C,X,B\oplus D};(1_{A\oplus C}\otimes\delta^{L}_{X,B,D})=\\
&\delta^{L}_{X,A\otimes B,C\otimes D};({\alpha_{\otimes}}^{-1}_{X,A,B}\oplus 1_{C\otimes D});(({\sigma_{\otimes}}_{X,A}\otimes 1_{B})\oplus 1_{C\otimes D});({\alpha_{\otimes}}_{A,X,B}\oplus 1_{C\otimes D});\mu_{X\otimes A,B,C,D}\\
\\
&(\delta^{L}_{A,B,X}\oplus 1_{C\otimes D});{\alpha_{\oplus}^{-1}}_{A\otimes B,X,C\otimes D};(1_{A\otimes B}\oplus{\sigma_{\oplus}}_{X,C\otimes D});{\alpha_{\oplus}}_{A\otimes B,C\otimes D,X};(\mu_{A,B,C,D}\oplus 1_{X})=\\
&\mu_{A,B\oplus X,C,D};(1_{A\oplus C}\otimes{\alpha_{\oplus}^{-1}}_{B,X,D});(1_{A\oplus C}\otimes(1_{B}\oplus{\sigma_{\oplus}}_{X,D}));(1_{A\oplus C}\otimes{\alpha_{\oplus}}_{B,D,X});\delta^{L}_{A\oplus C,B\oplus D,X}\end{split}

(56)

Proof.

It is immediate that Definition
70
implies the definition presented in the Proposition, since the former is a version of the latter with more structures and conditions. Coherence condition (
56
) is obtained by replacing the right linear distribution in (
46
) by its definition as a composite of braidings and the linear distributions, and simplifying using (
3
) and (
52
).

Now, consider a symmetric LDC
(\mbox{${\mathbb{X}}$},\otimes,\top,\oplus,\bot)
as outlined in the Proposition. Then, the following diagram commutes:

\begin{gathered}\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern m\,\colon\bot\rightarrow\top

\top
with a commutative
\oplus

24
, the symmetric LDC is mix.

∎

This alternative definition is of particular use because it involves fewer coherence conditions to check when looking at examples and it will be helpful in showing that symmetric medial LDCs with negation are equivalent to Lamarche’s definition detailed in
[
29
]
in the following section

5.3
Adding Negation

As linear negation is central to linear logic and a component of many models of multiplicative linear logic, it was essential when developing LDCs that Cockett and Seely demonstrate how to include negation in their categorical framework to recover
*
-autonomous categories. Indeed, many of the key examples of LDCs are
*
-autonomous. The same holds true in our extension to medial LDCs. It is important we describe how the duoidal structure interacts with linear negation and recover Lamarche’s definition of
*
-autonomous categories with finitary (binary and nullary) medial, and with the absorption law
[
29
]
.

Definition 73
.

A symmetric medial linearly distributive category
(\mbox{${\mathbb{X}}$},\otimes,\top,\oplus,\bot)

has negation
if

•

it has negation as a symmetric linearly distributive category (Definition
29
),

•

\bot
-contraction
\Delta_{\bot}\,\colon\bot\rightarrow\bot\otimes\bot
and
\top
-cocontraction
\nabla_{\top}\,\colon\top\oplus\top\rightarrow\top
are dual to one another (
57
), and

•

the medial maps are self-dual (
58
).

B)^{\perp}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 46.41118pt\raise-67.0556pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-2.13113pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\epsilon_{D\otimes B,C\otimes A}^{-1}}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 87.39197pt\raise-59.94672pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 87.39197pt\raise-59.94672pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.55554pt\hbox{$\textstyle{((D\otimes B)\oplus(C\otimes A))^{\perp}}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces\end{gathered}

(58)

Lemma 74
.

A symmetric medial linearly distributive category with negation
(\mbox{${\mathbb{X}}$},\otimes,\top,\oplus,\bot)
has a self-dual nullary mix map
m\,\colon\bot\rightarrow\top
, i.e.

\begin{gathered}\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.55554pt\hbox{\kern 13.09612pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&\cr&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-6.8889pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.55554pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\bot\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-13.09612pt\raise-14.06999pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-1.50694pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{m}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-22.58446pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 8.93227pt\raise 5.1875pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-0.8264pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\chi_{\top}}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 27.8889pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 27.8889pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.55554pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\top^{\perp}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 37.6528pt\raise-13.27776pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-2.38391pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{(m)^{\perp}}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 37.6528pt\raise-20.99998pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern-6.8889pt\raise-29.97336pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.55554pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\top\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 8.98972pt\raise-36.77391pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-2.43947pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\beta^{-1}_{\top}}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 27.8889pt\raise-29.97336pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 27.8889pt\raise-29.97336pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.55554pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\bot^{\perp}}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces\end{gathered}

(59)

Proof.

This follows from the definition of
m\,\colon\bot\rightarrow\top
as a composite of unitors and the medial map, i.e. Proposition
71
, and the fact that the medial maps are self-dual themselves.
∎

By Theorem
32
, which states the correspondence between symmetric LDCs with negation and
*
-autonomous categories, and by comparing Lamarche’s definition to the alternative definition of a symmetric medial LDC in Proposition
72
, it is immediate that:

Proposition 75
.

The notions of symmetric medial linearly distributive categories with negation and of with that
*
-autonomous categories with finitary medial, and with the absorption law, as defined in
[
29
]
, coincide.

We will however also be particularly invested in symmetric medial LDCs which do not have a notion of negation. This is due to the ultimate goal of providing a 2-functor which maps symmetric medial LDCs to cartesian LDCs as part of the linearly distributive Fox theorem. If this construction preserves the negation of symmetric medial LDCs, then by Joyal’s paradox, the resulting cartesian LDCs would necessarily be posets. This will be discussed in greater detail in Section
7.1
.

5.4
Examples

The most important class example of a MLDC is of course the CLDCs, as the former were defined precisely to be an appropriate generalization of the latter.

The canonical duoidal structure of a CLDC was discussed by this author and co-author Lemay. It is precisely the one for cartesian and co-cartesian categories as described in Proposition
61
.

Proposition 76
.

[
27
, Prop 3.19]

A cartesian linearly distributive category
(\mbox{${\mathbb{X}}$},\times,\top,+,\bot)
is a duoidal category, with structure maps

\displaystyle\Delta_{\bot}=b_{\bot\times\bot}=\langle 1_{\bot},1_{\bot}\rangle\,\colon\bot\rightarrow\bot\times\bot\qquad\nabla_{\top}=t_{\top+\top}=[1_{\top},1_{\top}]\,\colon\top+\top\rightarrow\top

\displaystyle m=t_{\bot}=b_{\top}\,\colon\bot\rightarrow\top

and interchange natural transformation

\displaystyle\mu_{A,B,C,D}\,\colon(A\times B)+(C\times D)\rightarrow(A+C)\times(B+D)

\displaystyle=\langle\pi^{0}_{A,B}+\pi^{0}_{C,D},\pi^{1}_{A,B}+\pi^{1}_{C,D}\rangle=[\iota^{0}_{A,C}\times\iota^{0}_{B,D},\iota^{1}_{A,C}\times\iota^{1}_{B,D}]

Moreover, Proposition 3.20 in
[
27
]
details the how this duoidal structure interacts with the linear distributivities. The resulting commuting diagrams imply that:

Theorem 77
.

Every cartesian linearly distributive category
(\cX,\times,\bone,+,\bzero)
is canonically a symmetric medial linearly distributive category.

Another immediate class of examples are the braided monoidal categories, as every monoidal category is a degenerate LDC and every braided category is a strong duoidal category.

Proposition 78
.

Consider a braided monoidal category
(\cX,\oslash,I)
, then it is a medial linearly distributive category
(\cX,\oslash,I,\oslash,I)
with

\nabla_{I}={u^{-1}_{\oslash}}_{I}\,\colon I\oslash I\rightarrow I\qquad\qquad\Delta_{I}={u_{\oslash}}_{I}\,\colon I\rightarrow I\oslash I\qquad\qquad m=1_{I}\,\colon I\rightarrow I

\mu_{A,B,C,D}=\tau^{\oslash}_{A,B,C,D}\,\colon(A\oslash B)\oslash(C\oslash D)\rightarrow(A\oslash C)\oslash(B\oslash D)

\delta^{L}_{A,B,C}={\alpha_{\oslash}^{-1}}_{A,B,C}\,\colon A\oslash(B\oslash C)\rightarrow(A\oslash B)\oslash C

\delta^{R}_{A,B,C}={\alpha_{\oslash}}_{A,B,C}\,\colon(A\oslash B)\oslash C\rightarrow A\oslash(B\oslash C)

Proof.

By Example
33
, every monoidal category is a degenerate LDC with associators for linear distributivities. By Example
54
and Proposition
55
, we know ever braided monoidal category is a strong duoidal category with the canonical flip as its interchange map. Therefore, it only remains to verify (
46
), which follow immediately by the coherence theorem for braided monoidal categories.
∎

Our final source of examples stems from generalizing
Q
-coherences to the linearly distributive context, as first introduced by Lamarche
[
28
]
to capture Girard’s coherence spaces and Ehrhard’s hypercoherences, and later investigated in the context of
*
-autonomous categories with finitary (binary and nullary) medial, and with the absorption law
[
29
]
.

Firstly, let
(\mbox{${\mathbb{P}}$},\otimes,\top,\oplus,\bot)
be a posetal symmetric MLDC. In other words,
{\mathbb{P}}
is a poset with two commutative, associative and unital operations
\otimes
and
\oplus
, equipped with units
\top
and
\bot
respectively, such that the following inequalities hold
\forall a,b,c,d\in\mbox{${\mathbb{P}}$}
.

\top\oplus\top\leq\top\qquad\bot\leq\bot\otimes\bot\qquad\bot\leq\top

(a\otimes b)\oplus(c\otimes d)\leq(a\oplus c)\otimes(b\oplus d)

(a\oplus b)\otimes c\leq a\oplus(b\otimes c)\qquad a\otimes(b\oplus c)\leq(a\otimes b)\oplus c

While this may seem at first glance to be an involved definition, one can quickly see that every bounded distributive lattice is such a poset with
\otimes=\wedge
and
\oplus=\vee
. Indeed, as proved in
[
27
]
, a CLDC is a poset if and only if it is a bounded distributive lattice, and a posetal CLDC is of course a posetal symmetric MLDC as previously discussed.

Definition 79
.

[
29
, Def 4.5]

•

A
{\mathbb{P}}
-coherence
A=(|A|,\rho_{A})
consists of a pair of a poset
(|A|,\sqsubseteq)
and a symmetric monotone function
\rho_{A}\,\colon|A|\times|A|\rightarrow\mbox{${\mathbb{P}}$}
.

•

A
{\mathbb{P}}
-coherence map
f\,\colon A\rightarrow B
is a relation
f\,\colon|A|\nrightarrow|B|
which is

1.

down-closed in the source:
(a,b)\in f\wedge a^{\prime}\sqsubseteq a\implies(a^{\prime},b)\in f
,

2.

up-closed in the target:
(a,b)\in f\wedge b\sqsubseteq b^{\prime}\implies(a,b^{\prime})\in f
, and

3.

compatible with the monotone functions:
(a,b)\in f\wedge(a^{\prime},b^{\prime})\in f\implies\rho_{A}(a,a^{\prime})\leq\rho_{B}(b,b^{\prime})

Definition 80
.

[
29
, Def 4.5]

Let
{\mathbb{P}}
-
{\sf Coh}
be denote the following structure:

•

Objects:
{\mathbb{P}}
-coherences
A=(|A|,\rho_{A})

•

Morphisms:
{\mathbb{P}}
-coherence maps
f\,\colon A\rightarrow B

•

Composition: standard composition of relations, i.e. given
f\,\colon A\rightarrow B
and
g\,\colon B\rightarrow C
,
f;g\,\colon A\rightarrow C
is the relation
f;g\,\colon|A|\nrightarrow|C|
defined as

(a,c)\in f;g\iff\exists b\in B,(a,b)\in f\wedge(b,c)\in g

•

Identities: given
{\mathbb{P}}
-coherence
A=(|A|,\rho_{A})
,
1_{A}\,\colon A\rightarrow A
is defined by

(a,a^{\prime})\in 1_{A}\iff a\sqsubseteq\ a^{\prime}\in|A|

•

Tensor product:
\otimes\,\colon\mbox{$\mbox{${\mathbb{P}}$}$-${\sf Coh}$}\times\mbox{$\mbox{${\mathbb{P}}$}$-${\sf Coh}$}\rightarrow\mbox{$\mbox{${\mathbb{P}}$}$-${\sf Coh}$}
is defined by

(A=(|A|,\rho_{A}),B=(|B|,\rho_{B}))\mapsto A\otimes B=(|A|\times|B|,\rho_{A\otimes B})

(f\,\colon A\rightarrow A^{\prime},g\,\colon B\rightarrow B^{\prime})\mapsto(f\otimes g\,\colon A\otimes B\rightarrow A^{\prime}\otimes B^{\prime})

where the monotone function
\rho_{A\otimes B}\,\colon(|A|\times|B|)\times((|A|\times|B|)\rightarrow\mbox{${\mathbb{P}}$}
is given by

\rho_{A\otimes B}((a,b),(a^{\prime},b^{\prime}))=\rho_{A}(a,a^{\prime})\otimes\rho_{B}(b,b^{\prime})

and where
f\otimes g
is given by the relation
f\times g\,\colon|A|\times|B|\nrightarrow|A^{\prime}|\times|B^{\prime}|
, i.e.

((a,b),(a^{\prime},b))\in f\times g\iff(a,a^{\prime})\in f\wedge(b,b^{\prime})\in g

•

Tensor unit:
{\mathbb{P}}
-coherence
\top=(\{*\},\rho_{\top})
where
\rho_{\top}(*,*)=\top\in\mbox{${\mathbb{P}}$}

•

Par product:
\oplus\,\colon\mbox{$\mbox{${\mathbb{P}}$}$-${\sf Coh}$}\times\mbox{$\mbox{${\mathbb{P}}$}$-${\sf Coh}$}\rightarrow\mbox{$\mbox{${\mathbb{P}}$}$-${\sf Coh}$}
is defined by

(A=(|A|,\rho_{A}),B=(|B|,\rho_{B}))\mapsto A\oplus B=(|A|\times|B|,\rho_{A\oplus B})

(f\,\colon A\rightarrow A^{\prime},g\,\colon B\rightarrow B^{\prime})\mapsto(f\oplus g\,\colon A\oplus B\rightarrow A^{\prime}\oplus B^{\prime})

where the monotone function
\rho_{A\oplus B}\,\colon(|A|\times|B|)\times((|A|\times|B|)\rightarrow\mbox{${\mathbb{P}}$}
is given by

\rho_{A\oplus B}((a,b),(a^{\prime},b^{\prime}))=\rho_{A}(a,a^{\prime})\oplus\rho_{B}(b,b^{\prime})

and where
f\oplus g
is given by the relation
f\times g\,\colon|A|\times|B|\nrightarrow|A^{\prime}|\times|B^{\prime}|
, i.e.

((a,b),(a^{\prime},b))\in f\times g\iff(a,a^{\prime})\in f\wedge(b,b^{\prime})\in g

•

Par unit:
{\mathbb{P}}
-coherence
\top=(\{*\},\rho_{\bot})
where
\rho_{\bot}(*,*)=\bot\in\mbox{${\mathbb{P}}$}

•

Bottom-contraction map:
\Delta_{\bot}\,\colon\bot\rightarrow\bot\otimes\bot
is relation
|\bot|\nrightarrow|\bot|\times|\bot|
given by

(*,(*,*))\in\nabla_{\top}

•

Top-cocontraction map:
\nabla_{\top}\,\colon\top\oplus\top\rightarrow\top

|\top|\times|\top|\nrightarrow|\top|
given by

((*,*),*)\in\nabla_{\top}

•

Nullary mix map:
m\,\colon\bot\rightarrow\top
is the relation
|\bot|\nrightarrow|\top|
given by

(*,*)\in m

•

Medial maps:
\mu_{A,B,C,D}\,\colon(A\otimes B)\oplus(C\otimes D)\rightarrow(A\oplus D)\otimes(C\oplus D)
is relation
(|A|\times|B|)\times(|C|\times|D|)\nrightarrow(|A|\times|C|)\times(|B|\times|D|)
given by

(((a,b),(c,d)),((a^{\prime},c^{\prime}),(b^{\prime},d^{\prime})))\in\delta^{L}_{A,B,C}\iff a\sqsubseteq a^{\prime}\wedge b\sqsubseteq b^{\prime}\wedge c\sqsubseteq c^{\prime}\wedge d\sqsubseteq s^{\prime}

•

Left linear distributivity:
\delta^{L}_{A,B,C}\,\colon A\otimes(B\oplus C)\rightarrow(A\otimes B)\oplus C
is relation
|A|\times(|B|\times|C|)\nrightarrow(|A|\times|B|)\times|C|
given by

(((a,(b,c)),((a^{\prime},b^{\prime}),c^{\prime}))\in\delta^{L}_{A,B,C}\iff a\sqsubseteq a^{\prime}\wedge b\sqsubseteq b^{\prime}\wedge c\sqsubseteq c^{\prime}

Proposition 81
.

{\mathbb{P}}
-
{\sf Coh}
is a symmetric medial linearly distributive category.

As the above construction is simply a small generalization of Lamarche’s work, we shall not include the proof in this paper.

5.5
Properties

Notice that in a symmetric medial LDC, we have the canonical flip maps for the symmetric
\otimes
-monoidal product and symmetric
\oplus
-monoidal product. The interaction between the medial maps, the linear distributivities and the canonical flip maps are given below.

Proposition 82
.

The following diagrams commute in any symmetric medial linearly distributive category:

(60)

\displaystyle\delta^{L}_{A\otimes B,C\otimes D,E\otimes F};(\tau^{\otimes}_{A,B,C,D}\oplus 1_{E\otimes F});\mu_{A\otimes C,B\otimes D,E,F}

\displaystyle=(1_{A\otimes B}\otimes\mu_{C,D,E,F});\tau^{\otimes}_{A,B,C\oplus E,D\oplus F};(\delta^{L}_{A,C,E}\otimes\delta^{L}_{B,D,F})

\displaystyle\mu_{A,B\oplus C,D,E\oplus F};(1_{A\oplus D}\otimes\tau^{\oplus}_{B,C,E,F});\delta^{L}_{A\oplus D,B\oplus E,C\oplus F}=

\displaystyle(\delta^{L}_{A,B,C}\oplus\delta^{L}_{D,E,F});\tau^{\oplus}_{A\otimes B,C,D\otimes E,F};(\mu_{A,B,D,E}\oplus 1_{C\oplus F})

Proof.

The proof of the first equality is given by the commuting diagram in Figure
1
, in the Appendix
C
due to its size, and the other equality follows similarly.
∎

The above proposition allows us to show that the medial maps are in some sense the canonical flip maps “modulo” the mix maps.

Proposition 83
.

Given a symmetric medial linearly distributive category, the following diagram commutes

\begin{gathered}\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.55554pt\hbox{\kern 70.64249pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&\cr&\cr&\cr&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-45.11414pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.55554pt\hbox{$\textstyle{(A\otimes B)\oplus(C\otimes D)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern (61)

Proof.

The proof of the first equality is given by the commuting diagram in Figure
2
in the Appendix
C
due to its size, and the other equality follows similarly.
∎

If we consider a MLDC with invertible linear distributivities, we know it must know the
\oplus
-monoidal product must be equivalent to the
\bot
-shifted tensor, but in the presence of medial structure, we can say even more:

Proposition 84
.

Consider a medial linearly distributive category
(\mbox{${\mathbb{X}}$},\otimes,\top,\oplus,\bot)
with invertible linear distributivities, then its linearly distributive structure is compact.

Proof.

Consider a MLDC
(\mbox{${\mathbb{X}}$},\otimes,\top,\oplus,\bot)
whose linear distributivities
\delta^{L}_{A,B,C}
and
\delta^{R}_{A,B,C}
have inverses. By Proposition
35
and the details of the proof in
[
15
]
,
\top\oplus\top
is the tensor inverse of
\bot
with isomorphisms

s^{L}=\bot\otimes(\top\oplus\top)\xrightarrow{\delta^{L}_{\bot,\top,\top}}(\bot\otimes\top)\oplus\top\xrightarrow{{u^{R}_{\otimes}}^{-1}_{\bot}\oplus 1_{\top}}\bot\oplus\top\xrightarrow{{u^{L}_{\oplus}}_{\top}}\top

s^{R}=(\top\oplus\top)\otimes\bot\xrightarrow{\delta^{R}_{\top,\top,\bot}}\top\oplus(\top\otimes\bot)\xrightarrow{1_{\top}\oplus{u^{L}_{\otimes}}^{-1}_{\bot}}\top\oplus\bot\xrightarrow{{u^{R}_{\oplus}}_{\top}}\top

This implies that the MLDC is isomix as
m\,\colon\bot\rightarrow\top
has inverse defined by

m^{-1}=\top\xrightarrow{{s^{L}}^{-1}}\bot\otimes(\top\oplus\top)\xrightarrow{1_{\bot}\otimes\nabla_{\top}}\bot\otimes\top\xrightarrow{{u^{R}_{\otimes}}_{\bot}^{-1}}\bot

m^{-1};m=1_{\top}
holds by the following commuting diagram

187.66797pt\raise-146.90059pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.60004pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-2.30057pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{{u^{R}_{\otimes}}_{\bot}^{-1}}$}}}\kern 3.60004pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 160.5557pt\raise-139.16664pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawline@}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 193.6202pt\raise-115.55829pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.60004pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-1.725pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{m\otimes 1_{\top}}$}}}\kern 3.60004pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 168.3335pt\raise-109.76389pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawline@}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawline@}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces\end{gathered}

Similarly, for
m;m^{-1}=1_{\bot}
.

As the linear distributivities are isomorphisms and
m\,\colon\bot\rightarrow\top
is invertible,
\mathrm{mix}_{A,B}\,\colon A\otimes B\rightarrow A\oplus B
is an isomorphism.
∎

Given a normal duoidal category, there is a construction providing a linearly distributive structure. Indeed, let
(\cX,\diamond,I,\star,J)
be a duoidal category where
\iota\,\colon I\rightarrow J
is an isomorphism, we can define the following linear distributivities:

\displaystyle\partial^{L}_{A,B,C}=A\diamond(B\star C)\cong(A\star J)\diamond(B\star C)\xrightarrow{\zeta_{A,J,B,C}}(A\diamond B)\star(J\diamond C)\cong(A\diamond B)\star C

\displaystyle\partial^{R}_{A,B,C}=(A\star B)\diamond C\cong(A\star B)\diamond(J\star C)\xrightarrow{\zeta_{A,B,J,C}}(A\diamond J)\star(B\diamond C)\cong A\star(B\diamond C)

Then,
(\cX,\diamond,I,\star,J)
is an isomix LDC
[
36
]
.

Consider now an isomix MLDC
(\mbox{${\mathbb{X}}$},\otimes,\top,\oplus,\bot)
with linear distributivities

\delta^{L}_{A,B,C}\,\colon A\otimes(B\oplus C)\rightarrow(A\otimes B)\oplus C\qquad\qquad\delta^{R}_{A,B,C}\,\colon(A\oplus B)\otimes C\rightarrow A\oplus(B\oplus C)

Then, it is in particular a normal duoidal category
(\mbox{${\mathbb{X}}$},\oplus,\bot,\otimes,\top)
. By above, we know that
(\mbox{${\mathbb{X}}$},\oplus,\bot,\otimes,\top)
is an isomix LDC with linear distributivities

\partial^{L}_{A,B,C}\,\colon A\oplus(B\otimes C)\rightarrow(A\oplus B)\otimes C\ \qquad\qquad\partial^{R}_{A,B,C}\,\colon(A\otimes B)\oplus C\rightarrow A\otimes(B\oplus C)

Notice the direction of the latter linear distributivities is opposite to the former. It turns out they are inverses of each other and, therefore, when isomix, the linearly distributive structure is compact.

Proposition 85
.

Consider an isomix medial linearly distributive category
(\mbox{${\mathbb{X}}$},\otimes,\top,\oplus,\bot)
, then its linearly distributive structure is compact.

Proof.

Consider an isomix MLDC
(\mbox{${\mathbb{X}}$},\otimes,\top,\oplus,\bot)
and define the natural transformations
\partial^{L}
and
\partial^{R}
as follows.

\displaystyle\partial^{L}_{A,B,C}=

\displaystyle A\oplus(B\otimes C)\xrightarrow{{u^{R}_{\otimes}}_{A}\oplus 1_{B\otimes C}}(A\otimes\top)\otimes(B\otimes C)\xrightarrow{\mu_{A,\top,B,C}}(A\oplus B)\otimes(\top\oplus C)

\displaystyle\xrightarrow{1_{A\oplus B}\otimes(m^{-1}\oplus 1_{C})}(A\oplus B)\otimes(\bot\oplus C)\xrightarrow{1_{A\oplus B}\otimes{u^{L}_{\oplus}}_{C}}(A\oplus B)\otimes C

\displaystyle\partial^{R}_{A,B,C}=

\displaystyle(A\otimes B)\oplus C\xrightarrow{1_{A\otimes B}\oplus{u^{R}_{\otimes}}_{C}}(A\otimes B)\oplus(\top\otimes C)\xrightarrow{\mu_{A,B,\top,C}}(A\oplus\top)\otimes(B\oplus C)

\displaystyle\xrightarrow{(1_{A}\oplus m^{-1})\otimes 1_{B\oplus C}}(A\oplus\bot)\otimes(B\oplus C)\xrightarrow{{u^{R}_{\oplus}}_{A}\otimes 1_{B\oplus C}}A\otimes(B\oplus C)

Then,
\partial^{R}_{A,B,C}
is the inverse of
\delta^{L}_{A,B,C}
and
\partial^{L}_{A,B,C}
is the inverse of
\delta^{R}_{A,B,C}
. The proof that
\delta^{L}_{A,B,C};\partial^{R}_{A,B,C}=1_{A\otimes(B\oplus C)}
follows from the commuting diagram in Figure
3
in the Appendix
C
due to its size. The other equalities follow similarly.

As the linear distributivities are isomorphisms and
m\,\colon\bot\rightarrow\top
is invertible,
\mathrm{mix}_{A,B}\,\colon A\otimes B\rightarrow A\oplus B
is an isomorphism.
∎

Remark 86
.

The fact that the linearly distributive structure of a MLDC is compact does not mean however mean that the duoidal structure is strong, in other words that the structure maps are all invertible and the medial maps are in particular the canonical flip. We shall see an example of such a MLDC in Section
5.4
.

6
Medial Linear Functors and Transformations

With the definition of medial LDCs well established, we now turn to the appropriate functors between these categories and the transformations between such functors. Similar to the 2-category of cartesian LDCs
\CLDC
, the corresponding 2-category would form a sub-2-category of
\LDC
.

As such, these “medial linear functors” are linear functors that also interact coherently with the duoidal structure. Given that there are two established definitions of duoidal functors, there are naturally at least two distinct types of “medial linear functors”. However, we shall define only the specific class required for the linearly distributive Fox theorem. These functors are symmetric and strong, paralleling the strong symmetric monoidal functors found between symmetric monoidal categories. It is important to emphasize that these should not be regarded as the only valid choices for 1-cells between medial LDCs. Rather, they are the appropriate ones within the specific context of our work.

Definition 87
.

Let
{\mathbb{X}}
and
{\mathbb{Y}}
be symmetric linearly distributive categories. A
strong symmetric medial linear functor

F=(F_{\otimes},F_{\oplus})\,\colon\mbox{${\mathbb{X}}$}\rightarrow\mbox{${\mathbb{Y}}$}
consists of:

•

a functor
F_{\otimes}\,\colon\mbox{${\mathbb{X}}$}\rightarrow\mbox{${\mathbb{Y}}$}
, equipped with

–

an invertible morphism
m_{\top}\,\colon\top\xrightarrow{\sim}F_{\otimes}(\top)
,

–

a natural isomorphism

\displaystyle m_{\otimes}\,\colon(F_{\otimes}\times F_{\otimes});\otimes\Rightarrow\otimes;F_{\otimes}

\displaystyle{m_{\otimes}}_{A,B}\,\colon F_{\otimes}(A)\otimes F_{\otimes}(B)\xrightarrow{\sim}F_{\otimes}(A\otimes B)

–

a morphism
m_{\bot}\,\colon\bot\rightarrow F_{\otimes}(\bot)
,

–

a natural transformation

\displaystyle m_{\oplus}\,\colon(F_{\otimes}\times F_{\otimes});\oplus\Rightarrow\otimes;F_{\oplus}

\displaystyle{m_{\oplus}}_{A,B}\,\colon F_{\otimes}(A)\oplus F_{\otimes}(B)\rightarrow F_{\otimes}(A\oplus B)

such that

–

(F_{\otimes},m_{\top},m_{\otimes})\,\colon(\mbox{${\mathbb{X}}$},\otimes,\top)\rightarrow(\mbox{${\mathbb{Y}}$},\otimes,\top)
is a strong symmetric monoidal functor,

–

(F_{\otimes},m_{\bot},m_{\oplus})\,\colon(\mbox{${\mathbb{X}}$},\oplus,\bot)\rightarrow(\mbox{${\mathbb{Y}}$},\oplus,\bot)
is a symmetric monoidal functor,

•

a functor
F_{\oplus}\,\colon\mbox{${\mathbb{X}}$}\rightarrow\mbox{${\mathbb{Y}}$}
, equipped with

–

an invertible morphism
n_{\bot}\,\colon F_{\oplus}(\bot)\xrightarrow{\sim}\bot
,

–

a natural isomorphism

\displaystyle n_{\oplus}\,\colon\oplus;F_{\oplus}\Rightarrow(F_{\oplus}\times F_{\oplus});\oplus

\displaystyle{n_{\oplus}}_{A,B}\,\colon F_{\oplus}(A\oplus B)\xrightarrow{\sim}F_{\oplus}(A)\oplus F_{\oplus}(B)

–

a morphism
n_{\top}\,\colon F_{\oplus}(\top)\rightarrow\top
,

–

a natural transformation

\displaystyle n_{\oplus}\,\colon\otimes;F_{\oplus}\Rightarrow(F_{\oplus}\times F_{\oplus});\otimes

\displaystyle{n_{\otimes}}_{A,B}\,\colon F_{\oplus}(A\otimes B)\rightarrow F_{\oplus}(A)\otimes F_{\oplus}(B)

such that

–

(F_{\oplus},n_{\bot},n_{\oplus})\,\colon(\mbox{${\mathbb{X}}$},\oplus,\bot)\rightarrow(\mbox{${\mathbb{Y}}$},\oplus,\bot)
is a strong symmetric comonoidal functor,

–

(F_{\oplus},n_{\top},n_{\otimes})\,\colon(\mbox{${\mathbb{X}}$},\oplus,\bot)\rightarrow(\mbox{${\mathbb{Y}}$},\oplus,\bot)
is a symmetric comonoidal functor,

•

linear strength natural transformations

\displaystyle v_{\otimes}^{R}\,\colon\oplus;F_{\otimes}\Rightarrow(F_{\oplus}\times F_{\otimes});\oplus

\displaystyle{v_{\otimes}^{R}}_{A,B}\,\colon F_{\otimes}(A\oplus B)\rightarrow F_{\oplus}(A)\oplus F_{\otimes}(B)

\displaystyle v_{\otimes}^{R}\,\colon(F_{\otimes}\times F_{\oplus});\otimes\Rightarrow\otimes;F_{\oplus}

\displaystyle{v_{\oplus}^{R}}_{A,B}\,\colon F_{\otimes}(A)\otimes F_{\oplus}(B)\rightarrow F_{\oplus}(A\otimes B)

satisfying the coherence conditions ensuring that

–

(F_{\otimes},m_{\bot},m_{\oplus},m_{\top}^{-1},m_{\otimes}^{-1})\,\colon(\mbox{${\mathbb{X}}$},\oplus,\bot,\otimes,\top)\rightarrow(\mbox{${\mathbb{Y}}$},\oplus,\bot,\otimes,\top)
is a bilax duoidal functor, i.e. (
62
) holds,

–

(F_{\oplus},n_{\bot}^{-1},n_{\oplus}^{-1},n_{\top},n_{\otimes})\,\colon(\mbox{${\mathbb{X}}$},\oplus,\bot,\otimes,\top)\rightarrow(\mbox{${\mathbb{Y}}$},\oplus,\bot,\otimes,\top)
is a bilax duoidal functor, i.e. (
63
) holds,

–

F=(F_{\otimes},F_{\oplus})\,\colon\mbox{${\mathbb{X}}$}\rightarrow\mbox{${\mathbb{Y}}$}
is a symmetric linear functor, and

–

the linear strengths interact coherently with the
\bot
-contraction and
\top
-cocontraction maps (
64
), with the medial transformation (
65
), and
m_{\oplus}
and
n_{\otimes}
(
66
).

\begin{gathered}\begin{aligned} &\Delta_{\bot};(m_{\bot}\otimes m_{\bot});{m_{\otimes}}_{\bot,\bot}=m_{\bot};F_{\otimes}(\Delta_{\bot})\\
&(m_{\top}\oplus m_{\top});{m_{\oplus}}_{\top,\top};F_{\otimes}(\nabla_{\top})=\nabla_{\top};m_{\top}\\
&m;m_{\top}=m_{\bot};F_{\otimes}(m)\\
\\
&({m_{\otimes}}_{A,B}\oplus{m_{\otimes}}_{C,D});{m_{\oplus}}_{A\otimes B,C\otimes D};F_{\otimes}(\mu_{A,B,C,D})\\
&=\mu_{F_{\otimes}(A),F_{\otimes}(B),F_{\otimes}(C),F_{\otimes}(D)};({m_{\oplus}}_{A,C}\otimes{m_{\oplus}}_{B,D});{m_{\otimes}}_{A\oplus C,B\oplus D}\end{aligned}\end{gathered}

(62)

\begin{gathered}\begin{aligned} &F_{\oplus}(\Delta_{\bot});{n_{\otimes}}_{\bot,\bot};(n_{\bot}\otimes n_{\bot})=n_{\bot};\Delta_{\bot}\\
&{n_{\oplus}}_{\top,\top};(n_{\top}\oplus n_{\top});\nabla_{\top}=F_{\oplus}(\nabla_{\top});n_{\top}\\
&n_{\bot};m=F_{\oplus}(m);n_{\top}\\
\\
&F_{\oplus}(\mu_{A,B,C,D});{n_{\otimes}}_{A\oplus C,B\oplus D};({n_{\oplus}}_{A,C}\otimes{n_{\oplus}}_{B,D})\\
&={n_{\oplus}}_{A\otimes B,C\otimes D};({n_{\otimes}}_{A,B}\oplus{n_{\otimes}}_{C,D});\mu_{F_{\oplus}(A),F_{\oplus}(B),F_{\oplus}(C),F_{\oplus}(D)}\end{aligned}\end{gathered}

(66)

We record here some commuting diagrams that will of use in the next Section
7.2
, involving the linear strengths, the duoidal functor structure maps and the canonical flip.

Proposition 88
.

F=(F_{\otimes},F_{\oplus})\,\colon\mbox{${\mathbb{X}}$}\rightarrow\mbox{${\mathbb{Y}}$}

0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 6.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-2.1867pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{F_{\otimes}(\tau^{\oplus}_{A,B,C,D})}$}}}\kern 6.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-49.94446pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 104.68361pt\raise-28.94446pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.55554pt\hbox{$\textstyle{(F_{\oplus}(A)\oplus F_{\oplus}(C))\oplus(F_{\otimes}(B)\oplus F_{\otimes}(D))\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 194.4923pt\raise-43.47223pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 6.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-2.13113pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{{n_{\oplus}^{-1}}_{A,C}\oplus{m_{\oplus}}_{B,D}}$}}}\kern 6.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 194.4923pt\raise-49.94446pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern-56.28777pt\raise-57.88892pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.55554pt\hbox{$\textstyle{F_{\oplus}((A\oplus C)\oplus(B\oplus D))\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 67.264pt\raise-68.09503pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 6.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-2.22836pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{{\nu^{R}_{\otimes}}_{A\oplus C,B\oplus D}}$}}}\kern 6.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 134.80869pt\raise-57.88892pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 134.80869pt\raise-57.88892pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.55554pt\hbox{$\textstyle{F_{\oplus}(A\oplus C)\oplus F_{\otimes}(B\oplus D)}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces\end{gathered}

(67)

\displaystyle({\nu^{R}_{\otimes}}_{A,B}\oplus{\nu^{R}_{\otimes}}_{C,D});\tau^{\oplus}_{F_{\oplus}(A),F_{\otimes}(B),F_{\oplus}(C),F_{\otimes}(D)};({n_{\oplus}^{-1}}_{A,C}\oplus{m_{\oplus}}_{B,D})

\displaystyle={m_{\oplus}}_{A\oplus B,C\oplus D};F_{\otimes}(\tau^{\oplus}_{A,B,C,D});{\nu^{R}_{\otimes}}_{A\oplus C,B\oplus D}

\displaystyle{\nu^{R}_{\oplus}}_{A\otimes B,C\otimes D};F_{\oplus}(\tau^{\otimes}_{A,B,C,D});{n_{\otimes}}_{A\otimes C,B\otimes D}

\displaystyle=({m_{\otimes}^{-1}}_{A,B}\otimes{n_{\otimes}}_{C,D});\tau^{\otimes}_{F_{\otimes}(A),F_{\otimes}(B),F_{\oplus}(C),F_{\oplus}(D)};({\nu^{R}_{\oplus}}_{A,C}\otimes{\nu^{R}_{\oplus}}_{B,D})

Proof.

The proof for the first equality is given by the commuting diagram in Figure
4
, in the Appendix
C
due to its size, and the second equality is proved similarly.
∎

Now, we turn to the discussion of transformations. As there are several potential notions of medial linear functors, there are multiple definitions of “medial linear transformations”. However, we will introduce only the specific definition relevant to our treatment of medial LDCs.

Definition 89
.

Let
F,G\,\colon\mbox{${\mathbb{X}}$}\rightarrow\mbox{${\mathbb{Y}}$}
be strong symmetric medial linear functors. A
strong medial linear transformation

\alpha=(\alpha_{\otimes},\alpha_{\oplus})\,\colon F\Rightarrow G
consists of:

•

a natural transformation

\alpha_{\otimes}\,\colon F_{\otimes}\Rightarrow G_{\otimes}\qquad\qquad{\alpha_{\otimes}}_{A}\,\colon F_{\otimes}(A)\rightarrow G_{\otimes}(A)

such that

–

\alpha_{\otimes}\,\colon(F_{\otimes},m_{\top}^{F},m_{\otimes}^{F})\Rightarrow(G_{\otimes},m_{\top}^{G},m_{\otimes}^{G})
is a monoidal transformation,

–

\alpha_{\otimes}\,\colon(F_{\otimes},m_{\bot}^{F},m_{\oplus}^{F})\Rightarrow(G_{\otimes},m_{\bot}^{G},m_{\oplus}^{G})
is a monoidal transformation,

•

a natural transformation

\alpha_{\oplus}\,\colon G_{\oplus}\Rightarrow F_{\oplus}\qquad\qquad{\alpha_{\oplus}}_{A}\,\colon G_{\oplus}(A)\rightarrow F_{\oplus}(A)

such that

–

\alpha_{\oplus}\,\colon(G_{\oplus},n_{\bot}^{G},n_{\oplus}^{G})\Rightarrow(F_{\oplus},n_{\bot}^{F},n_{\oplus}^{F})
is a comonoidal transformation,

–

\alpha_{\oplus}\,\colon(G_{\oplus},{n_{\top}}^{G},{n_{\otimes}}^{G})\Rightarrow(F_{\oplus},{n_{\top}^{F}},{n_{\otimes}}^{F})
is a comonoidal transformation,

satisfying the coherence conditions ensuring that
\alpha=(\alpha_{\otimes},\alpha_{\oplus})
is a linear transformation, i.e. (
32
) holds.

Remark 90
.

Notice that the conditions that
\alpha_{\otimes}\,\colon F_{\otimes}\Rightarrow G_{\otimes}
satisfies in the above definition are equivalent to asking that

\alpha_{\otimes}\,\colon(F_{\otimes},m_{\bot}^{F},{m_{\oplus}}^{F},{m_{\top}^{-1}}^{F},{m_{\otimes}^{-1}}^{F})\Rightarrow(G_{\otimes},m_{\bot}^{G},{m_{\oplus}}^{G},{m_{\top}^{-1}}^{G},{m_{\otimes}^{-1}}^{G})

is a bilax duoidal transformation and, similarly, the conditions that
\alpha_{\oplus}\,\colon G_{\oplus}\Rightarrow F_{\oplus}
satisfies is equivalent to

\alpha_{\oplus}\,\colon(G_{\oplus},{n_{\bot}^{-1}}^{G},{n_{\oplus}^{-1}}^{G},{n_{\oplus}}^{G},{n_{\top}}^{G})\Rightarrow(F_{\oplus},{n_{\bot}^{-1}}^{F},{n_{\oplus}^{-1}}^{F},{n_{\oplus}}^{F},{n_{\top}}^{F})

being a bilax duoidal transformation.

6.1
Frobenius Medial Linear Functors and Transformations

As in the case of Frobenius linear functors, we can consider strong symmetric medial linear functors whose pair of functors
F_{\otimes}
and
F_{\oplus}
are equal.

Definition 91
.

A strong symmetric medial linear functor
F=(F_{\otimes},F_{\oplus})\,\colon\mbox{${\mathbb{X}}$}\rightarrow\mbox{${\mathbb{Y}}$}
is
Frobenius
if

1.

F_{\otimes}=F_{\oplus}
,

2.

m_{\bot}=n_{\bot}^{-1}
,
{m_{\oplus}}_{A,B}={n_{\oplus}^{-1}}_{A,B}
,
n_{\top}=m_{\top}^{-1}
, and
{n_{\otimes}}_{A,B}={m_{\otimes}^{-1}}_{A,B}
,

3.

{\nu^{R}_{\otimes}}_{A,B}={n_{\oplus}}_{A,B}
, and
{\nu^{R}_{\oplus}}_{A,B}={m_{\otimes}}_{A,B}
.

Given the degeneracy, we can once more give an alternative characterization of such linear functors.

Proposition 92
.

Consider
{\mathbb{X}}
and
{\mathbb{Y}}
be symmetric linearly distributive categories, then the following notions coincide:

•

Frobenius strong symmetric medial linear functors
F=(F_{\otimes},F_{\oplus})\,\colon\mbox{${\mathbb{X}}$}\rightarrow\mbox{${\mathbb{Y}}$}
, and

•

strong symmetric
\otimes
-monoidal and strong symmetric
\oplus
-comonoidal functors

(F,m_{\top},m_{\otimes},n_{\bot},n_{\oplus})\,\colon\mbox{${\mathbb{X}}$}\rightarrow\mbox{${\mathbb{Y}}$}
satisfying (
33
), (
34
) and (
68
).

6.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 133.30865pt\raise-57.88892pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 133.30865pt\raise-57.88892pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.55554pt\hbox{$\textstyle{(F(A)\oplus F(C))\otimes(F(B)\oplus F(D))}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces\end{gathered}

(68)

Remark 93
.

Recall that (
33
) is equivalent to asking that
(F,m_{\otimes},m_{\top},n_{\oplus},n_{\bot})\,\colon\mbox{${\mathbb{X}}$}\rightarrow\mbox{${\mathbb{Y}}$}
is a Frobenius linear functor. Moreover, (
34
) and (
68
) are equivalent to asking that

(F,n_{\bot}^{-1},n_{\oplus}^{-1},m_{\top}^{-1},m_{\otimes}^{-1})\,\colon(\mbox{${\mathbb{X}}$},\oplus,\bot,\otimes,\top)\rightarrow(\mbox{${\mathbb{Y}}$},\oplus,\bot,\otimes,\top)
is a bilax duoidal functor. As Frobenius linear functors and bilax duoidal functors compose, we know Frobenius strong symmetric medial linear functors compose as well.

If we then consider strong medial linear transformations between such linear functors, we can see that the paired transformations must in fact be a section-retraction pair.

Proposition 94
.

Consider Frobenius strong symmetric medial linear functors
F,G\,\colon\mbox{${\mathbb{X}}$}\rightarrow\mbox{${\mathbb{Y}}$}
, then

•

\alpha_{\otimes}\,\colon F\Rightarrow G
and
\alpha_{\oplus}\,\colon G\Rightarrow F
are a
\otimes
-monoidal and
\oplus
-comonoidal transformations, and

•

for each object
A\in\mbox{${\mathbb{X}}$}
,
{\alpha_{\otimes}}_{A}\,\colon F(A)\rightarrow G(A)
is a section of
{\alpha_{\oplus}}_{A}\,\colon G(A)\rightarrow F(A)
, i.e.
{\alpha_{\otimes}};\alpha_{\oplus}=1_{F}
.

Proof.

Let
\alpha=(\alpha_{\otimes},\alpha_{\oplus})\,\colon F\Rightarrow G
be a strong medial linear transformation between Frobenius strong symmetric medial linear functors. This means in particular that:

•

\alpha_{\otimes}\,\colon(F,m_{\top}^{F},m_{\otimes}^{F})\Rightarrow(G,m_{\top}^{F},m_{\otimes}^{G})
is a
\otimes
-monoidal transformation

•

\alpha_{\otimes}\,\colon(F,{n_{\bot}^{F}}^{-1},{n_{\oplus}^{F}}^{-1})\Rightarrow(G,{n_{\bot}^{G}}^{-1},{n_{\oplus}^{G}}^{-1})
is a
\oplus
-monoidal transformation, meaning
\alpha_{\otimes}\,\colon(F,n_{\bot}^{F},n_{\oplus}^{F})\Rightarrow(G,n_{\bot}^{G},n_{\oplus}^{G})

Similarly, for
\alpha_{\oplus}
. Further, as
\alpha=(\alpha_{\otimes},\alpha_{\oplus})\,\colon F\Rightarrow G
is a linear transformation, the diagrams in (
32
) holds, the first of which is as follows in the context of Frobenius linear functors:

{\alpha_{\otimes}}_{A\oplus B};{n_{\oplus}^{G}}_{A,B};({\alpha_{\oplus}}_{A}\oplus 1_{G(B)})={n_{\oplus}^{F}}_{A,B};(1_{F(A)}\oplus{\alpha_{\otimes}}_{B})\qquad(*)

Letting
B=\bot
implies that
\alpha_{\otimes};\alpha_{\oplus}=1_{F(A)}
by the following commuting diagram.

\textstyle{G(A)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{G({u^{R}_{\oplus}}_{A}^{-1})}
\scriptstyle{(\mathrm{nat})}
\textstyle{F(A)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{{\alpha_{\otimes}}_{A}}
\scriptstyle{F({u^{R}_{\oplus}}_{A}^{-1})}
\scriptstyle{{u^{R}_{\oplus}}^{-1}_{F(A)}}
(
10
)
\textstyle{F(A\oplus\bot)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{(*)}
\scriptstyle{{n_{\oplus}^{F}}_{A,\bot}}
\scriptstyle{{\alpha_{A\oplus\bot}}}
\textstyle{G(A\oplus\bot)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{G({u^{R}_{\oplus}}_{A})}
\scriptstyle{{n_{\oplus}^{G}}_{A,\bot}}
(
10
)
\textstyle{G(A)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{{\alpha_{\oplus}}_{A}}
\scriptstyle{{u^{R}_{\oplus}}^{-1}_{G(A)}}
\textstyle{G(A)\oplus G(\bot)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{{\alpha_{\oplus}}_{A}\oplus 1_{G(\bot)}}
\scriptstyle{1_{G(A)}\oplus{n^{G}_{\bot}}^{-1}}
\scriptstyle{(\mathrm{exch})}

\scriptstyle{(\mathrm{nat})}

\scriptstyle{1_{F(A)}\oplus{n_{\bot}^{F}}^{-1}}
\textstyle{F(A)\oplus F(\bot)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{1_{F(A)}\oplus{\alpha_{\oplus}}_{\bot}}
\textstyle{F(A)\oplus G(\bot)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{1_{F(A)}\oplus n^{G}_{\bot}}
\textstyle{F(A)\oplus\bot\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{{u^{R}_{\oplus}}_{F(A)}}

The top composite is
\alpha_{\otimes};\alpha_{\oplus}
, while the bottom composite is
1_{F(A)}
since
\alpha_{\oplus}
is
\oplus
-comonoidal.
∎

An immediate situation where one gets such a linear transformation is when considering an invertible
\otimes
-monoidal and
\oplus
-comonoidal natural transformation.

Proposition 95
.

Given an invertible
\otimes
-monoidal and
\oplus
-comonoidal natural transformations
\alpha\,\colon F\Rightarrow G
between strong symmetric Frobenius medial linear functors, the pair
(\alpha,\alpha^{-1})\,\colon F\Rightarrow G
is a strong medial linear transformation.

Proof.

Consider an invertible
\otimes
-monoidal and
\oplus
-comonoidal natural transformations
\alpha\,\colon F\Rightarrow G
, then by Lemma
45
,
(\alpha,\alpha^{-1})\,\colon F\Rightarrow G
is a linear transformation. To be a strong medial linear transformation, it remains to show
\alpha\,\colon(F_{\otimes},m_{\bot}^{F},m_{\oplus}^{F})\Rightarrow(G_{\otimes},m_{\bot}^{G},m_{\oplus}^{G})
is a
\oplus
-monoidal transformation and
\alpha^{-1}\,\colon(G_{\oplus},n_{\top}^{G},n_{\otimes}^{G})\Rightarrow(F_{\oplus},n_{\top}^{F},n_{\otimes}^{F})
is a
\otimes
-comonoidal transformation.

Since
F
and
G
are Frobenius, this is in fact requiring that
\alpha\,\colon(F,{n_{\bot}^{F}}^{-1},{n_{\oplus}^{F}}^{-1})\Rightarrow(G,{n_{\bot}^{G}}^{-1},{n_{\oplus}^{G}}^{-1})
is a
\oplus
-monoidal transformation and
\alpha^{-1}\,\colon(G,{m_{\top}^{G}}^{-1},{m_{\otimes}^{G}}^{-1})\Rightarrow(F,{m_{\top}^{F}}^{-1},{m_{\otimes}^{F}}^{-1})
is a
\otimes
-comonoidal transformation, which is true as
\alpha\,\colon(F,n_{\bot}^{F},n_{\oplus}^{F})\Rightarrow(G,n_{\bot}^{G},n_{\oplus}^{G})
is a
\oplus
-comonoidal transformation and
\alpha\,\colon(F,m_{\top}^{F},m_{\otimes}^{F})\Rightarrow(G,m_{\top}^{G},m_{\otimes}^{G})
is a
\otimes
-monoidal transformation respectively.
∎

6.2
2-Category
\SMLDC

We now prove that these functors and transformations as 1-cells and 2-cells, with symmetric medial LDCs as 0-cells, form a 2-category.

Theorem 96
.

Symmetric medial linearly distributive categories, strong symmetric medial linear functors, and strong medial linear transformations form a 2-category, which is denoted by
\SMLDC
.

Proof.

Consider symmetric medial LDCs
{\mathbb{X}}
,
{\mathbb{Y}}
and
{\mathbb{Z}}
. Let
F=(F_{\otimes},F_{\oplus})\,\colon\mbox{${\mathbb{X}}$}\rightarrow\mbox{${\mathbb{Y}}$}
and
G=(G_{\otimes},G_{\oplus})\,\colon\mbox{${\mathbb{Y}}$}\rightarrow\mbox{${\mathbb{Z}}$}
be strong symmetric medial linear functor. Then, their horizontal composite
F;G=(F_{\otimes};G_{\otimes},F_{\oplus};G_{\oplus})\,\colon\mbox{${\mathbb{X}}$}\rightarrow\mbox{${\mathbb{Y}}$}
is given by:

•

functor
F_{\otimes};G_{\otimes}\,\colon\mbox{${\mathbb{X}}$}\rightarrow\mbox{${\mathbb{Z}}$}
, equipped with

\displaystyle m^{G}_{\top};G_{\otimes}(m^{F}_{\top})\,\colon\top\rightarrow G_{\otimes}(F_{\otimes}(\top))

\displaystyle{m_{\otimes}}^{G}_{F_{\otimes}(A),F_{\otimes}(B)};G_{\otimes}({m^{F}_{\otimes}}_{A,B})\,\colon G_{\otimes}(F_{\otimes}(A))\otimes G_{\otimes}(F_{\otimes}(B))\rightarrow G_{\otimes}(F_{\otimes}(A\otimes B))

\displaystyle m^{G}_{\bot};G_{\otimes}(m^{F}_{\bot})\,\colon\bot\rightarrow G_{\otimes}(F_{\otimes}(\bot))

\displaystyle{m^{G}_{\oplus}}_{F_{\otimes}(A),F_{\otimes}(B)};G_{\otimes}({m^{F}_{\oplus}}_{A,B})\,\colon G_{\otimes}(F_{\otimes}(A))\oplus G_{\otimes}(F_{\otimes}(B))\rightarrow G_{\otimes}(F_{\otimes}(A\oplus B))

•

a functor
F_{\oplus};G_{\oplus}\,\colon\mbox{${\mathbb{X}}$}\rightarrow\mbox{${\mathbb{Z}}$}
, equipped with

\displaystyle G_{\oplus}(n^{F}_{\bot});n^{G}_{\bot}\,\colon G_{\oplus}(F_{\oplus}(\bot))\rightarrow\bot

\displaystyle G_{\otimes}({n^{F}_{\oplus}}_{A,B});{n^{G}_{\oplus}}_{F_{\oplus}(A),F_{\oplus}(B)}\,\colon G_{\oplus}(F_{\oplus}(A\oplus B))\rightarrow G_{\oplus}(F_{\oplus}(A))\oplus G_{\oplus}(F_{\oplus}(B))

\displaystyle G_{\oplus}(n^{F}_{\top});n^{G}_{\top}\,\colon G_{\oplus}(F_{\oplus}(\top))\rightarrow\top

\displaystyle G_{\otimes}({n^{F}_{\otimes}}_{A,B});{n^{G}_{\otimes}}_{F_{\oplus}(A),F_{\oplus}(B)}\,\colon G_{\oplus}(F_{\oplus}(A\otimes B))\rightarrow G_{\oplus}(F_{\oplus}(A))\otimes G_{\oplus}(F_{\oplus}(B))

•

linear strength natural transformations

\displaystyle G_{\otimes}({v_{\otimes}^{R}}_{A,B}^{F});{\nu^{R}_{\otimes}}^{G}_{F_{\oplus}(A),F_{\otimes}(B)}\,\colon G_{\otimes}(F_{\otimes}(A\oplus B))\rightarrow G_{\oplus}(F_{\oplus}(A))\oplus G_{\otimes}(F_{\otimes}(B))

\displaystyle{\nu_{\oplus}^{R}}_{F_{\otimes}(A),F_{\oplus}(B)}^{G};G_{\oplus}({\nu_{\oplus}^{R}}_{A,B}^{F})\,\colon G_{\otimes}(F_{\otimes}(A))\otimes G_{\oplus}(F_{\oplus}(B))\rightarrow G_{\oplus}(F_{\oplus}(A\otimes B))

It is immediate that
F;G\,\colon\mbox{${\mathbb{X}}$}\rightarrow\mbox{${\mathbb{Z}}$}
is a strong symmetric linear functor, and that
F_{\otimes};G_{\otimes}\,\colon\mbox{${\mathbb{X}}$}\rightarrow\mbox{${\mathbb{Z}}$}
and
F_{\oplus};G_{\oplus}\,\colon\mbox{${\mathbb{X}}$}\rightarrow\mbox{${\mathbb{Z}}$}
are bilax duoidal functors, as the composition is inherited from
\LDC
and
\DUO
in the appropriate ways. It remains only to show the additional coherence conditions also hold for
F;G\,\colon\mbox{${\mathbb{X}}$}\rightarrow\mbox{${\mathbb{Z}}$}
.

The first diagram of (

) holds by the following commuting diagram and the second diagram holds similarly.
