The second diagram of (
65
) and of (
66
) for
F;G\,\colon\mbox{${\mathbb{X}}$}\rightarrow\mbox{${\mathbb{Z}}$}
hold similarly.

Finally, it is immediate to show that the identity linear functors and the identity linear transformations are inherited by
\SMLDC
and that strong medial linear transformations are closed under the standard vertical and horizontal composition of linear transformations.
∎

Proposition 97
.

The inclusion map
\inc\,\colon\CLDC\rightarrow\SMLDC
determines a 2-functor.

Proof.

By Theorem
77
, every cartesian LDC has a canonical symmetric medial LDC structure.

Consider a cartesian linear functor

F=(F_{\times},F_{+})\,\colon(\mbox{${\mathbb{X}}$},\times,\bone,+,\bzero)\rightarrow(\mbox{${\mathbb{Y}}$},\times,\bone,+,\bzero)

Then, in particular,
(F_{\times},m_{\bone}^{-1},m^{-1}_{\times})\,\colon(\mbox{${\mathbb{X}}$},\times,\bone)\rightarrow(\mbox{${\mathbb{Y}}$},\times,\bone)
and
(F_{+},n^{-1}_{\bzero},n^{-1}_{+})\,\colon(\mbox{${\mathbb{X}}$},+,\bzero)\rightarrow(\mbox{${\mathbb{Y}}$},+,\bzero)
are respectively comonoidal and monoidal functors. Then, by Proposition
63
, the following are bilax duoidal functors,

(F_{\times},p_{\bzero},p_{+},m_{\bone}^{-1},m^{-1}_{\times})\,\colon(\mbox{${\mathbb{X}}$},+,\bzero,\times,\bone)\rightarrow(\mbox{${\mathbb{Y}}$},+,\bzero,\times,\bone)

(F_{+},n_{\bzero}^{-1},n_{+}^{-1},q_{\bone},q_{\times})\,\colon(\mbox{${\mathbb{X}}$},+,\bzero,\times,\bone)\rightarrow(\mbox{${\mathbb{Y}}$},+,\bzero,\times,\bone)

where
p_{\bzero},p_{\times},q_{\bone}
and
q_{\times}
are defined in Lemma . In order to prove
F=(F_{\times},F_{+})
is a strong symmetric medial linear functor, it remains to show the additional coherence conditions.

(
64
) holds by the universal property of initial and terminal objects:

\displaystyle\bzero\xrightarrow{b_{F_{+}(\bzero\times\bzero)}}F_{+}(\bzero\times\bzero)

\displaystyle=\bzero\xrightarrow{\Delta_{\bzero}}\bzero\times\bzero\xrightarrow{p_{\bzero}\times n_{\bzero}^{-1}}F_{\times}(\bzero)\times F_{\times}(\bzero)\xrightarrow{{\nu_{+}^{R}}_{\bzero,\bzero}}F_{+}(\bzero\times\bzero)

\displaystyle=\bzero\xrightarrow{n_{\bzero}^{-1}}F_{+}(\bzero)\xrightarrow{F_{+}(\Delta_{\bzero})}F_{+}(\bzero\times\bzero)

\displaystyle F_{\times}(\bone+\bone)\xrightarrow{t_{F_{\times}(\bone+\bone)}}\bone

\displaystyle=F_{\times}(\bone+\bone)\xrightarrow{{\nu_{\times}^{R}}_{\bone,\bone}}F_{+}(\bone)+F_{\times}(\bone)\xrightarrow{q_{\bone}+m^{-1}_{\bone}}\bone+\bone\xrightarrow{\nabla_{\bone}}\bone

\displaystyle=F_{\times}(\bone+\bone)\xrightarrow{F_{\times}(\nabla_{\bone})}F_{\times}(\bone)\xrightarrow{m_{\bone}^{-1}}\bone

The first coherence condition of (
65
) is

\displaystyle F_{\times}(\mu_{A,B,C,D});{m_{\times}^{-1}}_{A+C,B+D};({\nu_{\times}^{R}}_{A,C}\times{\nu_{\times}^{R}}_{B,D})

\displaystyle={\nu_{\times}^{R}}_{A\times B,C\times D};({q_{\times}}_{A,B}+{m^{-1}_{\times}}_{C,D});\mu_{F_{+}(A),F_{+}(B),F_{\times}(C),F_{\times}(D)}

and it holds as follows. The left-hand side and right-hand sides composed with the
0^{th}
projection are given by

\displaystyle F_{\times}(\mu_{A,B,C,D});{m_{\times}^{-1}}_{A+C,B+D};({\nu_{\times}^{R}}_{A,C}\times{\nu_{\times}^{R}}_{B,D});\pi^{0}_{F_{+}(A)+F_{\times}(C),F_{+}(B)+F_{\times}(D)}

\displaystyle=F_{\times}(\mu_{A,B,C,D});{m_{\times}^{-1}}_{A+C,B+D};\pi^{0}_{F_{\times}(A+C),F_{\times}(B+D)};{\nu_{\times}^{R}}_{A,C}

\displaystyle=F_{\times}(\mu_{A,B,C,D});F_{\times}(\pi^{0}_{A+C,B+D});{\nu_{\times}^{R}}_{A,C}=F_{\times}(\pi^{0}_{A,B}+\pi^{0}_{C,D});{\nu_{\times}^{R}}_{A,C}

\displaystyle{\nu_{\times}^{R}}_{A\times B,C\times D};({q_{\times}}_{A,B}+{m^{-1}_{\times}}_{C,D});\mu_{F_{+}(A),F_{+}(B),F_{\times}(C),F_{\times}(D)};\pi^{0}_{F_{+}(A)+F_{\times}(C),F_{+}(B)+F_{\times}(D)}

\displaystyle={\nu_{\times}^{R}}_{A\times B,C\times D};({q_{\times}}_{A,B}+{m^{-1}_{\times}}_{C,D});(\pi^{0}_{F_{+}(A),F+(B)}+\pi^{0}_{F_{\times}(C),F_{\times}(D)})

\displaystyle={\nu_{\times}^{R}}_{A\times B,C\times D};(F_{+}(\pi^{0}_{A,B})+F_{\times}(\pi^{0}_{C,D}))=F_{\times}(\pi^{0}_{A,B}+\pi^{0}_{C,D});{\nu_{\times}^{R}}_{A,C}

Similarly, when composed with the
1^{st}
projection,

\displaystyle F_{\times}(\mu_{A,B,C,D});{m_{\times}^{-1}}_{A+C,B+D};({\nu_{\times}^{R}}_{A,C}\times{\nu_{\times}^{R}}_{B,D});\pi^{0}_{F_{+}(A)+F_{\times}(C),F_{+}(B)+F_{\times}(D)}

\displaystyle=F_{\times}(\pi^{1}_{A,B}+\pi^{1}_{C,D});{\nu_{\times}^{R}}_{B,D}

\displaystyle{\nu_{\times}^{R}}_{A\times B,C\times D};({q_{\times}}_{A,B}+{m^{-1}_{\times}}_{C,D});\mu_{F_{+}(A),F_{+}(B),F_{\times}(C),F_{\times}(D)};\pi^{1}_{F_{+}(A)+F_{\times}(C),F_{+}(B)+F_{\times}(D)}

\displaystyle=F_{\times}(\pi^{1}_{A,B}+\pi^{1}_{C,D});{\nu_{\times}^{R}}_{B,D}

Then, by the universal properties of products, the first coherence condition of (
65
) holds. The second condition similarly by the universal properties of coproducts.

The first condition of (
66
) is

\displaystyle{\nu_{+}^{R}}_{A,B\times C};F_{+}({\alpha_{\times}^{-1}}_{A,B,C});{q_{\times}}_{A\times B,C}

\displaystyle=(1_{F_{\times}(A)}\times{q_{\times}}_{B,C});{\alpha_{\times}^{-1}}_{F_{\times}(A),F_{+}(B),F+(C)};({\nu^{R}_{+}}_{A,B}\times 1_{F_{+}(C)})

The left-hand side and right-hand sides composed with the projections are given by

\displaystyle{\nu_{+}^{R}}_{A,B\times C};F_{+}({\alpha_{\times}^{-1}}_{A,B,C});{q_{\times}}_{A\times B,C};\pi^{0}_{F_{+}(A\times B),F_{+}(C)}={\nu_{+}^{R}}_{A,B\times C};F_{+}({\alpha_{\times}^{-1}}_{A,B,C});F_{+}(\pi^{0}_{A\times B,C})

\displaystyle={\nu_{+}^{R}}_{A,B\times C};F_{+}(1_{A}\times\pi^{0}_{B,C})=(1_{F_{\times}(A)}\times F_{+}(\pi^{0}_{B,C}));{\nu_{+}^{R}}_{A,B}

\displaystyle(1_{F_{\times}(A)}\times{q_{\times}}_{B,C});{\alpha_{\times}^{-1}}_{F_{\times}(A),F_{+}(B),F+(C)};({\nu^{R}_{+}}_{A,B}\times 1_{F_{+}(C)});\pi^{0}_{F_{+}(A\times B),F_{+}(C)}

\displaystyle=(1_{F_{\times}(A)}\times{q_{\times}}_{B,C});{\alpha_{\times}^{-1}}_{F_{\times}(A),F_{+}(B),F+(C)};\pi^{0}_{F_{\times}(A)\times F_{+}(B),F_{+}(C)};{\nu^{R}_{+}}_{A,B}

\displaystyle=(1_{F_{\times}(A)}\times{q_{\times}}_{B,C});(1_{F_{\times}(A)}\times\pi^{0}_{F_{+}(B),F_{+}(C)});{\nu^{R}_{+}}_{A,B}=(1_{F_{\times}(A)}\times F_{+}(\pi^{0}_{B,C}));{\nu_{+}^{R}}_{A,B}

\displaystyle{\nu_{+}^{R}}_{A,B\times C};F_{+}({\alpha_{\times}^{-1}}_{A,B,C});{q_{\times}}_{A\times B,C};\pi^{1}_{F_{+}(A\times B),F_{+}(C)}={\nu_{+}^{R}}_{A,B\times C};F_{+}({\alpha_{\times}^{-1}}_{A,B,C});F_{+}(\pi^{1}_{A\times B,C})

\displaystyle={\nu_{+}^{R}}_{A,B\times C};F_{+}(\pi^{1}_{A,B\times C});F_{+}(\pi^{1}_{B,C})={\nu_{+}^{R}}_{A,B\times C};F_{+}(!_{A}\times 1_{B\times C});F_{+}({u^{L}_{\times}}_{B\times C}^{-1});F_{+}(\pi^{1}_{B,C})

\displaystyle=(F_{\times}(!_{A})\times F_{+}(1_{B\times C}));{\nu_{+}^{R}}_{\bone,B\times C};F_{+}({u^{L}_{\times}}_{B\times C}^{-1});F_{+}(\pi^{1}_{B,C})

\displaystyle=(F_{\times}(!_{A})\times F_{+}(1_{B\times C}));(m_{\bone}^{-1}\times 1_{F_{+}(B\times C)});{u^{L}_{\times}}_{F_{+}(B\times C)}^{-1};F_{+}(\pi^{1}_{B,C})=\pi^{1}_{F_{\times}(A),F_{+}(B\times C)};F_{+}(\pi^{1}_{B,C})

\displaystyle(1_{F_{\times}(A)}\times{q_{\times}}_{B,C});{\alpha_{\times}^{-1}}_{F_{\times}(A),F_{+}(B),F+(C)};({\nu^{R}_{+}}_{A,B}\times 1_{F_{+}(C)});\pi^{1}_{F_{+}(A\times B),F_{+}(C)}

\displaystyle=(1_{F_{\times}(A)}\times{q_{\times}}_{B,C});{\alpha_{\times}^{-1}}_{F_{\times}(A),F_{+}(B),F+(C)};\pi^{1}_{F_{\times}(A)\times F_{+}(B),F_{+}(C)}

\displaystyle=(1_{F_{\times}(A)}\times{q_{\times}}_{B,C});\pi^{1}_{F_{\times}(A),F+(B)\times F_{+}(C)};\pi^{1}_{F_{+}(B),F_{+}(C)}

\displaystyle=\pi^{1}_{F_{\times}(A),F_{+}(B\times C)};{q_{\times}}_{B,C};\pi^{1}_{F_{+}(B),F_{+}(C)}=\pi^{1}_{F_{\times}(A),F_{+}(B\times C)};F_{+}(\pi^{1}_{B,C})

Then, by the universal property of products, the first condition of (
66
) holds. The second condition of (
66
) follows by the universal properties of coproducts for the same reasons as its counterpart.

Consider a linear transformation
\alpha=(\alpha_{\times},\alpha_{+})\,\colon F\Rightarrow G\,\colon\mbox{${\mathbb{X}}$}\rightarrow\mbox{${\mathbb{Y}}$}
between strong symmetric linear functors between cartesian LDCs. In particular, it is a pair consisting of a monoidal transformation
\alpha_{\times}\colon(F_{\times},m^{F}_{\bone},m^{F}_{\times})\Rightarrow(G_{\times},m^{G}_{\bone},m^{G}_{\times})
and
\alpha_{+}\colon(F_{+},n^{F}_{\bzero},n^{F}_{+})\Rightarrow(G_{+},n^{G}_{\bzero},n^{G}_{+})
. By Proposition
6.2
,
\alpha_{\times}\colon(F,p^{F}_{\bzero},p^{F}_{+})\Rightarrow(G,p^{G}_{\bzero},p^{G}_{+})
is a monoidal transformation and
\alpha_{+}\colon(F_{+},q^{F}_{\bone},q^{F}_{\times})\Rightarrow(G_{+},q^{G}_{\bone},q^{G}_{\times})
is a comonoidal transformation. As such,
\alpha
is a strong medial linear transformation.

Therefore, the inclusion map
\inc\,\colon\CLDC\rightarrow\SMLDC
is well-defined. The proof that it is moreover a 2-functor follows by examining that canonical medial linear functor structure of a cartesian linear functor described above respects composition and identities.
∎

It is fairly immediate to see that the above results can restricted to Frobenius linear functors.

Corollary 98
.

Symmetric medial linearly distributive categories, strong symmetric Frobenius medial linear functors and strong medial linear transformations form a sub 2-category of
\SMLDC
, denoted by
\mathbf{SFMLDC}
. Further, the inclusion map
\inc\,\colon\CLDC\rightarrow\SMLDC
restricts to an inclusion 2-functor
\inc\,\colon\mathbf{FCLDC}\rightarrow\mathbf{SFMLDC}

Proof.

The only technicality to verify is that given a Frobenius cartesian linear functor
F=(F_{\times},F_{+})\,\colon\mbox{${\mathbb{X}}$}\rightarrow\mbox{${\mathbb{Y}}$}
, the induced strong symmetric medial linear functor is Frobenius, in other words that the canonical
+
-monoidal structure on
(F_{\times},p_{\bzero},p_{+})\,\colon(\mbox{${\mathbb{X}}$},\bzero,+)\rightarrow(\mbox{${\mathbb{Y}}$},\bzero,+)
corresponds to
(F_{+},n_{\bzero}^{-1},n_{+}^{-1})\,\colon(\mbox{${\mathbb{X}}$},\bzero,+)\rightarrow(\mbox{${\mathbb{Y}}$},\bzero,+)
and similarly for the canonical
\times
-comonoidal structure on
F_{+}
. This is immediate however as
F_{+}=F_{\times}
. While the structure maps
n_{\bzero}
and
{n_{+}}_{A,B}
are not guaranteed to have inverses, if they do, they must be the canonical ones
p_{\bzero}
and
{p_{+}}_{A,B}
. Similarly,
q_{\bone}={m_{\bone}}^{-1}
and
{q_{\times}}_{A,B}={m_{\times}^{-1}}_{A,B}
.
∎

7
Linearly Distributive Fox Theorem

It is now time to discuss the construction on a symmetric medial LDCs which will yield a cartesian LDCs, mirroring the category of cocommutative comonoids of a symmetric monoidal category to create a cartesian category in the standard Fox theorem.

If we review our earlier characterization of cartesian LDCs in Proposition
65
, which followed as a direct consequence of the Fox theorem and its dual, we see that a cartesian LDC
{\mathbb{X}}
is in particular a symmetric LDC, where each object
A\in\mbox{${\mathbb{X}}$}
is equipped with four morphisms

\Delta_{A}\,\colon A\rightarrow A\otimes A\qquad e_{A}\,\colon A\rightarrow\top\qquad\nabla_{A}\,\colon A\oplus A\rightarrow A\qquad u_{A}\,\colon\bot\rightarrow A

such that
\langle A,\Delta_{A},e_{A}\rangle
is a cocommutative
\otimes
-comonoid and
\langle A,\nabla_{A},u_{A}\rangle
is a commutative
\oplus
-monoid.

To someone well-versed in duoidal category theory, this structure will be familiar. Indeed, these are the same maps and structures of a duoidal bimonoid in a duoidal category. This is the key to the theorem and why the duoidal structure of the medial LDCs is essential.

7.1
Medial Bimonoids

Definition 99
.

Let
{\mathbb{X}}
be a symmetric medial linearly distributive category.

•

A
medial bimonoid
in
{\mathbb{X}}
is a quintuple
\langle A,\Delta_{A},u_{A},\nabla_{A},e_{A}\rangle
consisting of an object
A
and four morphisms

\Delta_{A}\,\colon A\rightarrow A\otimes A\qquad e_{A}\,\colon A\rightarrow\top\qquad\nabla_{A}\,\colon A\oplus A\rightarrow A\qquad u_{A}\,\colon\bot\rightarrow A

in
{\mathbb{X}}
such that

–

\langle A,\Delta_{A},e_{A}\rangle
is a
\otimes
-comonoid,

–

\langle A,\nabla_{A},u_{A}\rangle
is a
\oplus
-monoid,

satisfying coherence conditions between
\otimes
-comonoid and
\oplus
-monoid structure maps (
69
).

\begin{gathered}\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.55554pt\hbox{\kern 44.11104pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&&&&\cr&&&\cr&&&\cr&&&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-16.61108pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.55554pt\hbox{$\textstyle{A\oplus (69)

Alternatively, a medial bimonoid in
{\mathbb{X}}
is a duoidal bimonoid in the duoidal category
(\mbox{${\mathbb{X}}$},\oplus,\bot,\otimes,\top)
.

•

A medial bimonoid
\langle A,\Delta_{A},e_{A},\nabla_{A},u_{A}\rangle
is
bicommutative
if
\langle A,\Delta_{A},e_{A}\rangle
is cocommutative
\otimes
-comonoid and
\langle A,\nabla_{A},u_{A}\rangle
is a commutative
\oplus
-monoid.

•

A
bimonoid morphism

f\,\colon\langle A,\Delta_{A},u_{A},\nabla_{A},e_{A}\rangle\rightarrow\langle B,\Delta_{B},u_{B},\nabla_{B},e_{B}\rangle
is a morphism
f\,\colon A\rightarrow B
in
{\mathbb{X}}
such that

–

f\,\colon\langle A,\Delta_{A},e_{A}\rangle\rightarrow\langle B,\Delta_{B},e_{B}\rangle
is a
\otimes
-comonoid morphism, and

–

f\,\colon\langle A,\nabla_{A},u_{A}\rangle\rightarrow\langle B,\nabla_{B},u_{B}\rangle
is a
\oplus
-monoid morphism

It is immediate that the
\otimes
and
\oplus
units in a symmetric medial LDC have canonical medial bimonoid structures given by the duoidal structure maps, as the
\otimes
-comonoid structure of
\bot
and
\oplus
-monoid structure for
\top
are part of the definition of a medial LDC, and the other coherence conditions follow easily.

Proposition 100
.

Given a symmetric medial linearly distributive category
{\mathbb{X}}
, the following

\langle\top,{u_{\otimes}}_{\top}\,\colon\top\rightarrow\top\otimes\top,1_{\top}\,\colon\top\rightarrow\top,\nabla_{\top}\,\colon\top\oplus\top\rightarrow\top,m\,\colon\bot\rightarrow\top\rangle

\langle\bot,\Delta_{\bot}\,\colon\bot\rightarrow\bot\otimes\bot,m\,\colon\bot\rightarrow\top,{u_{\oplus}}_{\bot}\,\colon\bot\oplus\bot\rightarrow\bot,1_{\bot}\,\colon\bot\rightarrow\bot\rangle

are bicommutative bimonoids.

Furthermore, medial bimonoids combine together via the tensor and par monoidal products to produce new medial bimonoids as follows.

Proposition 101
.

Consider a pair of bicommutative medial bimonoids
\langle A,\Delta_{A},e_{A},\nabla_{A},u_{A}\rangle
and
\langle B,\Delta_{B},e_{B},\nabla_{B},u_{B}\rangle
in a symmetric medial linearly distributive category
{\mathbb{X}}
. Then, the object
A\otimes B
equipped with morphisms

\displaystyle\Delta_{A\otimes B}=A\otimes B\xrightarrow{\Delta_{A}\otimes\Delta_{B}}(A\otimes A)\otimes(B\otimes B)\xrightarrow{\tau^{\otimes}_{A,A,B,B}}(A\otimes B)\otimes(A\otimes B)

\displaystyle t_{A\otimes B}=A\otimes B\xrightarrow{e_{A}\otimes e_{B}}\top\otimes\top\xrightarrow{{u_{\otimes}}_{\top}^{-1}}\top

\displaystyle\nabla_{A\otimes B}=(A\otimes B)\oplus(A\otimes B)\xrightarrow{\mu_{A,B,A,B}}(A\oplus A)\otimes(B\oplus B)\xrightarrow{\nabla_{A}\otimes\nabla_{B}}A\otimes B

\displaystyle u_{A\otimes B}=\bot\xrightarrow{\Delta_{\bot}}\bot\otimes\bot\xrightarrow{u_{A}\otimes u_{B}}A\otimes B

and the object
A\oplus B
equipped with morphisms

\displaystyle\Delta_{A\oplus B}=A\oplus B\xrightarrow{\Delta_{A}\oplus\Delta_{B}}(A\otimes A)\oplus(B\otimes B)\xrightarrow{\mu_{A,A,B,B}}(A\oplus B)\otimes(A\oplus B)

\displaystyle t_{A\oplus B}=A\oplus B\xrightarrow{e_{A}\oplus e_{B}}\top\oplus\top\xrightarrow{\nabla_{\top}}\top

\displaystyle\nabla_{A\oplus B}=(A\oplus B)\oplus(A\oplus B)\xrightarrow{\tau^{\oplus}_{A,B,A,B}}(A\oplus A)\oplus(B\oplus B)\xrightarrow{\nabla_{A}\oplus\nabla_{B}}A\oplus B

\displaystyle u_{A\oplus B}=\bot\xrightarrow{{u_{\oplus}}_{\bot}^{-1}}\bot\oplus\bot\xrightarrow{u_{A}\oplus u_{B}}A\oplus B

are bicommutative medial bimonoids.

Proof.

It follows immediately, by Lemma
13
that
\langle A\otimes B,\Delta_{A\otimes B},t_{A\otimes B}\rangle
is a cocommutative
\otimes
-comonoid.
\langle A\otimes B,\nabla_{A\otimes B},e_{A\otimes B}\rangle
is a commutative
\oplus
-monoid as follows:

\nabla_{A\otimes B}
satisfies the associativity coherence condition in (
15
) by the commuting diagram below.

∎

Let us for a moment consider our most important class of symmetric medial LDCs, the cartesian LDCs, and discuss the medial bimonoids present in these cases.

Proposition 102
.

Every object
A
in a cartesian linearly distributive category
{\mathbb{X}}
has a canonical unique bicommutative medial bimonoid structure

\langle A,\langle 1_{A},1_{A}\rangle\,\colon A\rightarrow A\times A,t_{A}\,\colon A\rightarrow\bone,[1_{A},1_{A}]\,\colon A+A\rightarrow A,b_{A}\,\colon\bzero\rightarrow A\rangle

and every arrow
f\,\colon A\rightarrow B
in
{\mathbb{X}}
is a medial bimonoid morphism with respect to these canonical structures.

Proof.

By Lemma
12
, it is clear
\langle A,\langle 1_{A},1_{A}\rangle,t_{A}\rangle
is the unique cocommutative
\times
-comonoid structure on
A
and, dually,
\langle A,[1_{A},1_{A}],b_{A}\rangle
is the unique commutative
+
-monoid structure on
A
. The proof the structure maps equally satisfy the additional medial bimonoid coherence conditions is a simple exercises using the canonical medial structure of a cartesian LDC and universal properties. Once more, by Lemma
12
and its dual, every arrow is a medial bimonoid morphism with respect to these structures.
∎

Definition 103
.

Let
{\mathbb{X}}
be a symmetric medial linearly distributive category. Define
\B[\mbox{${\mathbb{X}}$}]
to be the category of bicommutative medial bimonoids and medial bimonoid morphisms in
{\mathbb{X}}
.

The fact that this is in fact a category is an easy exercise, as the category of commutative
\oplus
-monoids and of cocommutative
\otimes
-comonoids in
{\mathbb{X}}
would themselves be categories. The above propositions will be the key to showing that
\B[\mbox{${\mathbb{X}}$}]
is more than a simple category, but is in fact a symmetric LDC.

Lemma 104
.

Let
{\mathbb{X}}
be a symmetric medial linearly distributive category, then
\B[\mbox{${\mathbb{X}}$}]

\otimes
and
\oplus

and

\B[\mbox{${\mathbb{X}}$}]

{\mathbb{X}}

{\mathbb{X}}
. They are left up to the reader.
∎

Theorem 105
.

Let
{\mathbb{X}}
be a symmetric medial linearly distributive category, then
\B[\mbox{${\mathbb{X}}$}]
is a cartesian linearly distributive category.

Proof.

We will prove this by showing
\B[\mbox{${\mathbb{X}}$}]
satisfies the characterization in Proposition
65
.

Given a bicommutative medial bimonoid
\langle A,\Delta_{A},e_{A},\nabla_{A},u_{A}\rangle
, then the coherence conditions in (
69
) ensure that the four bimonoid maps
\Delta_{A}
,
e_{A}
,
\nabla_{A}
and
u_{A}
are themselves medial bimonoid morphisms. These give exactly the component maps of the four necessary natural transformations. These in fact determine natural transformations as the naturality squares correspond exactly to the conditions for every map to be a bimonoid morphism.

The conditions that the component maps form comonoid and monoid structures is immediate by the definition of a bicommutative medial bimonoid and the remaining coherence conditions are satisfied as the correspond to exactly to the construction of the tensor and par monoidal structures in
\B[\mbox{${\mathbb{X}}$}]
as outlined in Propositions
100
and
101
.
∎

And with this theorem, the first part of the linearly distributive Fox theorem has been proven. It remains only to show that the
\B[-]
construction extends canonically to the strong symmetric medial linear functors and the medial linear transformation, giving a right adjoint to the inclusion functor outlined in Proposition
97
.

It is also important to note that negation is preserved by the
\B[-]
construction.

Proposition 106
.

Let
{\mathbb{X}}
be a symmetric medial linearly distributive category with negation, then
\B[\mbox{${\mathbb{X}}$}]
is a cartesian linearly distributive category with negation, and therefore a bounded distributive lattice.

Proof.

As
{\mathbb{X}}
is a symmetric medial LDC with negation, there is a full and faithful functor
(-)^{\perp}\,\colon\mbox{${\mathbb{X}}$}^{op}\rightarrow\mbox{${\mathbb{X}}$}
such that
\mbox{${\mathbb{X}}$}(A\otimes B,C^{\perp})\cong\mbox{${\mathbb{X}}$}(A,(B\oplus C)^{\perp})
defined in , in other words
{\mathbb{X}}
is a
*
-autonomous category. We shall show that
(-)^{\perp}
extends to a functor on
\B[\mbox{${\mathbb{X}}$}]
, meaning
\B[\mbox{${\mathbb{X}}$}]
is a
*
-autonomous category whose monoidal product is cartesian. Then, by Joyal’s paradox, it is a bounded distributive lattice.

Consider a bicommutative medial bimonoid
\langle A,\Delta_{A},e_{A},\nabla_{A},u_{A}\rangle
, then define

\langle A^{\perp},\Delta_{A^{\perp}},e_{A^{\perp}},\nabla_{A^{\perp}},u_{A^{\perp}}\rangle
by

\displaystyle\Delta_{A^{\perp}}=A^{\perp}\xrightarrow{(\nabla_{A})^{\perp}}(A\oplus A)^{\perp}\xrightarrow{\epsilon_{A,A}}A^{\perp}\otimes A^{\perp}

\displaystyle e_{A^{\perp}}=A^{\perp}\xrightarrow{(u_{A})^{\perp}}\bot^{\perp}\xrightarrow{\beta_{\top}}\top

\displaystyle\nabla_{A^{\perp}}=A^{\perp}\oplus A^{\perp}\xrightarrow{\phi_{A,A}}(A\otimes A)^{\perp}\xrightarrow{(\Delta_{A})^{\perp}}A^{\perp}

\displaystyle u_{A^{\perp}}=\bot\xrightarrow{\chi_{\bot}}\top^{\perp}\xrightarrow{(e_{A})^{\perp}}A^{\perp}

This is a bicommutative medial bimonoid as follows.

\langle A^{\perp},\Delta_{A^{\perp}},e_{A^{\perp}}\rangle
is a cocommutative
\otimes
-comonoid as:

\displaystyle\Delta_{A^{\perp}};(1_{A^{\perp}}\otimes e_{A^{\perp}})

\displaystyle=(\nabla_{A})^{\perp};\epsilon_{A,A};(1_{A^{\perp}}\otimes(u_{A})^{\perp});(1_{A^{\perp}}\otimes\beta_{\top})

by definition

\displaystyle=(\nabla_{A})^{\perp};(u_{A}\oplus 1_{A})^{\perp};\epsilon_{\bot,A};(1_{A^{\perp}}\otimes\beta_{\top})

by naturality of
\epsilon

\displaystyle=((u_{A}\oplus 1_{A});\nabla_{A})^{\perp};\epsilon_{\bot,A};(1_{A^{\perp}}\otimes\beta_{\top})

by functoriality of
(-)^{\perp}

\displaystyle=({u^{A}_{\otimes}}_{\bot})^{\perp};\epsilon_{\bot,A};(1_{A^{\perp}}\otimes\beta_{\top})

as
A
is a medial bimonoid

\displaystyle={u^{R}_{\otimes}}_{A^{\perp}}

by
(*)

\displaystyle\Delta_{A^{\perp}};(\Delta_{A^{\perp}}\otimes 1_{A^{\perp}});{\alpha_{\otimes}}_{A^{\perp},A^{\perp},A^{\perp}}

\displaystyle=(\nabla_{A})^{\perp};\epsilon_{A,A};((\nabla_{A})^{\perp}\otimes 1_{A^{\perp}});(\epsilon_{A,A}\otimes 1_{A^{\perp}});{\alpha_{\otimes}}_{A^{\perp},A^{\perp},A^{\perp}}

by definition

\displaystyle=(\nabla_{A})^{\perp};(1_{A}\oplus\nabla_{A})^{\perp};\epsilon_{A,A\oplus A};(\epsilon_{A,A}\otimes 1_{A^{\perp}});{\alpha_{\otimes}}_{A^{\perp},A^{\perp},A^{\perp}}

by naturality of
\epsilon

\displaystyle=((1_{A}\oplus\nabla_{A});\nabla_{A})^{\perp};\epsilon_{A,A\oplus A};(\epsilon_{A,A}\otimes 1_{A^{\perp}});{\alpha_{\otimes}}_{A^{\perp},A^{\perp},A^{\perp}}

by functoriality of
(-)^{\perp}

\displaystyle=({\alpha_{\oplus}}_{A,A,A};(\nabla_{A}\oplus 1_{A});\nabla_{A})^{\perp};\epsilon_{A,A\oplus A};

\displaystyle\qquad(\epsilon_{A,A}\otimes 1_{A^{\perp}});{\alpha_{\otimes}}_{A^{\perp},A^{\perp},A^{\perp}}

as
A
is a medial bimonoid

\displaystyle=(\nabla_{A})^{\perp};(\nabla_{A}\oplus 1_{A})^{\perp};({\alpha_{\oplus}}_{A,A,A})^{\perp};

\displaystyle\qquad\epsilon_{A,A\oplus A};(\epsilon_{A,A}\otimes 1_{A^{\perp}});{\alpha_{\otimes}}_{A^{\perp},A^{\perp},A^{\perp}}

by functoriality of
(-)^{\perp}

\displaystyle=(\nabla_{A})^{\perp};(\nabla_{A}\oplus 1_{A})^{\perp};\epsilon_{A\oplus A,A};(1_{A^{\perp}}\otimes\epsilon_{A,A})

by
(*)

\displaystyle=(\nabla_{A})^{\perp};\epsilon_{A,A};(1_{A^{\perp}}\otimes(\nabla_{A})^{\perp});(1_{A^{\perp}}\otimes\epsilon_{A,A})

by naturality of
\epsilon

\displaystyle=\Delta_{A^{\perp}};(1_{A^{\perp}}\otimes\Delta_{A^{\perp}})

by definition

\displaystyle\Delta_{A^{\perp}};{\sigma_{\otimes}}_{A^{\perp},A^{\perp}}

\displaystyle=(\nabla_{A})^{\perp};\epsilon_{A,A};{\sigma_{\otimes}}_{A^{\perp},A^{\perp}}

by definition

\displaystyle=(\nabla_{A})^{\perp};({\sigma_{\oplus}}_{A,A})^{\perp};\epsilon_{A,A}

by
(*)

\displaystyle=({\sigma_{\oplus}}_{A,A};\nabla_{A})^{\perp};\epsilon_{A,A}

by functoriality of
(-)^{\perp}

\displaystyle=(\nabla_{A})^{\perp};\epsilon_{A,A}

as
A
is a medial bimonoid

\displaystyle=\Delta_{A^{\perp}}

by definition

The equalities marked with
(*)
are incredibly long to show directly, but fairly straightforward if one uses the circuit diagram (or proof net) calculus introduced by Blute, Cockett, Seely and Trimble
[
5
]
.

Similarly,
\langle A,\nabla_{A^{\perp}},u_{A^{\perp}}\rangle
is a commutative
\oplus
-monoid.

The first coherence condition of (
69
) holds as:

\displaystyle\nabla_{A^{\perp}};\Delta_{A^{\perp}}

\displaystyle=\phi_{A,A};(\Delta_{A})^{\perp};(\nabla_{A})^{\perp};\epsilon_{A,A}

by definition

\displaystyle=\phi_{A,A};(\nabla_{A};\Delta_{A})^{\perp};\epsilon_{A,A}

by functoriality of
(-)^{\perp}

\displaystyle=\phi_{A,A};((\Delta_{A}\oplus\Delta_{A});\mu_{A,A,A,A};(\nabla_{A}\oplus\nabla_{A}))^{\perp};\epsilon_{A,A}

as
A
is a medial bimonoid

\displaystyle=\phi_{A,A};(\nabla_{A}\oplus\nabla_{A})^{\perp}(\mu_{A,A,A,A})^{\perp};(\Delta_{A}\oplus\Delta_{A})^{\perp};\epsilon_{A,A}

by functoriality of
(-)^{\perp}

\displaystyle=((\nabla_{A})^{\perp}\otimes(\nabla_{A})^{\perp});\phi_{A\oplus A,A\oplus A};

\displaystyle\qquad(\mu_{A,A,A,A})^{\perp};\epsilon_{A\otimes A,A\otimes A};((\Delta_{A})^{\perp}\otimes(\Delta_{A})^{\perp})

by naturality of
\phi
and
\epsilon

\displaystyle=((\nabla_{A})^{\perp}\otimes(\nabla_{A})^{\perp});(\epsilon_{A,A}\otimes\epsilon_{A,A});

\displaystyle\qquad\mu_{A^{\perp},A^{\perp},A^{\perp},A^{\perp}};(\phi_{A,A}\oplus\phi_{A,A});((\Delta_{A})^{\perp}\otimes(\Delta_{A})^{\perp})

by (
58
)

\displaystyle=(\Delta_{A^{\perp}}\oplus\Delta_{A^{\perp}});\mu_{A^{\perp},A^{\perp},A^{\perp},A^{\perp}};(\nabla_{A^{\perp}}\otimes\nabla_{A^{\perp}})

by definition

The second coherence condition of (
69
) holds as:

\displaystyle u_{A^{\perp}};e_{A^{\perp}}

\displaystyle=\chi_{\bot};(e_{A})^{\perp};(u_{A})^{\perp};\beta_{\top}

by definition

\displaystyle=\chi_{\bot};(u_{A};e_{A})^{\perp};\beta_{\top}

by functoriality of
(-)^{\perp}

\displaystyle=\chi_{\bot};(m)^{\perp};\beta_{\top}

as
A
is a medial bimonoid

\displaystyle=m

by (
59
)

The third coherence condition of (
69
) holds as:

\displaystyle\nabla_{A^{\perp}};e_{A^{\perp}}

\displaystyle=\phi_{A,A};(\Delta_{A})^{\perp};(u_{A})^{\perp};\beta_{\top}

by definition

\displaystyle=\phi_{A,A};(u_{A};\Delta_{A})^{\perp};\beta_{\top}

by functoriality of
(-)^{\perp}

\displaystyle=\phi_{A,A};(\Delta_{\bot};(u_{A}\otimes u_{A}))^{\perp};\beta_{\top}

as
A
is a medial bimonoid

\displaystyle=\phi_{A,A};(u_{A}\otimes u_{A})^{\perp};(\Delta_{\bot})^{\perp};\beta_{\top}

by functoriality of
(-)^{\perp}

\displaystyle=(u_{A})^{\perp}\oplus(u_{A})^{\perp};\phi_{\bot,\bot};(\Delta_{\bot})^{\perp};\beta_{\top}

by naturality of
\phi

\displaystyle=(u_{A})^{\perp}\oplus(u_{A})^{\perp};(\beta_{\top}\oplus\beta_{\top});\nabla_{\top}

by (
57
)

\displaystyle=(e_{A^{\perp}}\oplus e_{A^{\perp}});\nabla_{\top}

by definition

The final coherence condition of (
69
) holds as:

\displaystyle u_{A^{\perp}};\Delta_{A^{\perp}}

\displaystyle=\chi_{\bot};(e_{A})^{\perp};(\nabla_{A})^{\perp};\epsilon_{A,A}

by definition

\displaystyle=\chi_{\bot};(\nabla_{A};e_{A})^{\perp};\epsilon_{A,A}

by functoriality of
(-)^{\perp}

\displaystyle=\chi_{\bot};((e_{A}\oplus e_{A});\nabla_{\top})^{\perp};\epsilon_{A,A}

as
A
is a medial bimonoid

\displaystyle=\chi_{\bot};(\nabla_{\top})^{\perp};(e_{A}\oplus e_{A})^{\perp};\epsilon_{A,A}

by functoriality of
(-)^{\perp}

\displaystyle=\chi_{\bot};(\nabla_{\top})^{\perp};\epsilon_{\top,\top};((e_{A})^{\perp}\otimes(e_{A})^{\perp})

by naturality of
\epsilon

\displaystyle=\Delta_{\bot};(\chi_{\bot}\otimes\chi_{\bot});((e_{A})^{\perp}\otimes(e_{A})^{\perp})

by (
57
)

\displaystyle=\Delta_{\bot};(u_{A^{\perp}}\oplus u_{A^{\perp}})

by definition

Now, let
f\,\colon\langle A,\Delta_{A},e_{A},\nabla_{A},u_{A}\rangle\rightarrow\langle B,\Delta_{B},e_{B},\nabla_{B},u_{B}\rangle
be a medial bimonoid morphism, then
f^{\perp}\,\colon B^{\perp}\rightarrow A^{\perp}
is a
\otimes
-comonoid morphism as follows.

\displaystyle\Delta_{B^{\perp}};(f^{\perp}\otimes f^{\perp})

\displaystyle=(\nabla_{B})^{\perp};\epsilon_{B,B};(f^{\perp}\otimes f^{\perp})

by definition

\displaystyle=(\nabla_{B})^{\perp};(f\oplus f)^{\perp};\epsilon_{A,A}

by naturality of
\epsilon

\displaystyle=((f\oplus f);\nabla_{B})^{\perp};\epsilon_{A,A}

by functoriality of
(-)^{\perp}

\displaystyle=(\nabla_{A};f)^{\perp};\epsilon_{A,A}

as
f
is a medial bimonoid morphism

\displaystyle=f^{\perp};(\nabla_{A})^{\perp};\epsilon_{A,A}

by functoriality of
(-)^{\perp}

\displaystyle=f^{\perp};\Delta_{A^{\perp}}

\displaystyle f^{\perp};e_{A^{\perp}}

\displaystyle=f^{\perp};(u_{A})^{\perp};\beta_{\top}

by definition

\displaystyle=(u_{A};f)^{\perp};\beta_{\top}

by functoriality of
(-)^{\perp}

\displaystyle=(u_{B})^{\perp};\beta_{\top}

as
f
is a medial bimonoid morphism

\displaystyle=u_{B^{\perp}}

by definition

Similarly,
f^{\perp}\,\colon B^{\perp}\rightarrow A^{\perp}
is a
\oplus
-monoid morphism and therefore a medial bimonoid morphism.

∎

7.2
The Right Adjoint 2-Functor
\B
[-]

Lemma 107
.

Consider a strong symmetric medial linear functor
F=(F_{\otimes},F_{\oplus})\,\colon\mbox{${\mathbb{X}}$}\rightarrow\mbox{${\mathbb{Y}}$}
between symmetric medial linearly distributive categories, then it canonically extends to a cartesian linear functor
\B[F]=(\B[F]_{\otimes},\B[F]_{\oplus})\,\colon\B[\mbox{${\mathbb{X}}$}]\rightarrow\B[\mbox{${\mathbb{Y}}$}]
.

Proof.

Consider a strong symmetric medial linear functor
F=(F_{\otimes},F_{\oplus})\,\colon\mbox{${\mathbb{X}}$}\rightarrow\mbox{${\mathbb{Y}}$}
. Recall that

(F_{\otimes},m_{\bot},m_{\oplus},m_{\top}^{-1},m_{\otimes}^{-1}),\,\,(F_{\oplus},n_{\bot}^{-1},n_{\oplus}^{-1},n_{\top},n_{\oplus})\,\colon(\mbox{${\mathbb{X}}$},\oplus,\bot,\otimes,\top)\rightarrow(\mbox{${\mathbb{Y}}$},\oplus,\bot,\otimes,\top)

are bilax duoidal functors and therefore preserve medial bimonoids and medial bimonoid morphisms by Proposition
60
.

Thus, given a bicommutative medial bimonoid
\langle A,\Delta_{A},e_{A},\nabla_{A},u_{A}\rangle
in
{\mathbb{X}}
, the object
F_{\otimes}(A)
equipped with the four maps

\displaystyle\Delta_{F_{\otimes}(A)}=F_{\otimes}(A)\xrightarrow{F_{\otimes}(\Delta_{A})}F_{\otimes}(A\otimes A)\xrightarrow{{m_{\otimes}}^{-1}_{A,A}}F_{\otimes}(A)\otimes F_{\otimes}(A)

\displaystyle e_{F_{\otimes}(A)}=F_{\otimes}(A)\xrightarrow{F_{\otimes}(e_{A})}F_{\otimes}(\top)\xrightarrow{m_{\top}^{-1}}\top

\displaystyle\nabla_{F_{\otimes}(A)}=F_{\otimes}(A)\oplus F_{\otimes}(A)\xrightarrow{{m_{\oplus}}_{A,A}}F_{\otimes}(A\oplus A)\xrightarrow{F_{\otimes}(\nabla_{A})}F_{\otimes}(A)

\displaystyle u_{F_{\otimes}(A)}=\bot\xrightarrow{m_{\bot}}F_{\otimes}(\bot)\xrightarrow{F_{\otimes}(u_{A})}F_{\otimes}(A)

and the object
F_{\oplus}(A)
equipped with the four maps

\displaystyle\Delta_{F_{\oplus}(A)}=F_{\oplus}(A)\xrightarrow{F_{\oplus}(\Delta_{A})}F_{\oplus}(A\otimes A)\xrightarrow{{n_{\otimes}}_{A,A}}F_{\oplus}(A)\otimes F_{\oplus}(A)

\displaystyle e_{F_{\oplus}(A)}=F_{\oplus}(A)\xrightarrow{F_{\oplus}(e_{A})}F_{\oplus}(\top)\xrightarrow{n_{\top}}\top

\displaystyle\nabla_{F_{\oplus}(A)}=F_{\oplus}(A)\oplus F_{\oplus}(A)\xrightarrow{{n_{\oplus}}^{-1}_{A,A}}F_{\oplus}(A\oplus A)\xrightarrow{F_{\oplus}(\nabla_{A})}F_{\oplus}(A)

\displaystyle u_{F_{\oplus}(A)}=\bot\xrightarrow{n^{-1}_{\bot}}F_{\oplus}(\bot)\xrightarrow{F_{\oplus}(u_{A})}F_{\oplus}(A)

are bicommutative medial bimonoid in
{\mathbb{Y}}
. Moreover, given a medial bimonoid morphism

f\,\colon\langle A,\Delta_{A},e_{A},\nabla_{A},u_{A}\rangle\rightarrow\langle B,\Delta_{B},e_{B},\nabla_{B},u_{B}\rangle

in
{\mathbb{X}}
, then

F_{\otimes}(f)\,\colon\langle F_{\otimes}(A),\Delta_{F_{\otimes}(A)},e_{F_{\otimes}(A)},\nabla_{F_{\otimes}(A)},u_{F_{\otimes}(A)}\rangle\rightarrow\langle F_{\otimes}(B),\Delta_{F_{\otimes}(B)},e_{F_{\otimes}(B)},\nabla_{F_{\otimes}(B)},u_{F_{\otimes}(B)}\rangle,

F_{\oplus}(f)\,\colon\langle F_{\oplus}(A),\Delta_{F_{\oplus}(A)},e_{F_{\oplus}(A)},\nabla_{F_{\oplus}(A)},u_{F_{\oplus}(A)}\rangle\rightarrow\langle F_{\oplus}(B),\Delta_{F_{\oplus}(B)},e_{F_{\oplus}(B)},\nabla_{F_{\oplus}(B)},u_{F_{\oplus}(B)}\rangle

are medial bimonoid morphisms in
{\mathbb{Y}}
.

As such, let
\B[F]_{\otimes}\,\colon\B[\mbox{${\mathbb{X}}$}]\rightarrow\B[\mbox{${\mathbb{Y}}$}]
denote the functor mapping bicommutative medial bimonoid on
A
to the bicommutative medial bimonoid on
F_{\otimes}(A)
and mapping medial bimonoid morphism
f
to
F_{\otimes}(f)
. Similarly, let
\B[F]_{\oplus}\,\colon\B[\mbox{${\mathbb{X}}$}]\rightarrow\B[\mbox{${\mathbb{Y}}$}]
denote the functor mapping bicommutative medial bimonoid on
A
to the bicommutative medial bimonoid on
F_{\oplus}(A)
and mapping medial bimonoid morphism
f
to
F_{\oplus}(f)
.

Further,
\B[F]_{\otimes}
is a strong symmetric monoidal functor
(\B[\mbox{${\mathbb{X}}$}],\otimes,\langle\top\rangle)\rightarrow(\B[\mbox{${\mathbb{Y}}$}],\otimes,\langle\top\rangle)
when equipped with

•

morphism
m_{\langle\top\rangle}\,\colon\langle\top\rangle\rightarrow\B[F]_{\otimes}(\langle\top\rangle)
, defined to be
m_{\top}\,\colon\top\rightarrow F_{\otimes}(\top)
, and

•

natural transformation

defined to be
{m_{\otimes}}_{A,B}\,\colon F_{\otimes}(A)\otimes F_{\otimes}(B)\rightarrow F_{\otimes}(A\otimes B)

as
(F_{\otimes},m_{\top},m_{\otimes})\,\colon(\mbox{${\mathbb{X}}$},\otimes,\top)\rightarrow(\mbox{${\mathbb{Y}}$},\otimes,\top)
is a strong symmetric monoidal functor by definition of strong symmetric medial linear functor, and its structure morphisms are bimonoid morphisms as follows.

n_{\langle\bot\rangle}\,\colon\B[F]_{\oplus}(\langle\bot\rangle)\rangle\langle\bot\rangle
, defined to be
m_{\bot}\,\colon F_{\oplus}(\bot)\rightarrow\bot
, and

•

natural transformation

{n_{\oplus}}_{\langle A\rangle,\langle B\rangle}\,\colon\B[F]_{\oplus}(\langle A\rangle\oplus\langle B\rangle)\rightarrow\B[F]_{\oplus}(\langle A\rangle)\oplus\B[F]_{\oplus}(\langle B\rangle)\,,

defined to be
{n_{\oplus}}_{A,B}\,\colon F_{\oplus}(A\oplus B)\rightarrow F_{\oplus}(A)\oplus F_{\oplus}(B)
.

Then,
\B[F]=(\B[F]_{\otimes},\B[F]_{\oplus})\colon\B[\mbox{${\mathbb{X}}$}]\rightarrow\B[\mbox{${\mathbb{Y}}$}]
is a strong symmetric linear functor, with linear strengths:

\displaystyle{v_{\otimes}^{R}}_{\langle A\rangle,\langle B\rangle}\,\colon\B[F]_{\otimes}(\langle A\rangle\oplus\langle B\rangle)\rightarrow\B[F]_{\oplus}(\langle A\rangle)\oplus\B[F]_{\otimes}(\langle B\rangle)

\displaystyle{v_{\oplus}^{R}}_{\langle A\rangle,\langle B\rangle}\,\colon\B[F]_{\otimes}(\langle A\rangle)\otimes\B[F]_{\oplus}(\langle B\rangle)\rightarrow\B[F]_{\oplus}(\langle A\rangle\otimes\langle B\rangle)

defined to be
{\nu^{R}_{\otimes}}_{A,B}\,\colon F_{\otimes}(A\oplus B)\rightarrow F_{\oplus}(A)\oplus F_{\otimes}(B)
and
{\nu^{R}_{\oplus}}_{A,B}\,\colon F_{\otimes}(A)\otimes F_{\oplus}(B)\rightarrow F_{\oplus}(A\otimes B)
respectively in
{\mathbb{Y}}
, as

{\nu^{R}_{\otimes}}_{A,B}
is a medial bimonoid morphism by the following commuting diagrams:

\textstyle{F_{\otimes}(A\oplus B)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}

\scriptstyle{F_{\otimes}(\Delta_{A}\oplus\Delta_{B})}
\scriptstyle{(\mathrm{nat})}

\scriptstyle{{\nu^{R}_{\otimes}}_{A\otimes A,B\otimes B}}

(

\scriptstyle{F_{\oplus}(u_{A})\oplus F_{\otimes}(u_{B})}
\textstyle{F_{\oplus}(A)\oplus F_{\oplus}(B)}

and
{v^{R}_{\oplus}}_{A,B}
is a medial bimonoid morphism similarly.
∎

Lemma 108
.

Given a strong medial linear transformations
\alpha=(\alpha_{\otimes},\alpha_{\oplus})\,\colon F\Rightarrow G
between strong symmetric medial linear functors, then it canonically extends to a linear transformation
\B[\alpha]=(\B[\alpha]_{\otimes},\B[\alpha]_{\oplus})\,\colon\B[F]\Rightarrow\B[G]
between strong symmetric linear functors.

Proof.

Consider a strong medial linear transformations
\alpha=(\alpha_{\otimes},\alpha_{\oplus})\,\colon F\Rightarrow G
between strong symmetric medial linear functors. Define
\B[\alpha]=(\B[\alpha]_{\otimes},\B[\alpha]_{\oplus})\,\colon\B[F]\Rightarrow\B[G]
, where

•

\B[\alpha]_{\otimes}\,\colon\B[F]_{\otimes}\rightarrow\B[G]_{\otimes}
has component morphisms
{\alpha_{\otimes}}_{A}\,\colon F_{\otimes}(A)\rightarrow G_{\otimes}(A)
in
{\mathbb{Y}}
,

•

\B[\alpha]_{\oplus}\,\colon\B[G]_{\oplus}\rightarrow\B[F]_{\oplus}
has component morphisms
{\alpha_{\oplus}}_{A}\,\colon G_{\oplus}(A)\rightarrow F_{\oplus}(A)
in
{\mathbb{Y}}
.

This is well-defined as
{\alpha_{\otimes}}_{A}\,\colon F_{\otimes}(A)\rightarrow G_{\otimes}(A)
is a medial bimonoid morphism since
\alpha_{\otimes}\,\colon(F_{\otimes},m_{\top}^{F},m_{\otimes}^{F})\Rightarrow(G_{\otimes},m^{G}_{\top},m^{G}_{\otimes})
is a monoidal transformation and therefore is a
\otimes
-monoid morphism and
\alpha_{\otimes}\,\colon(F_{\otimes},m_{\bot}^{F},m_{\oplus}^{F})\Rightarrow(G_{\otimes},m^{G}_{\bot},m^{G}_{\oplus})
is a monoidal transformation and therefore is a
\oplus
-monoidal morphism.

Similarly,
{\alpha_{\oplus}}_{A}\,\colon G_{\oplus}(A)\rightarrow F_{\oplus}(A)
is a medial bimonoid morphism.
∎

With the previous lemmas, we can state that the medial bimonoid constructions is a 2-functor between the 2-category of symmetric medial LDCs and the 2-category of cartesian LDCs as desired.

Proposition 109
.

\B[-]\,\colon\SMLDC\rightarrow\CLDC
is a 2-functor.

Proof.

By Theorem
105
, Lemma
107
and Lemma
108
,
\B[-]
determines well-defined maps from the 0-cells, 1-cells and 2-cells respectively of
\SMLDC
to
\CLDC
. The proof that this is 2-functorial follows immediately as composition in the 2-categories are defined similarly, the composite of two medial bimonoid morphisms is a bimonoid morphism, and 1-cells and 2-cells in
\SMLDC
preserve medial bimonoid morphisms.
∎

It is fairly immediate that given a strong symmetric Frobenius medial linear functor
F=(F_{\otimes},F_{\oplus})\,\colon\mbox{${\mathbb{X}}$}\rightarrow\mbox{${\mathbb{Y}}$}
, the induced cartesian linear functor
\B[F]\,\colon\B[\mbox{${\mathbb{X}}$}]\rightarrow\B[\mbox{${\mathbb{Y}}$}]
is itself Frobenius as
F_{\otimes}(A)=F_{\oplus}(A)
and the medial bimonoid structure maps of
F_{\otimes}(A)
are equal to the ones of
F_{\oplus}(A)
in the Frobenius contexts. Therefore:

Corollary 110
.

\B[-]\,\colon\SMLDC\rightarrow\CLDC
restricts to a 2-functor
\B[-]\,\colon\mathbf{SFMLDC}\rightarrow\mathbf{FCLDC}
.

We have finally arrived at the main result of our paper which motivated all the work:

Theorem 111

(The Linearly Distributive Fox Theorem)
.

\B[-]
is the right adjoint to the inclusion 2-functor,
\inc\vdash\B[-]\colon\CLDC\rightarrow\SMLDC
.

Proof.

To prove this 2-adjunction, we will provide the unit and counit 2-transformations.

Firstly, let us consider the unit
\boldsymbol{\eta}\,\colon 1_{\CLDC}\Rightarrow\inc;\B[-]
. Let
(\mbox{${\mathbb{X}}$},\times,\bone,+,\bzero)
be a cartesian LDC. Recall that every object
A\in\mbox{${\mathbb{X}}$}
has a canonical bicommutative medial bimonoid structure

\langle A,\langle 1_{A},1_{A}\rangle\,\colon A\rightarrow A\times A,t_{A}\,\colon A\rightarrow\bone,[1_{A},1_{A}]\,\colon A+A\rightarrow A,b_{A}\,\colon\bzero\rightarrow A\rangle

and every arrow
f\,\colon A\rightarrow B\in\mbox{${\mathbb{X}}$}
is a medial bimonoid morphism with respect to these canonical medial bimonoid structures, as discussed in Proposition
102
. Then, let
\eta_{\mbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\mathbb{X}}$}}\,\colon\mbox{${\mathbb{X}}$}\rightarrow\B[\mbox{${\mathbb{X}}$}]
be the functor that sends the objects in
{\mathbb{X}}
to their canonical medial bimonoid structures and that is identity on the arrows in
{\mathbb{X}}
.

\eta_{\mbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\mathbb{X}}$}}
is further a strong symmetric monoidal functor between
(\mbox{${\mathbb{X}}$},\times,\bone)\rightarrow(\B[\mbox{${\mathbb{X}}$}],\times,\langle\bone\rangle)
when equipped with

•

morphism
m_{\bone}\,\colon\langle\bone\rangle\rightarrow\eta_{X}(\bone)
to be
1_{\bone}\,\colon\bone\rightarrow\bone

•

natural transformation with component morphisms
{m_{\times}}_{A,B}\,\colon\eta_{X}(A)\times\eta_{X}(B)\rightarrow\eta_{X}(A\times B)
given by
1_{A\times B}\,\colon A\times B\rightarrow A\times B

as the canonical medial bimonoid structure on
\bone
described above is the same as as the medial bimonoid structure on
\bone
when
{\mathbb{X}}
is viewed as medial LDC:

\langle\bone,{u_{\times}}_{\bone},1_{\bone},\nabla_{\bone},m\rangle=\langle\bone,\langle 1_{\bone},1_{\bone}\rangle,t_{\bone},[1_{\bone},1_{\bone}],b_{\bone}\rangle

and the canonical medial bimonoid structure on
A\times B
is the same as the medial bimonoid structure of the tensor product
\times
in
\B[\mbox{${\mathbb{X}}$}]
between the canonical structures on
A
and
B
, i.e.

\displaystyle\langle A\times B,

\displaystyle([1_{A},1_{A}]\times[1_{B},1_{B}]);\tau^{\times}_{A,A,B,B},(t_{A}\times t_{B});{u_{\times}}_{\bone}^{-1},

\displaystyle\mu_{A,B,A,B};([1_{A},1_{A}]\times[1_{B},1_{B}]),\Delta_{\bot};(b_{A}\times b_{B})\rangle

\displaystyle=\langle A\times B,\langle 1_{A\times B},1_{A\times B}\rangle,t_{A\times B},[1_{A\times B},1_{A\times B}],b_{A\times B}\rangle

Similarly,
\eta_{X}
is a strong symmetric comonoidal functor between
(\mbox{${\mathbb{X}}$},+,\bzero)\rightarrow(\B[\mbox{${\mathbb{X}}$}],+,\langle\bzero,\rangle)
when equipped with

•

morphism
n_{\bzero}\,\colon\eta_{X}(\bzero)\rightarrow\langle\bzero\rangle
to be
1_{\bzero}\,\colon\bzero\rightarrow\bzero

•

natural transformation with component morphisms
{n_{+}}_{A,B}\,\colon\eta_{X}(A+B)\rightarrow\eta_{X}(A)+\eta_{X}(B)
given by
1_{A+B}\,\colon A+B\rightarrow A+B

Then,
\boldsymbol{\eta}_{\mbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\mathbb{X}}$}}=(\eta_{\mbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\mathbb{X}}$}},\eta_{\mbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\mathbb{X}}$}})\,\colon\mbox{${\mathbb{X}}$}\rightarrow\B[\mbox{${\mathbb{X}}$}]
is a cartesian linear functor, when further equipped with identity linear strengths.

Let
\boldsymbol{\eta}\,\colon 1_{\CLDC}\Rightarrow\inc;\B[-]
be defined to be the family of maps
\boldsymbol{\eta}_{\mbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\mathbb{X}}$}}=(\eta_{\mbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\mathbb{X}}$}},\eta_{\mbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\mathbb{X}}$}})\,\colon\mbox{${\mathbb{X}}$}\rightarrow\B[\mbox{${\mathbb{X}}$}]
. This is a 2-transformation because

•

\boldsymbol{\eta}
satisfies 1-cell naturality:

Let
{\mathbb{X}}
and
{\mathbb{X}}
’ be cartesian LDCS, and
F=(F_{\times},F_{+})\,\colon\mbox{${\mathbb{X}}$}\rightarrow\mbox{${\mathbb{X}}$}^{\prime}
be a cartesian linear functor between them. 1-cell naturality in this case means

\eta_{\mbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\mathbb{X}}$}};\B[\inc(F)]_{\times}=F_{\times};\eta_{\mbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\mathbb{X}}$}}\qquad\eta_{\mbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\mathbb{X}}$}};\B[\inc(F)]_{+}=F_{+};\eta_{\mbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\mathbb{X}}$}}

Now, these functors are defined as follows

\displaystyle\eta_{\mbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\mathbb{X}}$}};

\displaystyle\B[\inc(F)]_{\times}\,\colon

\displaystyle A\mapsto\langle F_{\times}(A),F_{\times}(\langle 1_{A},1_{A}\rangle);{m_{\times}^{-1}}_{A,A},F_{\times}(t_{A});{m_{\bone}^{-1}},{p_{+}}_{A,A};F_{\times}([1_{A},1_{A}]),{p_{\bzero}};F_{\times}(b_{A})\rangle

\displaystyle f\mapsto F_{\times}(f)

\displaystyle F_{\times};

\displaystyle\eta_{\mbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\mathbb{X}}$}}\,\colon

\displaystyle A\mapsto\langle F_{\times}(A),\langle 1_{F_{\times}(A)},1_{F_{\times}(A)}\rangle,t_{F_{\times}(A)},[1_{F_{\times}(A)},1_{F_{\times}(A)}],b_{F_{\times}(A)}\rangle

\displaystyle f\mapsto F_{\times}(f)

which are equal as the medial bimonoid structures on
F_{\times}(A)
is unique since
{\mathbb{X}}
’ is a cartesian LDC. One shows similarly that the other equality holds as both sides map to the unique medial bimonoid structure on
F_{+}(A)
.

•

\boldsymbol{\eta}
satisfies 2-cell naturality:
Let
{\mathbb{X}}
and
{\mathbb{X}}
’ be cartesian LDCS,
F=(F_{\times},F_{+}),G=(G_{\times},G_{+})\,\colon\mbox{${\mathbb{X}}$}\rightarrow\mbox{${\mathbb{X}}$}^{\prime}
be a cartesian linear functor between them, and
\alpha=(\alpha_{\times},\alpha_{+})\,\colon F\Rightarrow G
be a linear transformation between them. 2-cell naturality in this context means

1_{\eta_{\mbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\mathbb{X}}$}}};\B[\inc(\alpha)]_{\times}=\alpha_{\times};1_{\eta_{\mbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\mathbb{X}}$}}}\qquad 1_{\eta_{\mbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\mathbb{X}}$}}};\B[\inc(\alpha)]_{+}=\alpha_{+};1_{\eta_{\mbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\mathbb{X}}$}}}

Now, these natural transformations as component morphisms for each object
A\in\mbox{${\mathbb{X}}$}
the medial bimonoid morphisms given by

(1_{\eta_{\mbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\mathbb{X}}$}}};\B[\inc(\alpha)]_{\times})_{A}={\alpha_{\times}}_{A}=(\alpha_{\times};1_{\eta_{\mbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\mathbb{X}}$}}})_{A}

and therefore are equal. Similarly, for the second equality.

Secondly, let us consider the counit
\boldsymbol{\epsilon}\,\colon\B[-];\inc\Rightarrow 1_{\SMLDC}
. Let
(\mbox{${\mathbb{X}}$},\otimes,\top,\oplus,\bot)
be a symmetric medial LDC, then define
\epsilon_{\mbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\mathbb{X}}$}}\,\colon\B[\mbox{${\mathbb{X}}$}]\rightarrow\mbox{${\mathbb{X}}$}
to be the forgetful functor

\langle A,\Delta_{A},e_{A},\nabla_{A},s_{A}\rangle\mapsto A\qquad f\mapsto f

Now,
(\epsilon_{\mbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\mathbb{X}}$}},1_{\top},1_{-\otimes-})\,\colon(\B[\mbox{${\mathbb{X}}$}],\otimes,\langle\top\rangle)\rightarrow(\mbox{${\mathbb{X}}$},\otimes,\top)
is a strong symmetric monoidal functor when equipped with identity structure maps, and similarly

(\epsilon_{\mbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\mathbb{X}}$}},1_{\bot},1_{-\oplus-})\,\colon(\B[\mbox{${\mathbb{X}}$}],\oplus,\langle\bot\rangle)\rightarrow(\mbox{${\mathbb{X}}$},\oplus,\bot)
is a strong symmetric comonoid functor. Further,
(\epsilon_{\mbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\mathbb{X}}$}},1_{\bot},1_{-\oplus-},1_{\top},1_{-\otimes-})\,\colon(\B[\mbox{${\mathbb{X}}$}],\oplus,\langle\bot\rangle,\otimes,\langle\top\rangle)\rightarrow(\mbox{${\mathbb{X}}$},\oplus,\bot,\otimes,\top)
is a bilax duoidal functor.

Therefore,
\boldsymbol{\epsilon}_{\mbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\mathbb{X}}$}}=(\epsilon_{\mbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\mathbb{X}}$}},\epsilon_{\mbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\mathbb{X}}$}})\,\colon\B[\mbox{${\mathbb{X}}$}]\rightarrow\mbox{${\mathbb{X}}$}
is a strong symmetric medial linear functor when equipped with identity linear strengths.

Let
\boldsymbol{\epsilon}\,\colon\B[-];\inc\Rightarrow 1_{\SMLDC}
be defined to be the family of maps
\boldsymbol{\epsilon}_{\mbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\mathbb{X}}$}}=(\epsilon_{\mbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\mathbb{X}}$}},\epsilon_{\mbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\mathbb{X}}$}})\,\colon\B[\mbox{${\mathbb{X}}$}]\rightarrow\mbox{${\mathbb{X}}$}
. It satisfies 1-cell and 2-cell naturality fairly trivially and therefore it is a 2-natural transformation.

To prove the desired 2-adjunction, it remains solely to show the triangle inequalities hold.

\inc(\boldsymbol{\eta});\boldsymbol{\epsilon}_{\inc}=1_{\inc}\qquad\boldsymbol{\eta}_{\B[-]};\B[\boldsymbol{\epsilon}]=1_{\B[-]}

Consider a cartesian LDC
{\mathbb{X}}
, then

(\inc(\boldsymbol{\eta_{\mbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\mathbb{X}}$}}});\boldsymbol{\epsilon}_{\inc(\mbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\mathbb{X}}$})})_{\times}=(1_{\inc(\mbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\mathbb{X}}$})})_{\times}\qquad(\inc(\boldsymbol{\eta_{\mbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\mathbb{X}}$}}});\boldsymbol{\epsilon}_{\inc(\mbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\mathbb{X}}$})})_{+}=(1_{\inc(\mbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\mathbb{X}}$})})_{+}

as the left-hand side functors behave as identities:

\displaystyle(\inc(\boldsymbol{\eta_{\mbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\mathbb{X}}$}}});\boldsymbol{\epsilon}_{\inc(\mbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\mathbb{X}}$})})_{\times}=(\inc(\boldsymbol{\eta_{\mbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\mathbb{X}}$}}});\boldsymbol{\epsilon}_{\inc(\mbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\mathbb{X}}$})})_{+}\,\colon\qquad

\displaystyle A\mapsto\langle A,\langle 1_{A},1_{A}\rangle,t_{A},[1_{A},1_{A}],b_{A}\rangle\mapsto A

\displaystyle f\mapsto f\mapsto f

Consider a symmetric medial LDC
{\mathbb{X}}
, then

(\boldsymbol{\eta}_{\B[\mbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\mathbb{X}}$}]};\B[\boldsymbol{\epsilon}])_{\otimes}=(1_{\B[\mbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\mathbb{X}}$}]})_{\otimes}\qquad(\boldsymbol{\eta}_{\B[\mbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\mathbb{X}}$}]};\B[\boldsymbol{\epsilon}])_{\oplus}=(1_{\B[\mbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\mathbb{X}}$}]})_{\oplus}

as the left-hand side functors behave as identities:

\displaystyle(\boldsymbol{\eta}_{\B[\mbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\mathbb{X}}$}]};\B[\boldsymbol{\epsilon}])_{\otimes}=

\displaystyle(\boldsymbol{\eta}_{\B[\mbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\mathbb{X}}$}]};\B[\boldsymbol{\epsilon}])_{\oplus}\,\colon

\displaystyle\langle A,\Delta_{A},e_{A},\nabla_{A},u_{A}\rangle

\displaystyle\mapsto\,\langle\langle A\rangle,\langle 1_{\langle A\rangle},1_{\langle A\rangle}\rangle,t_{\langle A\rangle},[1_{\langle A\rangle},1_{\langle A\rangle}],b_{\langle A\rangle}\rangle

\displaystyle\mapsto\,\langle\epsilon_{\mbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\mathbb{X}}$}}(\langle A\rangle),\epsilon_{\mbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\mathbb{X}}$}}(\langle 1_{\langle A\rangle},1_{\langle A\rangle}\rangle);1_{A\otimes A},\epsilon_{\mbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\mathbb{X}}$}}(t_{\langle A\rangle});1_{\top},1_{A\oplus A};\epsilon_{\mbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\mathbb{X}}$}}([1_{\langle A\rangle},1_{\langle A\rangle}]),1_{\bot};\epsilon_{\mbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\mathbb{X}}$}}(b_{\langle A\rangle})\rangle

\displaystyle=\langle A,\Delta_{A},e_{A},\nabla_{A},u_{A}\rangle

since, by Theorem
105
, the unique maps induced by the universal properties of products and coproducts in
\B[\mbox{${\mathbb{X}}$}]
are given by the structure maps of the medial bimonoids themselves, i.e.

\langle 1_{\langle A\rangle},1_{\langle A\rangle}\rangle,=\Delta_{A}\qquad t_{\langle A\rangle}=e_{A}\qquad[1_{\langle A\rangle},1_{\langle A\rangle}]=\nabla_{A}\qquad b_{\langle A\rangle}=u_{A}

Therefore,
\boldsymbol{\eta}
and
\boldsymbol{\epsilon}
are the unit and counit of the 2-adjunction
\inc\vdash\B[-]\colon\CLDC\rightarrow\SMLDC
.
∎

Perhaps the most commonly used result of the traditional of the Fox theorem is that a symmetric monoidal category is cartesian if and only if it is isomorphic to its category of comonoids, as stated in Corollary
16
. We can of course state an analogous Corollary to our 2-adjunction for symmetric medial LDCs:

Corollary 112
.

A symmetric medial linearly distributive category is cartesian if and only if it is isomorphic to its category of bicommutative medial bimonoids and medial bimonoid morphisms.

The above corollary can be unwrapped to give an explicit description in terms of medial bimonoid maps, as in the case of traditional cartesian categories.

Corollary 113
.

A symmetric medial linearly distributive category is cartesian if and only if there are natural transformations

\Delta_{A}\,\colon A\rightarrow A\otimes A\quad\quad e_{A}\,\colon A\rightarrow\top\quad\quad\nabla_{A}\,\colon A\oplus A\rightarrow A\quad\quad u_{A}:\bot\rightarrow A

such that,
\forall A,B\in\mbox{${\mathbb{X}}$},\langle A,\Delta_{A},e_{A},\nabla_{A},u_{A}\rangle
determines a bicommutative medial bimonoid, and

\Delta_{A\otimes B}=(\Delta_{A}\otimes\Delta_{B});\tau^{\otimes}_{A,A,B,B}\qquad e_{A\otimes B}=(e_{A}\otimes e_{B});{u^{R}_{\otimes}}_{\top}^{-1}

\nabla_{A\otimes B}=\mu_{A,B,A,B};(\nabla_{A}\otimes\nabla_{B})\qquad u_{A\otimes B}=\nabla_{\top};(u_{A}\otimes u_{B})

\nabla_{A\oplus B}=\tau^{\oplus}_{A,B,A,B};(\nabla_{A}\oplus\nabla_{B})\qquad u_{A\oplus B}={u^{R}_{\oplus}}^{-1}_{\bot};(u_{A}\oplus u_{B})

\Delta_{A\oplus B}=(\Delta_{A}\oplus\Delta_{B});\mu_{A,A,B,B}\qquad e_{A\oplus B}=(e_{A}\oplus e_{B});\Delta_{\bot}

\Delta_{\top}={u^{R}_{\otimes}}_{\top}\qquad e_{\top}=1_{\top}\qquad\nabla_{\bot}={u^{R}_{\oplus}}_{\bot}\qquad u_{\bot}=1_{\bot}

e_{\bot}=u_{\top}=m

This is in essence an amalgamations of Proposition
65
and Corollary
66
, developed at the start to get a better understanding of the underlying structure of CLDCs.

Appendix

C
